“This is a critical study on African social movements reflecting on political opportunities, framing and mobilisation structures with respect to the Kenyan Ufungamano Initiative. The book also considers the limits of ‘disruptive power’ of movements once absorbed into mainstream political processes.”
– Shauna Mottiar, Director, Centre for Civil Society, University of KwaZulu Natal, South Africa
“Mati’s new book on political protests in recent Kenya is outstanding in several ways. Most theoretical analyses of political protests in Africa, of which there have been many in recent decades, seldom give sufficient accounts of African contexts due to their overreliance on models derived from Europe and elsewhere, not from Africa. Mati, however, roots his analysis in African and especially Kenyan history, carefully taking account of ‘struggles of multiple competing classes, ethnicities, religious groups, generations and gender’. Mati shows how an inter-faith-based Ufungamano Initiative, as a uniquely Kenyan ‘movement of movements’, played a central long-term role in fostering popular citizen participation in positive constitutional change, in spite of many complexities and internal changes in the movement. I would highly recommend it to readers interested in political protest periods and theories, especially in Africa; it is vital to understanding recent Kenyan history and politics.”
– David Horton Smith, Research and Emeritus Professor,
Department of Sociology, Boston College,
Chestnut Hill, MA, USA

Political Protest in Contemporary Kenya

This book analyses the emergence, strategies, and outcomes of the struggle to embed democratic governance and constitutional order in Kenya, showcasing both the power and the limits of citizen agency in the struggle to transform a postcolonial African state.
Utilising data from primary interviews, media, and existing literature, this book analyses the emergence, diffusion, operational strategies, and outcomes of Kenyan constitutional reform struggles with a view to highlighting both the power and limits of social movement in transforming a postcolonial African state. It engages intersections of social movement and theories of democratisation to probe the production, operations, and outcomes of the disruptive yet creative power of the movements at the centre of the struggle to transform the Kenyan constitution. The book also appraises the “meanings” of, and developments after, the promulgation of the 2010 constitution with a view to illuminating the prospects for a transformative democratic political order in Kenya. This book is a useful tool in understanding the struggles specific to Kenya, but also offers insights into other democratic struggles on the African continent and beyond.
This book will be of interest to students and scholars of social movements and political change in Africa in general and Kenya in particular.
Jacob Mwathi Mati is a senior lecturer in sociology at Sol Plaatje University, South Africa and an associate research fellow at the Society, Work & Politics (SWOP) Institute at The University of the Witwatersrand, South Africa.

Acknowledgements

The ideas presented in this book are the result of years of cumulative reflection on my lived experiences as a Kenyan. In 1990, the Bretton Woods Institutions forced the government of Kenya to cut down on social and welfare expenditure and called for the liberalisation of the economy. This move was also in concert with the suspension of aid by the Paris Club of donors, in order to force political liberalisation. The subsequent impacts were manifold, with the brunt borne by the health and education sectors.
Citizens did not take these events lying down; soon calls for change reverberated in many corners of the country. When I joined Moi University as an undergraduate student in 1993, the militancy of student activism against the ascendancy of neo–liberal economic orthodoxy and bad governance had reached its peak. Our struggles as students centred on the immediate effects of cuts to funding and the introduction of hefty fees, but also resonated with the struggles of many other social groups in the country, crystallising in the struggle for constitutional change by disaffected Kenyans. Politically, Kenya was a society at war with itself, as open defiance against the regime of Daniel arap Moi gained momentum. While jargon such as the ‘Structural Adjustments Programmes’, ‘liberalisation’, and ‘democratisation’ featured prominently in my lectures, I was also living in the era of the effects of these policies. I began questioning what was happening around me.
Years later, as a postgraduate student at the University of the Witwatersrand, I became increasingly interested in making sense of the vortex I was living in. The result was a dissertation on struggles for Kenyan constitutional reform, the real genesis of the ideas presented in this book. This thesis sought to understand the dynamics in the motivations, drivers, and effects of all these struggles. I owe a great debt to Professor Michelle Williams, my Ph.D dissertation supervisor, who nurtured my ideas, offering insightful guidance and mentorship throughout my research. I received further guidance from Professor Karl Von Holdt as a post-doctoral research fellow at the Society, Work and Politics (SWOP) Institute at the University of the Witwatersrand on refining these ideas through further research, in order to reflect on new developments after the achievement of a new constitution in 2010. I did this round of fieldwork during and immediately after the 2013 general election. A final round of interviews was completed just after the 2017 general elections.
On this long journey of research and writing, I benefitted from financial support from the University of the Witwatersrand. I am grateful for the various awards and grants that enabled me study as well as attend and present at conferences. The National Research Foundation (NRF) funded my two-year post-doctoral fellowship at the SWOP Institute, and my affiliation with SWOP as an Associate Research Fellow continues. The German Academic Exchange Service (DAAD) funded my student exchange through Global Labour University to Kassel University. Endowed at the beginning of my doctoral studies, the DAAD fellowship was especially useful as it enabled me concentrate on the studies of the theoretical models in social movement and contentious politics that framed my subsequent study. The Council for the Development of Social Science Research in Africa (CODESRIA) gave me a grant for thesis-writing, which contributed greatly to the resources for the initial fieldwork. In Nairobi, Professor Justus Inonda Mwanje, then-Executive Director at the Institute for Policy Analysis and Research (IPAR), granted me office space through their visiting researchers’ fellowship that greatly aided my fieldwork.
In the course of this research, more than 100 participants generously set aside other tasks to provide insights into a dramatic period in Kenya’s recent history. For want of space, I cannot name each one of you individually, but know that I am very grateful. Lawrence Apiyo, Mashengu wa Mwachofi, and Ezekiel Rema, however, deserve a special mention for assisting me in reaching more participants in Nairobi, Kisumu, Mombasa, and Taita Taveta. The Citizens Coalition for Constitutional Culture (4Cs) and the NCCK Resource Centre gave me unfettered access to a trove of archived treasures in their libraries.
Finally yet importantly, my loved ones have greatly supported me in this process. First, my daughter, Zawadi Mati, and my wife, Patricia Kyungu: I know you have sacrificed a lot. You have been very understanding even in the face of the loneliness you have had to endure over the last few years. Thank you for your love and patience! Patricia – thank you, especially, for being my unpaid reviewer of every chapter. My parents – Abraham and Celina Mati – you have always been a great asset on this journey. Thank you for your love. My siblings – Kariungi, Cianthimbu, Mubira, Kajogo, Muthoni, and Kamene: thank you all for your love and encouragement.

1    The seeds of contemporary Kenyan constitutional reform struggles

An introduction

Introduction

Interest in African protests is growing substantially among academics and policymakers in light of high-profile, “third-wave” uprisings in recent years. Specifically, in the current decade, there have been widespread protests in, among other countries, Kenya, Senegal, Burkina Faso, Congo, South Africa, Guinea Bissau, Malawi, Sudan, Ivory Coast, Zimbabwe, Burundi, Ethiopia, Nigeria, Uganda, Democratic Republic of Congo, South Sudan, Algeria, Djibouti, Egypt, Mauritania, Morocco, Sudan, Western Sahara, Burkina, Togo, Zimbabwe, and many more. Some of these protests are vestiges from earlier waves of prodemocracy and anti-austerity protests in the 1990s.
Existing interest is heavily dominated by academics domiciled in Northern academies, with few locals in the fray. Additionally, the dominant analytical lenses are drawn from the North. Observing such separation between the sites of theoretical production and collective action, Foweraker (1995) wonders whether theories modelled on Western Europe and North America can sufficiently analyse social movements in other social, economic, and political settings. This book is centred on African knowledge and ideas often sidelined in academies of the Global North. Nonetheless, it is also a conversation with related analysis by non-Africans.
In Kenya, the dominant struggle has been centred on embedding a democratic constitutional order, which has been a constant in the country’s recent political history. This struggle is an exemplar of a constant dialectic in the postcolonial African state reform project which has been characterised on the one hand by vicious intra-elite competition and fragmentation,1 ethnic-based political mobilisation, and mass inter-ethnic conflict. On the other hand, there have been occasional moments of elite and mass consensus that resulted in the emergence of powerful movements and moments for change.
Many scholars of contemporary African protests and their politics are quick to employ ethnicity as a default analytical lens. Like Mueller (2018), this study illustrates that class distinctions also drive different actors in these social movements. The study, however, does not ignore ethnicity. Instead, it contributes to the understanding of how social distinctions, including class, ethnicity, religious, gender, and generation, can be overcome through carefully crafted frames and strategies in building alliances for a shared struggle. This shared struggle, however, does not equate to a common struggle, as each of the social classes, especially, brings with it a package of expectations, some of which are diametrically opposed to those of other social groups. The net effect, as the Kenyan constitutional reform struggles show, has been a movement beset with multiple contradictions.
The question to be answered is: what prevailing conditions allowed citizens from disparate social groupings of both secular and religious civil society2 and opposition political parties and movements of differing socioeconomic backgrounds and competing political and ideological orientations and interests to cohere into a movement for constitutional change and state reform in Kenya? More specifically, what were the particular reasons (i.e. social, economic, and political) and conditions for the emergence of a movement for constitutional change? Engaging the intersections of social movement and democratisation theories, the analysis in this book sheds light on this important question. It engages the political opportunities model in analysing the emergence, growth, and decay of successive waves of constitutional reform struggles that came into being from the early 1990s.
A dominant approach in studying African protests has also been comparative analysis, either within a country or cross-nationally (see, for example, Mueller 2018; LeBas 2013; Branch and Mampilly 2015). The current book departs from this, with a specific analytical gaze concentrated on the Ufungamano Initiative,3 a movement that crystallised in the late 1990s from disparate struggles of various social groupings of both secular and religious civil society and opposition political parties that managed to force substantial concessions from the regime of President Daniel arap Moi (Kenya’s second president; 1978–2002) and the then ruling party – the Kenyan African National Union (KANU).
By following in detail the Ufungamano Initiative, this approach rejects superficial descriptions of movement “types” and instead systematically analyses and illustrates how a multi-identity movement emerges and transforms in terms of membership, leadership, strategies, successes, and failures. The Ufungamano Initiative emerged from citizens’ palpable rage against the refusal of the government of President Moi to allow popular participation of the Kenyan people in reviewing their constitution. Emerging in an environment of deep societal divisions (especially class and ethnic, but also religious, generational, and gender) with multiple sites of struggle, the Ufungamano Initiative is a remarkable story of how and why previously disjointed and disparate individuals and groups came together in a broad “movement of movements” to become a critical contender in Kenyan constitutional reform. In its formative years, it epitomised a social movement with extraordinary mass appeal and a multiplicity of actors that many social movements, especially predecessors like the National Convention Executive Council (NCEC), had failed to achieve. Its membership was drawn from 54 stakeholders of citizens’ organisations, including human rights nongovernmental organisations (NGOs), several faith groups, student and youth organisations, women’s organisations, grassroots social movements, trade unions as well as some opposition political parties.
The Ufungamano Initiative mounted a credible challenge to the Moi/KANU state forcing it to capitulate (Ogony 2004; Andreassen and Tostensen 2006; Mati 2012a; 2012b; 2013; 2015; 2017). Of relevance here are three questions. What mobilisation strategies and resources did this multi-identity movement employ in its struggle to ensure it mounted a successful challenge against the state? What do the strategies employed and their outcomes tell us about the nature of successes, challenges, and failures of the state reform movement in Kenya? Put differently, how did a movement for change with disparate actors force popular participation in Kenya’s constitutional reform process?
The Ufungamano Initiative utilised direct social movement action through one of its organs – the People’s Commission of Kenya (PCK) – that was in direct competition with the Moi/KANU state for control of the constitutional reform process. This direct competition and challenge posed a legitimacy crisis for the state-led process, forcing an autocratic and intolerant regime to capitulate. But the Ufungamano Initiative is also a story of the limits of social movements, which, while holding so much power and promise, are limited in their ability to effect fundamental changes in society. Even after substantial gains in challenging the state, the Ufungamano Initiative was vulnerable and in a way agreed to a “coerced” merger with the state-led process in 2001. The merger dissipated the Ufungamano Initiative’s energy. This raises a counter question: what do the strategies that were employed and their outcomes tell us about the nature of the challenges and failures of this movement? Put differently, what were its shortcomings?
But this book is not just about the Ufungamano Initiative. Rather, building on the convergence of Polanyi’s (2001 [1944]) concept of “double movement” and the “political process model,” the book locates the generation, processes, actors, and outcomes of Ufungamano Initiative within the broader historical socioeconomic and political conditions of the late twentieth century. The Ufungamano Initiative is therefore treated as one wave of social mobilisations that emerged encumbered by memories and images of previous struggles. Using a periodisation approach, the long history of these struggles and their sustenance of protest are explained through “cycles or waves of contention,” where past struggles feed into succeeding ones.
This introductory chapter therefore sets the context and background which preceded the emergence of the Ufungamano Initiative. It offers a conceptual theorisation of the emergence and manifestations of contemporary state reform contentions in Kenyan. The approach is socio-historical with a view to explaining the nested and phased nature of these struggles. The intention here is not to recreate the entire canvas within which these struggles were embedded. Rather, the aim – to borrow from Burawoy (2012) – is to place these struggles in the context of the field of their production, competitors, allies, and antagonists, as these invariably shaped the emergence and practices of social movements and other actors involved. In so doing, the analysis builds on Tilly (2004), Wallerstein (1974), and Silver and Slater’s (1999) advocacy for the centrality of history in explaining contemporary socio-political struggles. This illumination of history is an effort to avoid a common pitfall of treating social movements and their contentious politics as “expressions of current attitudes, interests, or social conditions rather than as elements of longer-run histories” (Tilly 2004: 8).

Locating Kenyan constitutional reform struggles

Contemporary Kenyan contentions for constitutional change – like most other political protests in the rest of Africa – were reactions to social, political, and economic malaise. Specifically, they were citizens’ responses to a crisis orchestrated by the behaviour of the postcolonial political and economic elites, especially their failure to address the social grievances predating Kenya’s independence (Ochieng 2008). These grievances emanated from the contradictory expectations of the different social groups who had underwritten the struggle for independence (Murungi 1995). For a majority of the masses behind the struggle for independence, the legitimacy of the postcolonial state lay in meeting political economy imperatives: “huge developmental backlog of colonialism, in providing more schools, hospitals, jobs and other services and opportunities” (Veney and Zeleza 2013: 2). More importantly, most expected land – a primary source of the grievances against the colonial state – to be fairly redistributed to Africans after independence. However, the expectations of the masses were either ignored or pushed aside by the political elites without any serious redress to the fundamental issues that had led to agitations for independence.
Instead of addressing these grievances, the postcolonial political elite went for targeted co-optations of a few “peasants who benefited from the land resettlement schemes” (Veney and Zeleza 2013: 2) in the ethno-class formation project. Secondly, even among elites, there was serious factionalism resulting from disagreements over how to share the economic and political spoils after independence. Existing analyses of relationships between the various factions of socio-political elites in postcolonial Kenya, as in the rest of Africa, reveal a series of “betrayals” and false dawns of state transformation (see, for example, Davidson 1992; Zeilig et al. 2008). These “betrayals” were products as well as manifestations of fragmentations within these groups, and especially “indicative of the disintegrating [interclass] alliance that had been formed between the restive petty bourgeoisie and disaffected masses in the struggle for independence” (Veney and Zeleza 2013: 2; see also Mati 2012b). Additionally, these fragmentations were, by extension, products of exclusionary politics and economics that engineered high levels of conflict in postcolonial African nations (Mueller 2018). These class cleavages have rendered a contrast of both opportunities and constraints to reform of the postcolonial state across Africa.
A theorisation of the nature and character of these state reform contentions follows in the section below. Thereafter, an analysis of the contradictions in the postcolonial state – the principal reason for the dialectic of state reform contentions – follows. This is done in a periodised order, starting with the first post-independent decade to 1990. Next, the chapter examines how the contributions of the global political economy of Structural Adjustment Programmes (SAPs) in the aftermath of the Cold War choreographed the consolidation of pro-democracy forces into constitutional reform struggles.

Theorising Kenyan postcolonial state reform contentions

The struggles to change the Kenyan constitution emerged from contested meanings of the purposes of the state. A common narrative in the analysis of these contentions holds that it was a contest between a conservative postcolonial elite intent on retaining an authoritarian, illiberal state and social forces that aimed to transform the postcolonial state into a democratic and caring entity. This narrative greatly romanticises the middle-class4 actors behind these struggles (see, for example, Kibwana 1996; Nasong’o 2007; Mutua 2008; Mutunga 1999; Ngunyi 1996; Oloka-Onyango, Kibwana, and Peter 1996; Press 2004). However, a closer look at these struggles reveals that they are, instead, powerful illustrations of the duality and durability of hegemonic domination. On the one hand, it illustrates how a politically directed collective consciousness can incite transformative power on the part of the dominated to challenge their domination. Indeed, following Press (2004), this book shows that individual citizens’ agency was critical in creating an alternative norm that shifted mass compliance or indifference to resistance and defiance. Specifically, citizen agency was a key ingredient in the creation of opportunities and processes for collective mobilisations to force democratic concessions from authoritarian and illiberal elites. On the other hand, due to the heterogeneity of the actors, their competing ideologies, interests, and purposes, these struggles demonstrate the limits to the power of protest movements in achieving their objectives.
Succeeding chapters in this book will show that, resulting from its heterogeneous character, Kenya’s socioeconomic and political struggles have been undergirded by three central features: the primacy of economically induced social and political strains; the centrality of ethnicity in social contentions; and a constant dialectic of elite consensus and fragmentation (Mati 2012b; 2013). All these contributed to – but were also products of the contradictions in – the postcolonial state. In what follows, these three features are briefly explained before turning to illustrate how they pan out empirically as antinomies of the postcolonial state.

Economically induced social and political strains

European imperialism shepherded by the colonial state superintended over massive economic exploitation and deprivation of indigenous Africans. This was central in the germination of distrust and grievances against colonial state institutions. These grievances ignited anticolonial collective action formations. In this regard, it is noteworthy that early collective action formations in Kenya were direct responses to socioeconomic and political imperial occupation, predicated on the triple objectives of challenging the compulsory annexation of African land, opposing the exploitative colonial labour and taxation system, and preserving Africa’s imperilled traditions (Maina 1998). Since then, protests have been an ever-present feature of the Kenyan political scene.
The emphasis on state brutality and exploitation as fuel for anticolonial struggles is synonymous with the social movement literature’s stress on socioeconomic and political strains as generators of social contentions (see, for example, McAdam et al. 1988; 1996; Foweraker 1995; Scott 1991; Habermas 1973; 1979; Buechler 2000; Blumer 1951; Mueller 2018). Specifically, mass society theory, prominent in 1950s, theorised incubators of collective action to be social disorder resulting from socioeconomic disruptions (Kornhauser 1959; Buechler 2000; Foweraker 1995). Such dislocations occasioned by domination and control by the colonial state led to social stress and frustrations across Africa. To adapt to these strains, collective action behaviour evolved among Africans to confront their situation. This collective action behaviour became institutionalised over time as movements against socioeconomic and political dysfunctions of the colonial state. These movements tapped into available social and political resources and opportunities, and were therefore “extensions of politics by other means” (Tilly 1984). In most of Africa, including Kenya, economically induced social strains, as we shall see, did not end at independence; they were very much at the heart of the triggers for subsequent state and constitutional reform struggles. Yet, this is an aspect to which existing narratives fail to pay sufficient attention.

Centrality of ethnicity

The earliest social struggles that emerged as responses to the imposition and exploitative character of the colonial state had a distinctive ethnic character. Even after the amalgamation of the various ethnic and class-based protest movements into a nationalist anticolonial struggle after 1945, there remained distinct ethnic – if not intra-ethnic (family, clan) – cleavages in the pursuit of valued social goods such as power to control, recognition/respect, economic goods (e.g. land, jobs) (Mbithi 1974). Various commentators offer a variety of reasons for the primacy of ethnicity as a basic defining feature of African struggles. Two of them, Mamdani (1996) and Maina (1998), are useful here.
For Mamdani (1996), the policy of institutional segregation during colonial rule in Africa resulted in a racial dualism of two separate developments: a powerful colonial state with mainly white (enjoying political and legal rights) and a few Africans as citizens; and separate native communities of unprivileged subjects comprising mainly rural-based African peasants. This duality affected the nature of the social contentions that emerged. Principally, it divided African anticolonial protest movements along rural and urban lines. While urban African city-dwellers started demanding the same rights as the Europeans, “decentralized despotism” flourished in the customary courts of collaborationist African chiefs in the countryside (Mamdani 1996: 23). These dichotomies between urban and rural struggles remain a feature of modern African societies, including Kenya.
For his part, Maina (1998: 138) argues that in colonial Kenya, developing alongside the nascent labour movement which had urban and inter-tribal members, there was a cementing of the “traditionally high trust institutions such as the family, clan and tribe” into sites of political activism. This was because the colonial state’s deliberate policy of excluding African associations prevented Africans from having any formal institutional channels for expressing themselves and their grievances (Nzomo 2003). This was especially so, because, until the late 1950s, African populations were only allowed to form associations up to district level. Districts had themselves been chiselled as “insular ethnic enclaves” for the purpose of divide and rule (Maina 1998: 143). Nonetheless, the same were used as opportunities (rather than impediments) for collective action mobilisations. The Mau Mau, for example, along with many other struggles in colonial Kenya, serve to amplify the fact that transformative resistance occurred mostly within ethnic groupings.
Maina (1998: 138 citing Berman and Lonsdale 1992; original emphasis) argues that ethnicity remains central in Kenyan politics because:
[First,] Tribe and ethnic identity give groups a political language, unites people over what to argue about; … provides the images on which they can base their ideologies [… and] ideologies mobilize political support around social divisions … [Second,] people’s histories and identities are encoded in their customs, as are their philosophies of power, justice and entitlements.
Accordingly, ethnic identity provides an effective counterweight to the state’s hegemonic project of official histories that “have reworked the past to buttress predatory government, ethnic awareness and historiography” (Maina 1998: 138 citing Berman and Lonsdale 1992: 210).
To this day, formal social institutions manifest aspects of intra-elite struggles with ethnic undertones. The middle class perpetuate ethnic identity and use the same to shape their demands on the state because, as Maina (1998: 138) argues:
The African middle class straddles the twilight zone between individualism and communitarianism. They are not just members of a class, they are also sons and daughters of the tribe. They are held up as icons of progress and power, its emissaries at negotiations, and their exploits, the stuff of which fireside tales are made.
Moreover, as demonstrated later, such intra-elite contestations have always invoked ethnicity while ignoring grassroots struggles, thereby ensuring that everyday struggles of the masses are forever spatially localised. This temporarily changed in the 1990s when a faction of elite defectors joined hands with the masses in challenging the state. Existing accounts of Kenyan constitutional reform struggles fail to account for this convergence, instead privileging middle-class contributions (see, for example, Mutunga 1999). It will be shown in this book that, contrary to the prevailing narrative, middle-class demands for state reforms gained traction only after they united with subaltern struggles. This convergence involved framing historical and contemporary grievances around land dispossessions, evictions in urban informal settlements, the deteriorating economic and political situations, and the human rights abuses by the state as products of a bad constitution.

Elite consensus and fragmentation and cycles of contention

While the language of reform has been framed to indicate the need for fundamental changes in the architecture of the state and its relationship with citizens, this has not been achieved due to an ever-present dualism of elite consensus and fragmentation. Elite fragmentation can provide opportunities for a movement’s emergence and advancement (Tarrow 1998 [1994]). However, in the Kenyan case, while elite fragmentation offered vanguards of these struggles, further fragmentation and, at times, momentary consensus between the most influential actors in social movements, religious institutions, and political party elites always derails the reform train. The result is a series of “cycles” or “waves” of contention,5 where movements are formed, take off, take on lives of their own, and sometimes metamorphose into phenomenal forces of change or just fizzle out, fuelled chiefly by this dichotomy of momentary consensus followed by fragmentation. As a result, as Mutua (2008: 117–18) has argued,
[the Kenyan] state has shown a surprising ability to be impenetrable by the citizenry, a fact that makes its reform a daunting task. Invariably, even new elites who yesterday sang from the hymnbook of reform … quickly fall into the pit of anti-reform after they ascend to power.6
As such, elite consensus and fragmentation offers an explanation not just for the arrest of the radical transformation agenda but also for the elongation and the cycles of contention in these struggles. The next section identifies and analyses the key phases in Kenyan contentions since independence with a view to locating contemporary constitutional reform struggles in the contradictions in the postcolonial state.

Postcolonial contradictions and cycles of contention

The postcolonial Kenyan state, like others in Africa, has a pluralising potential but has also been utilised by the power elite as a “vehicle for its hegemonic project, shrinking the popular sectors of civil society and sundering and frustrating democratic deepening processes” (Maina 1998: 135). It is this dialectic character that has ensured the durability of exclusive politics in postcolonial Africa.
The anticolonial struggles that delivered independence had the active involvement of various social formations, including African labour unions as well as civic, cultural, and political associations. While aimed at delivering the twin objectives of political independence and improvements in the material conditions for the majority of Africans, independence became a charade as the postcolonial political elite failed to develop a satisfactory programme for socioeconomic reforms that would transform the material conditions for the majority (Zeilig et al. 2008). In Kenya for example, all effort instead went into ensuring the continuity of exploitative global capitalist production relations. In essence, the country continued to produce primary agricultural goods for Britain and other developed economies. At the same time, the quick institutionalisation of single-party states across Africa resulted in an erosion of democracy, political authoritarianism, and even military coups. Frantz Fanon (1967) and Basil Davidson (1992) described such developments throughout Africa as “the African encounter with the ‘curse of independence’ characterised by growing authoritarianism and corrupt economic aggrandisement of the political class at the expense of the nation” (cited in Zeilig et al. 2008: 2; see also Kamau 2007 and Muluka 20117 for similar arguments about Kenya).
In Kenya, it is important, however, not to jump too quickly to conclusions that the postcolonial elite were all too eager to embrace the exploitative capitalist economic model that they inherited. A couple of factors were in play that ensured this outcome. First, the departing colonial elite ensured that their rights to land remained unchallenged. The independence constitution said as much. The members of the capital- and land-owning class (the migrant white settler community) were constitutionally protected from any state acts that would undermine their continued monopoly rights over land and capital ownership. Moreover, they went to great lengths to ensure that no real transformation occurred in the new “independent” state. In this regard, a prominent member of the white settler community and a member of the colonial cabinet, the South African-born Bruce Mackenzie, was retained as the minister in the influential Agriculture Ministry in the post-independence government. The result was the protection of settler farmers’ rights and later the African comprador class that inherited large-scale commercial firms from them.
Second, the independence constitution was a compromise between the departing colonial regime and the incoming African elite which left intact the “bureaucratic state machinery that had been developed to facilitate extraction of an economic surplus, rather than furthering the needs of local welfare” (Currie and Ray 1986: 47–8). This compromise was a result of protracted constitutional negotiations in Lancaster House, London, in 1960, 1961, and 1963. Observing similar developments in postcolonial Africa, critics like Ruth First (1970: 57–8) charged that decolonisation had been nothing more than a “bargaining process with cooperative African elites … The former colonial … government guarded its options and … the careerist heirs to independence preoccupied themselves with ‘Africanization’ of the administration.” First (1970) could as well have been talking about Kenya, as uhuru (independence) was an opportunity for the local elite to become the repair and maintenance crew of foreign dominance and the state a broker of foreign dominance and peasant dependency.8
Third, independence exposed the new “independent” state to the geopolitical dynamics of the time. In particular, the Cold War played a significant role in the demobilisation of previously powerful social forces. This demobilisation was a two-stage process. First, the postcolonial power elite largely ignored the masses and their political economy grievances. Instead of uhuru delivering material goods like land that groups such as the Mau Mau had fought for, most of the freedom fighters were shunned, neglected, and forcefully told by President Jomo Kenyatta, “Hakuna cha bure” (Nothing is free) in independent Kenya (Hansard 2002; Mbataru 2005). Second, there was a deliberate, successful attempt to ensure that exclusive elitist politics continued in the postcolonial state. This exclusivity was manifest at two levels. First, elites in the ruling party (the Kenya African National Union – KANU) persuaded the opposition party (the Kenya African Democratic Union – KADU) to fold. Second the socialist-leaning elite faction in KANU was routed and forced into the political wilderness; with that, a clear hegemony of a non-transformative uhuru marshalled around careerist politicians ensued.
The US Central Intelligence Agency (CIA) was complicit in precipitating and sustaining this system under the Cold War. Even before independence, the CIA had ensnared a section of the elite by providing “carefully selective support” in terms of cash transfers to the Kenya Federation of Labour through Thomas Joseph Mboya (its Secretary General) to ensure that socialist ideas never gained traction in Kenya (Ramparts 1969). According to Ramparts (1969: 7), “both a credible nationalist and an economic conservative, Mboya was ideal for the CIA’s purposes [of] selective liberation” where the labour movement as well as other organised social groups such as Mau Mau that had been key actors in the fight for independence were completely demobilised as a strategy for stifling socialism while entrenching the power of the conservative elite faction.
After independence, Mboya became the Minister for Economic Planning and Development and was instrumental in pushing for the continuation of capitalism. In a ploy to counter true socialism, Mboya and his ilk ingeniously renamed capitalism “African socialism” through Sessional Paper No. 10 of 1965 that he aptly named “African Socialism and its Application to Planning in Kenya.” This became the economic blueprint for “independent” Kenya. For Mboya and others in his camp, African socialism favoured continuities of the colonial contradictions such as landownership by a few white foreigners despite the independence struggles having been against the same. Sessional Paper No. 10 therefore restated the government’s commitment to respect private ownership of capital, industrialisation, and human resources development, and rejected the existence of a class struggle in Kenya. Moreover, it embedded into the postcolonial Kenyan political economy the ideological demobilisation project that the Kenyatta/Mboya faction of KANU had started. Article 128, for example, stated:
Strikes cost the national output, the workers’ wages, companies’ profits, and Government taxes. Wages in excess of those warranted by productivity increase unemployment, encourage the substitution of capital for labour, and lead to bankruptcies. In order to avoid these drags on development, legislation will be needed providing for compulsory arbitration of major issues not resolved through regular bargaining processes.
Clearly, the government had no interest in fostering a vibrant labour movement.
The critical question here is: why did labour, opposition political parties, and other progressive social forces demobilise in the immediate aftermath of systemic colonial oppression? A confluence of means and goals between these social formations and the state defined this demobilisation. Specifically, the nation-building project of the 1960s throughout Africa played a big role in these demobilisations as one-party states were pushed as vehicles to achieve national unity. This required subordination of all other forces to the party and the president.
But it was not a trajectory entirely devoid of contestation among the postcolonial elite. Indeed, given the ideological differences of the time, maintaining unity among the political elite after independence proved difficult. Over time, intra-elite cleavages turned ethnic as competition for rent-seeking and distribution opportunities intensified. Ethnic competition theory offers useful insights for understanding why intra-elite tension turns ethnic in ethnically diverse societies. According to this theory, ethnic competition occurs when “overlap in the economic or political activities of multiple ethnic groups becomes a key mechanism through which particular boundaries [of ethnic difference] are reinforced” (Cunningham 2012: 507). Specifically, as Cunningham (2012) explains, ethnic competition increases the likelihood of conflict when there are grievances based on perceptions of exclusion or threats by members of competing ethnic groups. This was the case in Kenya, where ideologically oriented elite factions started framing their competition on the basis of the ethnicity of the principals and their followers. The principals in this case were President Jomo Kenyatta, leading the Kikuyu and allied ethnic groups, and his deputy, Vice-President Oginga Odinga, leading a mainly Luo troop. For the pro-capitalist elite faction led by Kenyatta that ultimately prevailed in this murky ethno-ideological war, the state was an instrument of accumulation (Veney and Zeleza 2013). They enriched themselves through legal and illegal rent-seeking activities, massive corruption, and flawed land-reform processes, quickly degenerating into an exploiting class akin to the white colonial one that they had supplanted (Currie and Ray 1986). They also resorted to systematic suppression of dissent and totally neglected the radical transformative agenda that had underwritten the fight for independence.
These developments sowed the seeds of disillusionment with uhuru. This disillusionment was the mortar that cemented the immediate postcolonial contentions. Specifically, it was easy to lure a majority of the masses who saw no difference with the change of status of Kenya from colony to independent state into one or other of the competing elite camps, especially after the Odinga-led leftist group broke away from KANU to form the Kenya People’s Union (KPU) in 1966.
The creation of KPU as the supposed vanguard of a transformative uhuru, and the ensuing response from the state – particularly its 1969 detention without trial of Odinga and the entire crop of KPU MPs after the stoning of Kenyatta’s motorcade in Kisumu (Odinga’s home town) – marked the death of open contention based on ideological and opposition party politics in postcolonial Kenya (Ng’weno 2007). What followed was a systematic “political tribalisation” of the contention (Mati 2019). Both protagonists (Kenyatta’s KANU and Odinga’s KPU) were guilty.
First, as Currie and Ray (1986: 48) observe, “growing regional inequalities were translated along ethnic lines and used to exacerbate tensions between Central Province and Western Kenya … apparent in the split between … KANU and … KPU.” Second, the Kenyatta state portrayed KPU as a Luo party led by a disgruntled Odinga because KPU had a majority of Luo support and leadership. Meanwhile, KPU painted KANU as a “greedy” Kikuyu party (Ng’weno 2007). This was despite the fact that each of these parties had support in different parts of the country. Henceforth, with subaltern support, the intra-elite contention took a decidedly ethnic turn. Years later, in the 1980s, a majority of those opposing Moi’s rule were non-Kalenjin (Moi’s ethnic group), which served to amplify the deep ethnicisation of Kenyan political contentions.
Third, the assassination of Thomas Mboya (an ethnic Luo9 and one Kenyatta’s strongest ideological allies) on July 5, 1969 by a KANU youth winger – Nashon Njenga (an ethnic Kikuyu) – did not help matters. Mboya was a man with immense international connections and widespread support across the country who was widely seen as a future president (Okwatch 2008). His assassination was perceived as authored by the Kikuyu elite close to Kenyatta who distrusted his power and influence. This was read as yet another plot by Kenyatta and his Kikuyu elite allies to prevent any Luo from assuming the presidency (Ng’weno 2007; Orengo 2008). Veteran journalist and publisher Hilary Ng’weno (2007) notes:
Within hours [of Mboya’s assassination], there were riots and demonstrations in Nairobi and in towns and villages in Luoland. The experience of the KPU had given most Luo the feeling that the Kikuyu were out to deny them any position of political leadership. They had pushed Oginga Odinga out of the ruling party – KANU. Now they had killed Mboya.
With Mboya assassinated and the opposition KPU leadership detained, Kenya became a de facto one-party state. This continued until 1982, when a de jure one-party state was legislated.
Besides the tribalisation of opposition against the Kenyatta regime, the challengers were bereft of ideas for democratic alternatives. This was because such challenges came from “mostly the same coalition of petty bourgeois elements who identified the problems of independence but could not resolve their contradictions” (Davidson 1992 cited in Zeilig et al. 2008: 3). Moreover, they failed to incorporate or align themselves with the everyday struggles of the masses. Instead of delving into the material contradictions between the Kenyatta-led elite and the masses, the opposition elected to capitalise on ethnic difference in swelling their ranks in their battles with the state. With these demobilisations and subsequent tribalisation of contention, the postcolonial Kenyan state–society relations ultimately came to reflect what Bates (1999: 93) describes as “the creation of characteristic political patterns; clientilistic and patronage politics, corruption, and privatization of public institutions.” This scenario continued into the 1980s. The following section illustrates how it served as an incubator for the constitutional reform struggles that began in earnest in the 1990s.

The 1970s: state predation versus opposition from within

The 1970s continued to be characterised by intra-elite political contentions. The key issues in these contentions were the increasing centralisation of state power into the presidency, the curtailing of freedoms of speech, association, and assembly, and murmurs against what was seen as immoral and primitive accumulation by the Kenyatta-led faction. This faction came to be known as the Kiambu Mafia because its members were mainly from Kiambu, the president’s home district. This group became wealthy through corruption, avaricious, and oppressive in a sea of “continuing poverty and deprivation for the masses” (Lamb 1975: 84). However, there were other pro-capitalists who were not Kikuyu. Some of these stood firm against what they saw as the immoral, primitive accumulation of the Kiambu Mafia. Chief among them was Mboya, then the minister for economic affairs, and the high priest of the capitalist bloc, who suggested in parliament that a number of Kikuyu politicians, including members of Kenyatta’s extended family, were enriching themselves at the expense of other Kenyans (Orengo 2008). But such complains always had ethnic undertones and seemed mostly fuelled by those who had lost out in the officially sanctioned looting frenzy. Besides ethnicity, there were many other sources of the cleavages behind these contentions.
By the early 1970s, only a few dissenting voices were left to question the state’s failure to transform landownership as the key driver of the economy and development. Josiah Mwangi Kariuki, a former Mau Mau detainee and a fierce anti-establishment MP was arguably the most visible. He questioned the willing buyer–willing seller policy on land reform adopted by the Kenyatta regime. For his troubles, he was assassinated on March 2, 1975 (Mutua 2008; Mutunga 1999; Ng’weno 2007).
Kariuki’s murder did not end the challenges against Kenyatta’s centralisation of power and his resolve to push Kenya further into the capitalist bloc. The mantle passed on to what Charles Njonjo – the attorney general at the time – would later derogatorily term the “seven bearded sisters.” This group consisted of the fiery legislators Koigi wa Wamwere, James Orengo, George Anyona, Onyango Midika, Dr Chibule wa Tsuma, Mashengu wa Mwachofi, and Lawrence Sifuna. Along with Ms Chelagat Mutai, Martin Shikuku, and Jean Marie Seroney, they comprised the only semblance of opposition left in KANU. The group pursued its anti-state crusades in parliament (Mutunga 1999). For this, some paid with detentions without trial, while others escaped into exile. The only exceptions were Sifuna and Mwachofi, who continued to be thorns in the flesh of the government until they were turfed out of parliament via the infamous mlolongo (queue) voting system in 1988.10
It is also instructive to note that after vanquishing their “common enemy,” the now clearly hegemonic capitalist group turned on each other, mainly on the basis of ethnicity, in their competition for the largest share of the loot. Furthermore, due to fears of losing out in the event of Kenyatta’s death (he was ageing and clearly frail), the early to mid-1970s were characterised by growing frictions, with calls for constitutional change pushed by Gikuyu, Embu, and Meru politicians (the president’s co-ethnics) designed to block the then vice-president, Daniel arap Moi (an ethnic Kalenjin), from ascending to power (Ng’weno 2007; Currie and Ray 1984).
Ethnicity continued to play a huge role as a key obstacle to the realisation of any mass cross-regional/inter-ethnic movement (Currie and Ray 1984). During the Kenyatta era, the so-called dissidents – with the exception of the likes of Ngugi wa Thiong’o, Kariuki, Bildad Kagia, and Wamwere (whose differences with Kenyatta were ideological) – were mainly Luo or non-Kikuyu. Despite a spirited campaign to prevent Moi from assuming office, he took over in 1978 after Kenyatta’s sudden death.11 Upon assuming office, his main critics continued to be Kikuyu. In time, they were joined by their erstwhile nemeses, the Luo. This illustrates that, when it suits them, elite factions can close ranks to oppose a commonly defined “enemy.”

The militancy of the 1980s

The 1980s were pockmarked by Polanyi-type “double movement” developments defined by heightened tensions and militancy between various elite formations as well as growing militancy among sections of the masses. This intensification of antagonism was itself a product of the political economy of the time. Specifically, resources for supporting the political elite’s retention of power started dwindling due to falling commodity (especially coffee) prices and reductions in foreign aid (Parsitau 2008; Rono 2002; Currie and Ray 1984). Furthermore, these tensions reflected grievances centred on deteriorating political rights and the unchanged land tenure system (Currie and Ray 19846; 1986; Kanogo 1987; Throup 1987; Furedi 1989; Maloba 1998; wa Kinyatti 1986; 1992).12
On the land question, its control and ownership had by now become an instrument for entrenching political patrimony. While a majority of the masses remained landless, the political elite acquired land through corruption and perpetuated colonial era large-scale commercial farming in the highly productive agricultural areas of the country (Ng’weno 2007). This sowed grievances against the political and economic elite that intensified until, in 1982, squatters occupied several farms (Currie and Ray 1986). This alarmed the political and economic elite. The Daily Nation ran an editorial in June 1982 that stated: “land is so central to the political health of this nation that we believe that whatever else is occupying the centre stage in politics should be relegated to second place” (Currie and Ray 1986: 49).
The Moi state responded to these challenges by further entrenching corruption, oppression of political competitors, pro-change elements, and the general masses, while at the same time flirting with “mass line populism” (Currie and Ray 1984: 570) in order to stymie militant calls for state reforms. The ruling elite also moved to formally criminalise political opposition in an environment of growing discontent. On June 9, 1982, amid rumours of attempts to register a new political party to challenge KANU’s de facto one-party rule, the Moi/KANU regime rushed Constitutional Amendment Act No. 7 through parliament. This made Kenya a de jure one-party state and declared KANU the sole legal political party.
The effect of this constitutional amendment confirmed Franz Fanon’s warning that single parties would be nothing but “the name, the emblem, and motto to immobilise the people, [and] a means for self-advancement” (cited in Murungi 1995: 16). Essentially, the move led to further erosion of basic liberal democratic rights like freedom of speech, assembly, and association. The state also clamped down on those who expressed dissent (Mbingu 1991). In the words of a leading human rights lawyer, Moi/KANU rule became so brutal that the division between right and wrong was clear. There was no grey area in between.13
For Moi and his apologists, the introduction of the Constitutional Amendment Act No. 7 of 1982, which closed avenues for political contest, was also meant to end future political wrangling and aid the development of a coherent disciplinary machinery (Imanyara 1991; Okoth-Ogendo 1993). From then on, state power became overtly centralised around the president. Kenyans witnessed a qualitative change as “the office of the president took an ever more active role in KANU and used the organization to control dissent within the country” (Widner 1992: 4). Increasingly ill at ease with alternative forms of political association, Moi banned ethnic welfare societies and created an internal KANU disciplinary committee and youth wing with surveillance responsibilities. The net effect of these changes was the creation of a political order characterised by fear, rumour, and opaqueness.
These socioeconomic and political strains and heightened repression by the state exacerbated the contradictions in the system. Instead of the death or even recession of contention, anti-regime sentiment reached a tipping point. This was used to “mobilise popular support on need for reforms, and also in the development of common principles and understanding of what the constitution should be like so as to curtail the excesses of Moi’s absolute and brutal powers.”14 The first major threat to the Moi regime – a coup attempt on August 1, 1982 – came just two months after the infamous Constitutional Amendment. The coup, spearheaded by non-commissioned officers of the Kenyan Air Force with the tacit support of students15 and members of the intelligentsia, was a direct response to dissatisfaction with the Moi/KANU regime (Mutunga 1999; Currie and Ray 1984; 1986; Mbingu 1991; Kamau 2007; Atieno-Odhiambo 2004; Badejo 2006; Mwaura 1997; Ajulu 2000; Nasong’o 2007; Cottrell and Ghai 2007). Specifically, the coup attempt was rooted in grievances over Kenyans’ material conditions, characterised by state corruption, “worsening economic crisis, [deepening] regional-ethnic tensions,16 open struggles over land allocation, deteriorating relations between the Government and the University of Nairobi” (Currie and Ray 1986: 47),17 the “problems of dictatorship, underdevelopment, cultural dependency and state unaccountability” (Mbingu 1991: 14).
While the coup failed, there emerged an extraordinary cocktail of social forces against the state as elite fragmentation deepened. Furthermore, with constraints on political association, churches and underground subversive movements led mainly by supposedly radical leftist intellectuals became refuges for dissenting voices.18 Civil servants and university professors identifying with this struggle were sacked, detained, or exiled (Atieno-Odhiambo 2004). State harassment did not end the struggle, though. Instead, it moved further afield into spheres occupied by other professional associations who so far had remained aloof from political issues (Nzomo 2003). The legal fraternity as well as activist religious groups stand out in this regard (Mati 2015; 2013; 2012b).
Teaming up with the radical leftist intellectuals and religious bodies, the Law Society of Kenya (LSK) emerged as an important pro-democracy civil society actor in the mid-1980s and was instrumental in the diffusion of the change agenda by actively campaigning against further erosion of the political space, especially after the introduction in 1986 of the infamous mlolongo (queue) voting system by the now openly intolerant and dystopic Moi/KANU regime (Mati 2013). This group of lawyers decided to take an anti-state stance primarily because they had suffered some of the same fates as their clients – harassment, abduction, imprisonment, assassination, and/or exile.19
A major illustration of such treatment includes lawyer and activist Gitobu Imanyara, who had made his name representing soldiers prosecuted for their role in the aborted 1982 coup. At the time, he was also publishing a highly influential journal – Nairobi Law Monthly – which was renowned for its outspoken condemnation of the Moi/KANU regime’s excesses.20 For his advocacy, Imanyara was jailed in 1987. Three years later, his journal was banned, albeit only for a short while. Another leading figure in these struggles, Kivutha Kibwana, a constitutional law professor turned politician, explained that state harassment forced the legal fraternity to resist:
For quite some time, people were abducted, many people were killed … colleagues died, others were imprisoned … we suffered a lot. As lawyers, we got involved because there was no more law to practise. You could not practise any more in the courts. Your kids could not go to school: we had kids that dropped out of school. When the universities went on strike, they kicked you out of the house because it was rented. If you had loans, especially from public banks, they would take you in. In fact, you would be very vulnerable. So you decide to have another system where you would make a livelihood and be free from this culture of both political and economic oppression.21
The motivation for the legal fraternity to join these struggles conforms with Tarrow’s (1998 [1994]: 56) assertion that, when bereft of a middle class as a buffer between the state and society, people become “self-seekers practicing narrow individualism … the result [is] a jealous egalitarianism, sporadic and uncoordinated mobilization, that may ultimately lead to a revolution.” In this regard, as shown in Chapter 2, what started as an intra-elite competition eventually cascaded downwards through multiple channels such as patron–client patronage (Haugerud 1995) and the agency of the subaltern and middle classes.
As already mentioned, the introduction of the mlolongo voting system was arguably the final straw that forced lawyers and activist religious groups to start opposing the excesses of the Moi/KANU state. In this system, electorates would openly queue behind their preferred candidates but cast no ballots. Hence, it was open to voter manipulation and intimidation. Indeed, it had been designed specifically for those purposes. In the 1988 elections, voters were ordered not to queue behind any politicians whom Moi did not favour. With mlolongo, KANU abandoned all pretence that it was a mass democratic party. Citizens were coerced, intimidated, and/or indoctrinated into supporting unpopular candidates approved by Moi.22 When electorates defied the threats and intimidation to queue behind a politician not endorsed by Moi, the outcome was invariably a declaration by the provincial administration – the supervising authority during these elections – that the candidate had lost even though he/she had the longest queue.
The first victims of mlolongo were the remnants of the outspoken “seven bearded sisters.”23 There were also attempts to deny victory to several other key politicians, most notably Moi’s own deputy, Mwai Kibaki, and cabinet minister Kenneth Matiba, both ethnic Kikuyus. At the height of this repression, Moi used the powerful KANU disciplinary committee to humiliate other politicians through expulsions from the party. Given that there was no other party, anyone expelled from KANU risked political isolation, loss of economic opportunities to partake in the division of rents and patronage, and, for most, loss of livelihood. Moreover, expulsion from KANU meant suffering at the hands of ruthless state secret agents who were quick to arrest and detain anyone on Moi’s orders.
Moi’s acts exposed deep factionalism within the ruling elite which changed the game forever. In the late 1980s prominent politicians who had fallen out of favour with Moi openly joined the intelligentsia, the activist clergy, and LSK in calling for political changes in the country, effectively turning the tide against the regime. However, despite emergent consensus among those opposed to Moi’s excesses, there were disagreements that continued to signpost fundamental ideological, class, religious, generational, and ethnic divergences in the Kenyan reform struggles. Specifically, as Mutua (2008: 99–100) posits, two schools of thought emerged:
The first, whose adherents advocated for the legalization of opposition, argued for minimum legal and constitutional reforms to level the playing field. Its main objective was the capture of state power. The second school … called for a constitutional overhaul of the state and a basic reformulation of the purposes of political society.
This divergence is important in illustrating the potential but also the limitations that characterised constitutional reform struggles when they took off in earnest in the 1990s.

Convergence of pro-democracy and constitutional reform struggles

Writing in 1910, Vladimir Lenin stated: “despair is [only] typical of those who do not understand the causes of evil, see no way out, and are incapable of struggle.” Kenyan pro-change advocates emboldened by state brutality started coalescing as a pro-democracy movement in 1990. Della Porta and Diani (2006: 3) explain such transformations in times of deep structural contradictions:
Often times, individual opinions and concerns turn into various forms of political and social participation. Moral and philosophical worldviews and deeply felt convictions are then paralleled by specific attempts by individuals to stop threatening developments, redress instances of injustice, promote alternative options to the managing of social life and economic activity.
In the remainder of this chapter, I address the question: why did the crystallisation of the pro-democracy struggles transform into a constitutional change movement. I also attempt to explain how this crystallisation led to a shift in the political norms for a significant segment of the Kenyan public by addressing two further questions. What opportunity structures allowed the emergence of alternatives? What form of framing did the pro-democracy activists use to elicit mass support?
The pro-democracy movement of the early 1990s was a product of new geopolitical opportunities occasioned by the end of the Cold War. This saw an “ascendancy of a new ideological dispensation among Western donor countries that increasingly linked aid disbursement to good governance and economic and political liberalisation” (Nzomo 2003: 189; Maina 1998; Murunga and Nasong’o 2007; Press 2004). These developments should be read together with the effects of almost a decade of neoliberal economic austerity measures implemented in Kenya through the infamous Structural Adjustment Programmes (SAPs) and especially how this impacted on the fragile hegemony of the dominant coalition of the power elite.24 As Haugerud (1995: 49) observes, “when the national or global economy enters a decline, and patronage rewards diminish, individual and community pressures for patronage rewards can destabilize regimes and engender conflict.” As shown in Chapter 2, the structural constraints resulting from SAPs ruptured the dominant elite coalition by limiting opportunities for rent seeking. Such factors, as North et al. (2013) observe in other countries, can limit possibilities for cooperation among dominant elite coalitions in fragile “limited access orders (LAOs)” polity.25 The result for Kenya was increasing intra-elite competition, factionalism, and deepening conflict.
As already mentioned, the members of the postcolonial elite, among other factors, relied on a system of patronage to maintain support and keep themselves in power. In this scenario, Press (2004: 23–4) observes, the regime stays in control because “the dominant economic groups … maintain their rule through … the practice of incorporating the leaders – cultural, political, social, economic – of potentially hostile groups into the elite network, the result being the formation of an ever broader ruling class.” Inducements range from “flattery, to offers of employment in administration, to the granting of substantial power in decision-making” (Femia 1987: 47 cited in Press 2004: 23–4). I suggest that a weak economy in the late 1980s and 1990s ruptured the effectiveness of Kenya’s patronage system. In turn, this led to elite fragmentation which opened opportunities for challengers. Specifically, a disgruntled section of the elite and the middle class comprising of lawyers, business people, religious leaders, and some politicians sought collective action solutions to the developments that were threatening their socioeconomic and political standing in society. In challenging the Moi/KANU state, they sought class alliances with existing mass struggles that they had chosen to ignore after independence. I discuss this proposition in more detail in the next section.

Global politics, SAPs, and the consolidation of state reform struggles

As already indicated, independence did not pose a serious challenge to capitalism. Sessional Paper No. 10 of 1965 – that deceptive Kenyan political economy blueprint – presented capitalism with an illusory coat of social justice and equity as well as contradictions. Paragraph 16, for instance, stated:
No matter how pressing immediate problems may be, progress towards the ultimate objectives [i.e. political equality; social justice; human dignity, including freedom of conscience, freedom of want, disease, and exploitation; equal opportunities; and high and growing per capita income, equitably distributed] will be a major consideration. In particular, political equality, social justice, human dignity will not be sacrificed to achieve more material ends more quickly. Nor will these objectives be compromised today in the faint hope that by so doing they can be reinstated more fully in some unknown and far distant future.
(Republic of Kenya 1965: 6; insertion drawn from Paragraph 4 of the same document)
Without belabouring the contractions in Sessional Paper No. 10, suffice to say that, in practice, capitalism deviated from the stated intent. The corrupt and avaricious postcolonial political elite accumulated their wealth in a sea of “poverty and deprivation for the masses” (Lamb 1975: 84). Nonetheless, the state – at least on paper – maintained its commitment to the advancement of social goals by providing some basic health care, and many other social services. However, the 1990s saw major structural changes in the management of the economy. Donor conditionalities aggressively advanced through SAPs required the country’s economy to embed itself further into the highly competitive global market economy, while simultaneously inducing growth and development in a volatile global political economy. Furthermore, in typical post-Cold War fashion, international donors stressed “both economic and democratic reforms as the overall goals of their aid programmes” (Maina 1998: 154).
Writing about democratic transitions in Africa, Mkandawire (1999: 119) wondered: “how can fragile democratic regimes improve their prospects for consolidation at a moment when the distributive impact of concurrent programs of economic liberalization and adjustments may be highly contested?” This question is highly relevant to the Kenyan case, where SAPs dealt a devastating blow. This was characterised by the collapse of the state’s ability to deliver essential public services, increased inequality, and the erosion of basic social solidarity, spiralling unemployment, deepening debt, and the depletion of foreign currency reserves (Ikamari 2004; Stiglitz 2002; Were 2001). Moreover, falling commodity prices forced many peasant producer cooperatives – the backbone of rural Kenyan economy – to collapse (Ombongi and Kanyinga 2001). Importantly for elite unity, reduced budget support from the international finance institutions (which suspended support to the Moi/KANU regime due to its intransigence on democratic reforms) meant there were fewer resources for maintaining patronage.
This was the perfect recipe for double-movement-type protests as well as the fracturing of fragile elite unity. Popular support for the Moi/KANU regime nosedived, resulting in a crisis of legitimacy as popular mass protests, workers’ protests and strikes, student protests, and social movements emerged to challenge the regime (Mutunga 1999; Ngunyi 2001; Press 2004; Nasong’o 2007; Karanja 2008). Besides ethnicity,26 which continued to provide some of the undercurrents in these agitations, students and the intelligentsia, the working class, an active religious elite, opposition politicians, and emergent human rights NGOs played central roles in the struggles. Their mobilisation and ensuing collective action were therefore clearly countermovements against the upheavals they faced. It was a time that also witnessed the resurgence of labour militancy. However, labour unions generally remained more concerned with their members’ bread-and-butter issues. In doing this, a natural starting point was to question neoliberalism.
Common among these emergent mobilisations was the shared feeling that the repressive political regime and limited economic opportunities were not insurmountable. Citizens therefore mobilised as organised civil society and social movements to confront the adverse socioeconomic and political issues, including existing laws that they considered unjust. It was no accident, therefore, that the increased thrust of economic neoliberalism from the late 1980s also witnessed a dramatic growth in the number of citizens’ groups whose primary mission was service delivery/self-help (Mati 2014; Kanyinga et al. 2007). This “associational revolution” – as it came to be known (Salamon 1994; Salamon and Anheier 1998) – was, in effect, a reaction to the deleterious effects of neoliberalism.
The prevailing socioeconomic constraints also created political opportunities for the nascent civil society and opposition groupings to push for the expansion of democratic space (Nzomo 2003; see also Mkandawire and Soludo 1999 for similar conclusions on other African countries). For instance, the release of political prisoners and the repeal of the constitutional clause that made Kenya a one-party state were directly due to pressure from civil society led by the Mothers of Political Prisoners and the Release Political Prisoners (RPP) pressure groups, combined with the suspension of balance-of-payment support for the Moi/KANU regime (Nzomo 2003; Maina 1998). The regime finally caved in on December 19, 1991, when Moi and the KANU leadership resolved to repeal Section 2A of the constitution. As Chapter 2 shows, this created opportunities for new forms of protest action. By 1992, calls for a total overhaul of the Kenyan constitution started crystallising. These calls were led by the emergent human rights NGOs that spearheaded the formation of the Coalition for National Convention (CNC) – the first coalition of forces seeking a national dialogue on the way forward for Kenya – on June 17, 1992. The CNC is significant because it brought together many civic actors and organisations, religious institutions, opposition political parties, and individuals who were instrumental throughout the constitutional reform struggles. Chapter 2 analyses the civil society organisations’ role in this phase of the struggle.

Outline of the book

Kenya’s postcolonial political history has been a turbulent one. This turbulence, as this chapter has shown, has resulted from contestations on the meanings and purposes of the postcolonial state. It is a contestation that up to the early 1990s was disguised as an intra-elite conflict, in parallel with fragmented subaltern struggles. This was arguably because of the hegemony of the postcolonial elite. Such hegemony allows postcolonial elites to cunningly impose a “national consensus” that reflects the interests of dominant social groups, with only a few concessions to other groups in society (Mutunga 1999; Nzomo 2003; Mutua 2008; Maina 1998; Amutabi 2007; Nasong’o 2007; Mwangola 2007; Murunga 2007; Murunga and Nasong’o 2007).
This chapter has shown that Kenyan reform struggles have a long history. These struggles have also been through successive waves of contention. The chapter has located the issues and principal actors behind contentious politics in the postcolonial state reform project. These contentions were responses to a long history of social, economic, and political contradictions within the postcolonial state. Specifically, the postcolonial elite, instead of delivering material benefits for the majority, resorted to misrule and executive excess, which irredeemably mutilated the class and ethnic covenant that had underwritten the anticolonial struggle.27 In an effort to redress these contradictions, different social groups agitated for a new covenant that would define the purposes of the state and the relationship between citizens and their governors.
The discussion so far has offered broad brushstrokes of the waves of contentions that preceded the early 1990s crystallisation of struggles for review of the constitution. These mobilisations accord with the Polanyian perspective that, when faced with increasing threats to its survival, society “react[s] by refusing to act like lemmings marching over a cliff to their own destruction” (Block 2001: xxv; see also Burawoy 2003). Movements and collective action therefore mirror mobilisations against discontents. But not all discontents in society translate into collective action and social movements. Therefore, the critical question here is: how did the discontents of the early 1990s translate into a constitutional reform movement?
Chapter 2 offers a theoretical conceptualisation that illustrates how factors associated with economically induced social and political strains, ethnicity, and the dualism of elite consensus and fragmentation co-produced a state reform movement. Specifically, it analyses why and how secular, middle-class, civil society organisations emerged to lead the transformation of pro-democracy struggles into a movement for constitutional reform in the early 1990s. It is argued that leadership by the middle class and a faction of the elite – or what Mueller (2018) calls “generals of the revolution” – mobilised and channelled mass discontent into a movement for reform. Moreover, the chapter illustrates intra-class divisions and their effect on the Kenyan state transformation project. The chapter further illustrates how the political context in the country was shaped by co-evolutionary actions of the state and the protest movement. This, coupled with the fragmentation of actors opposed to the Moi/KANU regime, allowed civil society organisations to emerge as leaders of the initial phase of contention. The chapter attributes the decay of this wave of contention to the infamous Inter-Parties Parliamentary Group (IPPG) political elite consensus. The mantle of leadership then passed to religious leaders under the Ufungamano Initiative.
Chapter 3 probes the specific conditions that allowed religious institutions and the clergy to seize control of the struggle through the Ufungamano Initiative in the late 1990s. Two key questions are addressed. What led different social and political forces to unite under the Ufungamano Initiative? And what new agenda and strategies did the Ufungamano Initiative bring to the constitutional and other socioeconomic and political struggles in Kenya?
Chapter 4 analyses issue framing and mobilisation structures that accounted for the Ufungamano Initiative’s success, but also its limitations. The chapter’s central thesis is that the Ufungamano Initiative had multiple internal contradictions, especially between its rhetoric in framing contention as a push for a people-driven constitutional reform process and its practice. These contradictions were products of: fragmentations emanating from the heterogeneity of actors and interests; and the various movements’ mobilisation structures and strategies. The chapter demonstrates that, despite its vision of constitution making as enshrined in the right of citizens to direct democratic participation, the Ufungamano Initiative’s internal practices and decision-making powers and structures did not guarantee the maximisation people’s participation. Nonetheless, the movement made significant progress in challenging the state. The chapter reflects on how this success was achieved, but also on the implications of the insufficient dispersal of authority and the concentration of decision-making power in the hands of a few leaders.
Chapter 5 addresses the paradoxical “success” of the Kenyan protest movement. It is argued that, although the Ufungamano Initiative mobilised citizens and forced an autocratic and intolerant regime to capitulate and allow mass participation, it also had its limits. The movement altered the trajectory of the radical constitutional reform project by creating mirages of spaces for democratic citizen participation in the constitutional review process, and its leaders dropped their guards as they were co-opted into positions of power. This co-optation became pronounced after the change of regime in 2002, when an opposition alliance (NARC) that enjoyed overwhelming support among the pro-change movement defeated KANU. The net effect of this co-optation, the chapter argues, was the abortion of the popular will of the people of Kenya to secure a new constitution due to fractures that led to the defeat of the government-sponsored constitution in the 2005 referendum.
In keeping with the cycles of contention thesis, Chapter 6 explains why and how a new wave of contention incubated in the ashes of the 2005 referendum led to post-election violence in 2007–2008 and ultimately a new constitution in 2010. It is argued that the referendum exposed deep cleavages not only within the new regime but also in the pro-change movement and the wider Kenyan public. In addition to scuppering delivery of a new constitution in 2005, these cleavages led to a realignment of social forces. Furthermore, frustrations over the new regime’s failure to deliver a new constitution to redress longstanding socioeconomic and political grievances found expression in the ethnic demagogy that characterised campaigning in the 2007 election.
