In the end, deep ethnic divisions precipitated the post-election violence, ignited by a dispute over the presidential ballot tally. This violence resulted in what Fowler (2011: 2) describes as “civic energy that is ‘uncivil’ and potentially destabilising,” but useful in the achievement of civil ends.
Chapter 7 builds on the idea that violence was necessary to force a political truce between competing elite formations and deliver a new constitution in 2010. The chapter advances a political economy argument to account for the realisation of the new constitution in that year. Specifically, collective threat (especially the loss of property) in the violence of 2007–2008 triggered elite bargains and concessions. The 2010 constitution was therefore a result of concessions by hegemonic economic and political elites who, with a view to self-preservation, agreed to a new constitution that in many ways was not radically different from the one rejected in 2005. However, the new constitution did not cure the fundamental contradictions in the relationship between the hegemonic power elites and Kenya’s citizens.
Chapter 8 asks whether the 2010 constitution has radically transformed governance institutions and practices with a view to expanding opportunities for ordinary Kenyans and, more importantly, mediating and managing intra-elite conflict to avoid further eruptions of violence. The chapter analyses the conduct of politics and political elite behaviour especially in the two elections since the enactment of the constitution. It argues that institutional reforms have allowed for the dispersal of rent-seeking opportunities to a wider political elite. In addition, there have been attempts to reform the public bureaucracy, especially the judiciary and the Electoral Commission, in the hope that they may become neutral arbiters of competing elites. However, a new just and democratic political order remains elusive; ethnic identity continues to be the master frame in political mobilisations; and predatory elite behaviour is still rampant. As such, a new wave of contention is in the offing.
Chapter 9 revisits the key issues of the preceding chapters and reiterates that the Kenyan constitutional change struggles were products not of singular actors but of cumulative pressures and the interactions of different social forces. The chapter further reflects on key lessons and contributions of the Kenyan struggles to the scholarship of social protest movements to argue that it is the “disruptive power” of movements that makes them forces of change. This disruptive power comes laden with creativity, resources, campaigns, repertoires of contention that challenge existing norms of government as well as an ability to envision alternative futures. This, the chapter argues, was an ever-present feature of the struggles in Kenya that helped to define their success or failure.
Notes
1    By “fragmentation,” I mean divisions within dominant elite groups with varying degrees of effects in a polity. For example, they can cause realignment between social forces to challenge the ruling elite. In extreme cases, they can cause political instability and even civil wars.
2    The analysis in this book adopts a Gramscian dialectical conception of civil society. Gramsci (1971) perceives civil society as “intertwined in a hegemonic historic bloc” in which the massive structures of modern democracies, both as state organisations and as complexes of associations in civil society, constitute for the art of politics, as it were, the “trenches” and the permanent fortifications of the front in the war of position (Gramsci 1971: 243). Civil society is therefore the “sturdy structure … and powerful system of fortresses and earthworks” (Gramsci 1971: 238) behind the state that serves as a stabilising, conservative force and ensures popular consent to hegemonic forces. It includes a range of non-coercive institutions: non-state and non-market structures and activities such as trade unions, schools, professional, educational and cultural associations, political parties, and churches that organise consent backed up by the potential application of force (coercion) (Bond 2006; Katz 2006; Price 2003). At the same time, if society is to free itself from hegemonic oppressive and exploitative forces in society, Gramsci advocates for a counter-hegemonic “war of position” for ideological dominance (hegemony) of civil society.
3    Ufungamano comes from the Kiswahili word fungamana, which means to join together. The Ufungamano Initiative was so named after Ufungamano House, an ecumenical centre owned by the National Council of Churches of Kenya (NCCK) and the Kenya Episcopal Conference (KEC) that provides a place of worship, pastoral care, guidance, and counselling to students and the wider academic community.
4    The definition of the middle class is based on the sociological notion of power (both political and economic) and follows a similar conception offered by Mueller (2018) which specifically departs from income-based metrics (Ncube and Lufumpa 2014). The middle class in Kenya, as in other African countries, has traditionally been university educated and possesses social power to influence the masses. It also has substantial economic resources, especially when compared to the masses.
5    Cycles or waves of contention refer to a “strong increase and subsequent decrease in the level of contention” (Koopmans 2004: 22; Tarrow 1998 [1994]; Strang and Soule 1998). According to Koopmans (2004: 22), there are three distinct characteristics of waves of contention:
First … a strong expansion of contention across social groups and sectors superseding the narrow boundaries of policy fields […] second […] a transformation of contention, that is, changes in strategies, alliances, structures […] which inevitably arise in the process of dynamic interactions and ensure that no protest wave ends up where it begun […] third […] contraction of contention.
6    See also Currie and Ray (1986), Zeilig et al. (2008), Davidson (1992), Lamb (1975), and Fanon (1967) for similar conclusions for Kenya and other African states.
7    Barrack Muluka registered his pessimism on the eve of South Sudanese independence: “Africans know how to turn a blessing into a curse.” He gave examples of past liberation stories turned disasters as heroes of yesteryear reinvented themselves as enemies of the people:
There have been far too many false dawns in Africa … Everywhere, the parties of independence became draconian behemoths. They began trampling on the people’s rights and freedoms soon after independence. African liberators have confounding capacity to transformation into dictators … and megalomaniacs … integrated harvester[s] of wealth and property [embracing a] political economy of negative ethnicity and exclusion … corruption … vaporisation of citizens and detention without trial.(www.standardmedia.co.ke/article/2000038593/as-africa-welcomes-southern-sudan-there-s-cause-to-celebrate-with-caution. Accessed July 9, 2011)
He has been vindicated.
8    Njoya, author interview, September 29, 2009. Many thanks to all of the actors who agreed to be interviewed for the purposes of this research project. A complete list of these interviewees is provided in the Appendix.
9    Mboya’s “Luoness” is contested. He was ethnically a Suba, a Bantu ethnic group assimilated by the Luo, a Nilotic group. Critics charge that Odinga appropriated Mboya’s ethnic proximity to the Luo after he was assassinated to advance his war against Kenyatta and his Kikuyu tribesmen.
10    Author interviews: wa Mwachofi, September 27, 2009; Gitari, September 21, 2010.
11    Moi’s ambitions were saved by decisive intervention by Attorney General Charles Njonjo, who, “in a strongly worded statement, subsequently endorsed by Kenyatta himself … ruled that it was an offence even to ‘imagine’ the President’s death” (Currie and Ray 1984: 585).
12    These authors place the unresolved land problem at the heart of Kenya’s contemporary constitutional conflicts.
13    Ndubi, author interview, September 24, 2009.
14    Ibid.
15    Students were seen prematurely celebrating in the streets immediately after the Kenyan Air Force announced that Moi’s government had been toppled. In the aftermath, the government charged numerous students as well as some professors for their alleged roles in the uprising (Mbingu 1991; Atieno-Odhiambo 2004; FES 2003). Prior to the coup, the student community was the only group to publicly challenge parliament’s decision to impose one-party rule by law in Kenya. The students organised several rallies, culminating in the presentation of a memorandum to President Moi that called for a national referendum on the issue (FES 2003: 2)
16    Currie and Ray (1986: 47) highlight the regional-ethnic tension, stating that “the short-lived ‘People’s Redemption Council’ … received extensive popular support in Nairobi, Mombasa, and Western Kenya.”
17    See Kindiki (2007), Mutunga (1999), Ngunyi (2001), Kamau (2007), and Bates (1999) for details of the deteriorating economic, social, and political conditions. Bates (1999: 93), for instance, notes that “disequilibria in the markets, clientilistic politics, fiscal crisis and growing political disaffection … constituted the core characteristics of the African malaise” – a phenomenon that included conditions imposed by international capital and the donor community that fragmented the domestic bourgeoisie and introduced new pressures for change on many African states. These conditions were part of the Bretton Woods institutions’ Structural Adjustment Programmes (SAPs), which were traceable to the international recession of the late 1960s and falling commodity prices of the mid-1970s. Kenya started implementing SAPs during the “1980/81 fiscal years … and did not become an important part of economic management until after the publication of the Sessional Paper No. 1 of 1986. Since then SAPs have been integrated as policy tools for economic management” (Rono 2002: 82–3). Nonetheless, by 1982, things were already getting out of hand as hope and optimism evaporated with the economic crisis that followed the gradual slide into social, economic, and political doldrums due to the implementation of SAPs. I deal with SAPs’ impact on the nature of contention later in this chapter.
18    Author interviews: Zein, October 7, 2010; Kibwana, October 21, 2009; Omar, October 11, 2009; Kibara, October 15, 2009.
19    Kibwana, author interview, October 21, 2009. See also Mutiga 2010; FES 2003.
20    Author interviews: Imanyara, October 28, 2009; Kihoro, September 23, 2009; Kuria, September 23, 2009.
21    Kibwana, author interview, October 21, 2009.
22    Such indoctrination included state propaganda channelled through Nyayo patriotic songs and the Loyalty Pledge. The latter was tailored to ensure loyalty to President Moi with the words:
I pledge my loyalty to the President and Nation of Kenya / My readiness and duty to defend the flag of our Republic / My life, strength and service in the task of nation building / In the living spirit embodied in our National motto “Harambee” and perpetuated in the Nyayo philosophy of Peace, Love and Unity.
Patriotic songs have their origins in the anti-colonial struggles. According to Dennis Onyango (2007), “Kenyans, as was the case in many colonised African nations, came up with inspiring compositions that sought to remind the populace of its need to free itself from oppression.” Post-independence, a Permanent Presidential Music Commission (PPMC) was established to nurture talent and facilitate research into Kenyan indigenous music. However, at the height of the Moi era, the PPMC went far beyond this mandate. It became a huge propaganda machine that was responsible for composing numerous songs in praise of Moi and his regime. See Ngaira 2011 for further information on this aspect of the PPMC’s activities.
23    Author interviews: Gitari, September 21, 2009; wa Mwachofi, September 27, 2009; Odenda, October 7, 2009.
24    Mkangi, author interview, April 9, 2013.
25    LAOs are explained in detail in Chapter 2.
26    Members of Moi’s Kalenjin community were perceived to be unfairly benefiting from state largesse at the expense of other Kenyan communities. As already noted, most of Moi’s adversaries, save for a few such as the Rev. Muge and Tirop Kitur, were non-Kalenjins.
27    Njoya, author interview, September 29, 2009.

2    Crystallisation of the constitutional change movement

Introduction

Social movements go through three phases in their evolution and development: incubation, action, and institutionalisation (Buechler 2000; McCarthy and Zald 1977; Jenkins 1983; McAdam and Paulsen 1993; Snow et al. 1980; Tilly 1978; Nasong’o 2007). This chapter engages social movement literature in demonstrating incubation, action, and institutionalisation phases in the evolution of Kenyan state reform movements. It illustrates how the political context was shaped by co-evolutionary actions of the state and its antagonists. This is in line with the arguments by, among others, Tilly (2004) and Oliver and Myers (2003), that a social movement’s target(s) are also responsible for the variation and evolution of the movement. The chapter also highlights the dialectic “interplays of local, national and international forces … conflict and cohesion, resistance and domination, small- and large-scale processes” that have been constant features of the postcolonial political mobilisations in Kenya (Haugerud 1995: 9).
The chapter has three main sections. In the first, I map the co-evolutionary dynamics of contention between the Moi/KANU state and its antagonists. I then illustrate how fragmentation among pro-democracy activists generated inertia but also an opportunity for emergent human rights and civil society organisations to incubate the constitutional change movement. Next, I discuss the three phases of incubation, action, and institutionalisation in the successive waves of contention led by three key civil society movements from the early to late 1990s – the Coalition for National Convention, the Citizens’ Coalition for Constitutional Change (4Cs), and the National Convention Executive Council (NCEC). These movements were characterised by both unity and tensions that stagnated, reversed, or advanced their missions. The chapter concludes with an analysis of how the civil society-led cycle of contention died due to politicians closing ranks, with the blessing of religious leaders and donors, in the Inter-Parties Parliamentary Group (IPPG) agreements of 1997.

Competing political melodies of change: the state and its antagonists

Oliver and Myers’s (2003) model of “diffusion and co-evolution” of contention is useful for understanding how the democratisation struggles in Kenya evolved into a constitutional change movement. For Oliver and Myers (2003), the diffusion and co-evolution of social contentions/movements occurs because the actions of competing parties influence the likelihood of subsequent actions. Consequently, this impacts the political opportunities for movements’ emergence, advancement, or even decay.
In line with Oliver and Myers (2003) postulation, this chapter illustrates that the actions of the Moi/KANU state and its antagonists affected subsequent actions. Specifically, contemporary contentions for reform began with a confluence of religious, civil society, and opposition political leaders’ activism crystallising under the leadership of secular civil society movements. In this regard, 1990 began with a deepening of elite fragmentation as a section of the clergy and the legal fraternity openly questioned the morality of the existing political order. Emboldened by changes in the global scene, activist clergy – including the Rev. Timothy Njoya and Anglican bishops Henry Okullu, Alexander Muge, and David Gitari – started calling for political changes. As the year progressed, these clergymen were joined by a number of prominent politicians who had fallen out of favour with the Moi regime. The most notable of these were two wealthy former cabinet ministers – Kenneth Matiba and Charles Rubia. The Moi/KANU state responded to these developments by detaining, expelling from the party, or even assassinating its critics to the point that violence became normalised and dispersed from a central authority. However, this repression did not stem the diffusion of anti-Moi state sentiment across the country.

Leadership and the incubation of discontents into a pro-democracy movement

Social/protest movements emerge “in part from prior planning, coalition building, and muting of local differences” (Tilly 2004: 13). Leadership, among other political resources, is indispensable for movements’ emergence and sustenance. It is important for creating, recognising, and utilising political opportunities to transform discontents into collective action (Mati 2013; McCarthy and Zald 1977; Nasong’o 2007; Katumanga 1999; Gusfield 1966; Morris and Staggenborg 2004). Specifically, leaders catalyse collective discontent into social movements by providing “a body of organising principles and slogans around which people are organised for action” (Nasong’o 2007: 21; see also Katumanga 1999) while “mobiliz[ing] resources and found[ing] organizations in response to incentives, risks, and opportunities” (Morris and Staggenborg 2004: 173; see also Nasong’o 2007; Katumanga 1999; McCarthy and Zald 1973: 1977; Oberschall 1973). Following Hoffer (1958), Nasong’o (2007) calls these catalysts “men and women of words,” while Tilly (2004) and McAdam (1982) refer to them as “social movement entrepreneurs” – mainly members of the intelligentsia and ideologues on whom social movements “depend heavily … for their scale, durability and effectiveness” (Tilly 2004: 13). Another term employed in describing movement leadership is “generals of the revolution” (Mueller 2018). Social movement entrepreneurs utilise their charisma, oratorical capacity, and the power of written word to publicise existing social dysfunctions and discontent among the people, and hypothesise how these may be fixed (Katumanga 1999). At the same time, these men and women of words seek to “undermine the existing belief systems and institutional arrangement while simultaneously promoting hunger for faith among masses” (Hoffer 1958 cited in Nasong’o 2007: 21).
In the Kenyan constitutional reform struggles, such men and women of words included a group of religious and political leaders who believed that the socioeconomic and political conditions in the country could be different. Borrowing from traditions as diverse as liberation theology and the US civil rights movement,1 one section of the clergy took a deliberate step to challenge the Moi/KANU regime based on the strong theological conviction that the Church’s mission is not just spiritual but also includes political, social, and economic dimensions. David Gitari captured this well, arguing:
Our theology [emphasises] that you cannot keep away from the welfare of your people. Yes, we are for heaven, but we are in this world, and cannot abandon the affairs of the world to politicians … politics is so important that it should not be left to politicians alone.2
In this context, as Timothy Njoya indicated, the clergy have a divinely ordained role to intervene on issues of injustice. Indeed, at the time of initiating these struggles, the activist clergy had strong convictions that they were doing God’s work.3 Njoya stands out as a quintessential “man of words” for his courage as an anti-establishment cleric and for his decisive leadership in incubating these contentions. On January 1, 1990, he delivered a politically charged sermon at St Andrew’s Church, Nairobi, in which he wondered how much longer it would be before Kenya and the rest of Africa caught up with events occurring in Eastern Europe since the fall of the Berlin Wall. In this sermon, Njoya challenged the political establishment to give serious consideration to a return to multi-partyism in line with changing global circumstances. Three years earlier, on October 5, 1986, he had challenged Moi/KANU’s repressive order by calling on “Kenyans to reclaim their humanity, sovereignty and freedom by ending the one-party autocratic state and writing a new constitution” (Njoya 2013: 4). This 1986 sermon was considered high treason, and a majority of Kenyans remained ambivalent about the prospects of such a political project. As a result, only a few members of the intelligentsia had dared to comment. By contrast, the 1990 sermon came just a few weeks after Bishop Henry Okullu of the Anglican Church had drawn parallels with the fall of Nicolae Ceaușescu’s dictatorship in Romania to predict the end of single-party dictatorships in Africa (Okullu 1997; Karanja 2008: 83) and consequently drew widespread condemnation from a shaky Moi/KANU political establishment. This development is important in explaining the diffusion and widening of elite cleavages that ultimately led to an expansion of opportunities to cultivate and consolidate dissent against the Moi/KANU state. It is also useful in mapping the divergent motivations of the various individuals and groups who participated in the ensuing contentions.
The driver of these two radical sermons was the deteriorating socio-political conditions in Kenya. Specifically, the regime had become rogue, and the state security services were killing, maiming, and detaining people every week. The funerals for such victims offered numerous platforms for politically charged sermons. For a while, these radical clergy (Ngunyi 1995) were the de facto political opposition, speaking out against the ills of the state and politicians (Weekly Review 1990a). It did not go unnoticed that, despite their anti-dictatorship stance, the state had yet to persecute these radical clergymen. This changed after Njoya’s January 1, 1990 sermon. The clergy were no longer the proverbial sacred cows as his sermon triggered a series of actions and reactions by the state and a wide array of those opposed to its excesses and injustices. For example, on March 19, 1990, a Presbyterian Church of East Africa (PCEA) priest, the Rev. Lawford Imunde, was arrested, charged with sedition, and jailed for six years. Then, on August 14, 1990, an Anglican bishop, Alexander Muge, was killed in a mysterious “traffic accident” in western Kenya. At the same time, the state’s repressive machinery, including the secret service police and KANU youth wingers, targeted middle-class professionals, especially lawyers and the intelligentsia, who dared to join the radical clergymen in questioning the state. For lawyers especially, their traditional economic comfort was ruptured by the economic meltdown precipitated by SAPs and corruption. They were therefore easily seduced by the words of the radical clergy and, in effect, became the cadres or fanatics who led the revolt against the Moi/KANU regime.
The tide truly started to turn against Moi’s authoritarianism after the fragmentation of the dominant elite faction. Specifically, two former cabinet ministers – Kenneth Matiba and Charles Rubia – who had fallen out of favour with Moi’s dominant faction started the clandestine organisation of an “elite-directed opposition to the Moi government” (Widner 1992: 172). Scholars such as Haugerud (1995) and Bratton and Van de Walle (1992) argue that this fragmentation was a result of dwindling of discretionary state resources that fuelled patronage networks, which resulted in “elite defections” that left the regime more vulnerable to unrest and less able to buy support and co-opt opposition (Haugerud 1995: 49). These elite defections, as discussed in Chapter 1, were products of multiple processes including Structural Adjustment Programmes (SAPs), corruption, and human rights abuses that fed the burgeoning struggles for political and economic pluralism, initially, at least, as part of a self-preservation strategy.
North et al.’s (2013) “Limited Access Order (LAO)” framework is useful for understanding how coordination among elites occurs and checks violence. The LAO framework stresses the “importance of organizations both as a way of coordinating individuals and as a way of generating rents and shaping incentives consistent with individual behaviour” (North et al. 2013: 3). Such organisation is used to discourage potential violence, even though violence remains latent. In the mid-1980s, social arrangements that had ensured limited competition within the dominant elite coalition started to be stifled due to the excesses of single-party rule. This drove a wedge into the dominant coalition, especially over distribution concerns. Those who lost out in these intra-elite conflicts adopted alternative strategies to remain relevant. This suggests self-interest motivations consistent with Tilly’s (1995) postulations.
The political economy of access to rents and patronage networks, therefore, is a key factor in explaining why a faction of the elite turned against Moi/KANU. Matiba and Rubia’s role in incubating the “elite-directed opposition” should be viewed in this context. Specifically, Rubia became a victim of the infamous mlolongo voting system, controversially losing his Starehe constituency seat in Nairobi in the 1988 election.4 Then, the following June, the KANU disciplinary committee suspended him from the party. According to Widner (1992: 172), Rubia was targeted because he had a history of displaying independent thought and had been part of an “elite opposition” that “played the role of ‘moderate’ and quiet defender of civil liberties during the 1970s.” In 1986, Rubia was one of three MPs (the others were Masinde Muliro and Kimani wa Nyoike) who opposed the introduction of queue voting and the abolition of security of tenure for the attorney general and the controller and auditor general (Widner 1992: 172). For this, he became a target of systematic police harassment prior to losing his seat in 1988. Thereafter, Moi denied Rubia’s companies opportunities to do business with the state and ensured his children were not employed in any state jobs (Widner 1992).
Matiba, on the other hand, had built a reputation as an effective civil servant and, later, a wealthy businessman and politician. A long-serving civil servant during the Kenyatta era, he joined parliament in 1979 and served in Moi’s government in a number of cabinet positions until December 1988, when he resigned in protest against the rigging of grassroots KANU elections. Thereafter, he became a vocal critic of the Moi government. For this, the state constantly harassed him.
Such state harassment as well as political and economic isolation forced these excluded members of the elite to step up their opposition to the Moi-led faction. On May 3, 1990 at the Stanley Hotel, Nairobi, Matiba and Rubia held a press conference during which they demanded “the repeal of Section 2(a) of the Constitution of Kenya, the dissolution of Parliament and the holding of free elections devoid of rigging and an end to the mismanagement of public affairs” (Agoya 2014). This press conference reverberated across the country and internationally. Matiba and Rubia’s demands coincided with a similar call by the US ambassador, Smith Hempstone, the same day. Furthermore, Hempstone indicated that the United States would “tie aid to political performance” in the future (Widner 1992: 174).
Predictably, the Moi/KANU government was not prepared to allow the development of alternative political organisations without a fight. Immediately afterwards, KANU condemned the press conference, labelled the idea of multi-party democracy “foreign,” and threatened to take action against Matiba and Rubia (Agoya 2014; Widner 1992). Nevertheless, the undeterred duo wrote a discussion paper on the merits of a multi-party system. In this, they attributed the country’s increasing economic problems to “KANU’s inflexibility and indifference to demands for public accountability, corruption and ‘tribal patronage’ that supplanted merit as a criterion for advancement” (Widner 1992: 174–5). Furthermore, on June 6, they provoked the Moi/KANU state by applying for a licence to hold a public rally on July 7 at the Kamukunji Grounds in Nairobi, with a view to discussing the political future of the country.
The choice of Kamukunji was manifestly symbolic. During the struggle for independence, public rallies had been held there. Thereafter, it had been neglected and virtually forgotten, but now it had been chosen as the launch-pad for another historic wave of political contention. Subsequently, Kamukunji entered the Kenyan political lexicon as a term for anti-government political assemblies, legal or otherwise.
As expected, the state banned the rally and warned citizens against attending any “illegal meeting.” On July 4, police arrested and detained Rubia, Matiba, Raila Odinga, and lawyers John Khaminwa, Gitobu Imanyara, and Mohamed Ibrahim under the “Public Security Regulations which provided for indefinite administrative detention without charge or trial of anyone held to be endangering the security of the state” (Amnesty International 1991: 1). Within a week, other prominent advocates of a multi-party system, including George Anyona (a former MP), Njeru Kathangu, Isaiah Ngotho Kariuki, and Professor Edward Oyugi, were arrested and charged with treason. A number of lawyers, including Kamau Kuria and Kiraitu Murungi, were forced into exile in the United States (Amnesty International 1991).
These detentions and expulsions added fuel to an already flammable situation as the exiles continued their activism, including lobbying Western donors to suspend all aid to Kenya. Moreover, despite the detentions and the ban, on July 7 citizens of various social classes attempted to congregate at Kamukunji. They were violently dispersed by the state, which sparked riots in Nairobi, Limuru, Nakuru, Murang’a, Nyeri, Nyandarua, Thika, Narok, and Kiambu. At least 22 people were killed in the violence, while many more were “arrested for using the two-finger V-salute, meaning that they supported ‘two parties,’ or for shouting ‘Release Matiba,’ amongst other anti-government slogans” (Amnesty International 1991: 4).
The violence of July 7, 1990 – or Saba Saba as the day later came to be known – had a significant impact on the nascent struggles as it exposed the Moi/KANU state’s vulnerability. This was especially so as “segments of Kenya’s large informal sector, long considered part of Moi’s ‘populist’ base, began to participate in opposition activities” (Widner 1992: 163). But it was not just people in the informal sector who started to participate. Other disadvantaged social groups, including urban slum dwellers and peasants in rural Kenya, the unemployed, and university students, among others, also joined the protests. This change of heart for a majority of the underclasses must be viewed, as Otiso (2002) has suggested, within a context of gross inequality as well as the deepening elite fragmentation that threatened the political structure at the time. It must also be seen as a reawakening of the agency of subalterns who, at independence, had “abandoned politics to their enemy: the dominant elite.”5
Under the circumstances, the subaltern social movements, such as Muungano wa Wanavijiji in Nairobi’s informal settlements and SEMA in Taita Taveta, emerged in response to grievances over land, jobs, and the allocation of resources. This crisis was framed by the Moi/KANU opposition as engineered by the Moi-allied elite who appropriated public resources for private ends, diverted development resources from other areas of the country to the Rift Valley – Moi’s home province – and stole money from producers’ cooperatives (Haugerud 1995). Furthermore, the ongoing widespread evictions of the poor, especially in urban slums (Widner 1992; Haugerud 1995; Klopp 2008), as well as systematic corruption in the allocation of public resources, such as land and jobs, greatly unnerved the urban poor. The words of one of the leaders of Muungano wa Wanavijiji vividly captures the prevailing attitude:
The public land where our small business stalls stand [Toy Market in Kibera] was said to be owned by a private individual and there were evictions happening every time. Even in 1995, when the president gave the order for some public land to be given to hawkers for the construction of the Kibera Hawkers Market, the allocations were done by the provincial administration in a very corrupt manner. Only six hawkers from Toy Market were allocated stalls from a total 196 stalls. The rest were allocated to the well-connected people, and harassment led by the provincial administration and the Nairobi city council continued.6
Under these circumstances, the urban masses mobilised for collective protests that soon developed into the establishment of Muungano wa Wanavijiji as a grassroots slum dwellers’ movement. This organisation later united with other groups to form a “movement of movements” that demanded a new constitution. The evictions were not just directed at individuals in the slums but also encompassed community development initiatives, such as schools, housing, and other physical infrastructure. Therefore, just like the middle class and the elite factions, the subaltern pro-change movements were driven by a combination of self-interest (i.e. rational choice) and principles. The critical question here is: how did this reawakening of subaltern agency happen? This question is addressed in the next section.

Protest fanatics: subalterns and the middle class in action

Leadership is instrumental in awakening and directing collective agency into movements and actions. At the action phase, as Nasong’o (2007: 21) notes, the “skills and temperament of fanatics are imperative for hatching and animating movements” (see also Katumanga 1999). For Nasong’o (2007: 21), fanatics – or “cadres,” to use Katumanga’s (1999) term – “take the ideology and words of the ideologues and translate them into comprehensible terms for the masses in distress.” For Katumanga, the ability to build cadres helps movements to undermine their opponents. These cadres are able to reach the rest of society easily because of their “ability to use and talk the language of the people” (Katumanga 2000: 6 cited in Nasong’o 2007: 21). Katumanga (2000), following Tilly (1977) and McAdam (1982), further contends that a movement’s advancement depends on its cadres’ ability to transmit their objectives.
Mobilisation processes and structures create support among the general population by forming recruitment networks to enlist new members. Recruitment takes place through cadres arousing, nurturing, and motivating targeted individuals and groups through issue framing. Once support is won, a movement must build collective identity and continue to pursue collective interests. In the case under discussion, these cadres/fanatics included a large pool of trained community organisers as well as grassroots community activists. These people played a critical role in mobilising support, especially at the grassroots in both urban slums and marginalised rural areas like Taita Taveta, where the struggle resonated greatly with the masses. The cadres were able to translate people’s distress into easily comprehensible collective action formations.
Religious organisations and their leaders, as well as human rights NGOs, played a crucial role in training cadres and organising and mobilising people to act against real and perceived injustices. They also contributed to the emergence of groups like Muungano wa Wanavijiji and, more critically, linked their individual struggles to a national struggle for change.7 In this regard, the Catholic Church in partnership with the African Network for the Prevention and Protection against Child Abuse and Neglect (ANPPCAN), for instance, introduced formal courses in community organisation whose principal beneficiaries were urban slum dwellers.8 The Exodus–Kutoka Network of Catholic parishes in the slums also demonstrated the Church’s central role in organising and mobilising the urban poor against forced evictions and other injustices, including constitutional issues. It was through such training and community organisation that cadres/fanatics were trained to lead the action phase of the Kenyan struggles.
The chair of Community Organisers’ Professional Association of Kenya (COPA), Lawrence Apiyo, was himself a beneficiary of this training. He amply demonstrated the zeal of community organisers when recalling:
I have been involved in political grassroots activism since the clamour for multi-party democracy. We used to mobilise people to hold night meetings during the push for change of Section 2A, because gatherings were not allowed. Because of this, Kibera slum hosted the first opposition party cell even before the repeal of Section 2A.9
This suggests that conviction and zeal were not limited to activist clergymen and a faction of the elite. On the contrary, cadres drawn from activists and community organisers in various parts of Kenya embraced and propagated the desire for change and ensured that it achieved a life of its own and remained alive. It was these cadres and fanatics, together with the masses of the urban poor, who attempted to congregate at Kamukunji on July 7, 1990. Their actions left the state shaken.
One of the most important political developments after the first Saba Saba protests was the convergence of middle-class and subaltern struggles under elite direction. This marked the beginning of the end of the one-party state as ever more people took inspiration from what had happened at Kamukunji to pile pressure on the government. Donors, especially the Paris Club, followed suit by withholding aid to Moi later in the year. These developments and their effects are analysed by Wachira Maina (1998), who argues that the Moi/KANU regime’s intransigence in the face of calls for democracy, and the ruthlessness with which it crushed pro-democracy protesters during the Saba Saba riots, gave donors the perfect excuse to make good on their threats. Over the next few months, relations between Moi and some Western countries became very strained. On October 22, 1990, for example, Kenya broke off diplomatic relations with Norway because the latter had offered political asylum to several high-profile dissident Kenyans.
Under the prevailing constricted economic circumstances discussed in the preceding chapter, it was only a matter of time before mounting pressure from activists and the nascent protest movements forced Moi to concede (Maina 1998). However, he had no intention of sitting by while his single-party state crumbled, as was already happening in several other African countries, including Kenneth Kaunda’s Zambia (Weekly Review 1990b). Besides criticising and detaining opponents, in an effort to steal the wind from the pro-democracy forces’ sails, he set in motion a process of “managed change” (Weekly Review 1990b).

Staging democratic transition

Immediately after Rubia and Matiba’s press conference on May 3, Moi and his cabinet held public rallies around the country during which they castigated Matiba and company for calling for multi-partyism and dismissed them as puppets of foreign masters. But Moi also seemed to be reading the signs of the time and knew he had to counter what he called “crowds of madmen and drug addicts” as well as complaints from donors or he would soon be out of the game. In the event, after Matiba and Rubia had applied for a licence to hold a public rally, Moi immediately convened the KANU Annual Delegates Conference on June 21, 1990 in Nairobi. A significant outcome of this conference was the appointment of review committee to look into the party’s nomination rules, election rules, and code of discipline with a view to making them more democratic and fit for purpose (KANU 1990: 3). These three issues had been at the heart of the widening of elite cleavages that had pushed the likes of Matiba and Rubia into opposition.
The KANU review committee held public hearings in all of Kenya’s provinces between July 25 and August 24 and presented its findings to a KANU Special Delegates Conference in December 1990. Among its bold recommendations – with far-reaching consequences for the emergent struggles – were proposals to abolish queue voting, the 70 per cent rule, and party expulsion as a disciplinary measure (KANU 1990: 33). These recommendations were too radical for party hawks, but Moi surprised many when he backed them. In his closing address to the conference on December 3, he stressed that KANU had to reform or risked Kenya sinking into an “abyss of chaos and disintegration” (Weekly Review 1990b: 19). Moreover, he not only supported the review committee’s recommendation that “any Kenyan who is qualified and wishes to do so, be cleared by the party to stand for preliminary election” but went further: “unlike in the past, where a person who felt aggrieved about the party’s decision on clearance could appeal to the president for redress, I propose that the KANU constitution be amended to enable such a person to seek redress in a court of law” (Weekly Review 1990b: 20). In essence, Moi was trying to disburse some of the power that had hitherto been concentrated within himself. Of course, the courts were still highly susceptible to political influence, especially from Moi, but he had at least displayed a willingness to countenance change.
Even as Moi made these pronouncements, however, his sole intention was to diffuse the mounting pressure for reform. Although he declared that expulsion from KANU would no longer be used as a disciplinary measure, the expulsions continued. For instance, in August 1991, the Mombasa branch of KANU expelled an outspoken civic leader, Councillor Ahmed Salim Bamahriz, after he declared that he had joined a yet to be registered Forum for the Restoration of Democracy (FORD). The ongoing expulsions offered opportunities for pro-democracy activists to engage in “accountability politics” (Keck and Sikkink 1998).10 In addition, even as Moi set the party on a reformist path, he refused to venture into the more critical question of a return to multi-partyism in the hope that the balm of reforms to electoral laws within KANU would stem the pro-reform tide. However, reform advocates read these changes as clear signs that the impossible might now be within reach. As such, they provided political opportunities to advance their activism. The following year, the clamour to repeal Section 2A of the constitution – the clause that made Kenya a de jure one-party state – increased.
When Moi was in the process of convincing the delegates to the KANU conference to accept minimal changes, Kenya’s former vice-president, Oginga Odinga, announced that he had formed a yet to be registered National Democratic Party (NDP) as an opposition political organisation. Little more was heard of this group for a couple of months, but then, in February 1991, Odinga submitted a request for registration to the registrar general. On March 13, the request was rejected. By then, the dominant faction in the Law Society of Kenya, now under the chairmanship of Paul Muite, had decided to challenge the state head-on and demand an alternative political order. This faction worked closely with the National Council of Churches of Kenya (NCCK), especially through the Public Law Institute, a legal aid and civil rights organisation (Widner 1992). Together with those expelled from the ruling elite, these groups started pushing hard for the repeal of Section 2A.
Co-evolving with calls for political reform was the normalisation and diffusion of violence in Kenyan politics. For example, the Moi/KANU government either initiated or abetted systematic ethnic violence against pro-opposition groups in many regions of the country (Kagwanja 2003; 2006). The infamous ruling party militia (KANU youth wingers, later replaced by Youth for KANU 92) were instrumental in the violence and intimidation that aimed to maintain KANU’s hold on power. Under the state’s watch, people were raped, killed, and their property destroyed on the basis of suspicions that their whole ethnic group supported the opposition. Massive dislocation ensued, especially in the Rift Valley, Coast, Eastern, and Western provinces. This violence, even after the repeal of section 2A, confined Kenya’s embryonic democracy to “cold storage” (Kagwanja 2003) and deepened the crisis of ethnicity. Since then, it has been repeated every five years, during and after general elections, with the post-election violence of 2008 being the most potent.
As a direct result of the state-sponsored violence, sections of the citizenry established ethnic-based vigilante groups, such as Mungiki among Kikuyu youth (Kagwanja 2003; 2005), Baghdad Boys (Luo), Chinkororo (Kisii), Sabaoti Land Defence Force (Sabaoti), Kamjesh, Jeshi la Mzee, Jeshi la Embakasi, and so on, mainly with the blessing of their respective elites. The state declared all of these groups illegal, but that only served to harden them. Most became clandestine criminal extortion gangs who sold their services to anyone who wished to terrorise political or economic competitors. In effect, Kenya endured the dispersal and privatisation of violence (Wamucii and Idwasi 2011), which allowed the state “not only [to] maintain control over the reins of power, but also sustain its nature” (Katumanga 1999: 8). In addition, there was a contestation over the legitimacy of the state and its institutions as the violent underground groups “became a preferred option to [the] ineffective” state in certain parts of the country (Wamucii and Idwasi 2011: 196).
However, not all of Kenya’s struggles turned violent. Pressure groups like Release Political Prisoners (RPP) and many other pro-democracy and human rights organisations remained peaceful despite the repression that their leaders and supporters faced. This may suggest that social struggles in pursuit of improved distribution of land, jobs, and so on are more likely to resort to violence than those that focus on more abstract civil and political rights. It is also instructive to note that this phenomenon should be viewed within the context of the state’s reaction to such groups.
It is important to note that outright political repression and violence were not the only instruments that the state used against the opposition. Other means, such as legislation, were also employed. For example, in an effort to control and regulate the activities of a large number of emergent civil society organisations at the forefront of the struggles, the state enacted the Non-Governmental Organisations Co-ordination Act No. 19 of 1990. This required NGOs to register with a central NGO bureau that was “ominously placed under the internal security secretariat of the Office of the President” (Ndegwa 1994; 1996). This law meant that the state could refuse to register civil society groups that it considered too adversarial. Hence, it forced the “NGOisation” of many social movements that wanted to operate within the law and criminalised many others.
Despite the ongoing, state-sponsored violence, new movements that challenged the status quo started crystallising. The first was the Forum for the Restoration of Democracy (FORD), launched on August 2, 1991 by, among others, Oginga Odinga and former parliamentarians Martin Shikuku and Masinde Muliro, who united with a group of younger, radical activists mainly drawn from the Law Society of Kenya (LSK) and the intelligentsia. Then, on October 28, two of Moi’s fiercest religious critics – Timothy Njoya and Bidan Mbugua (a former managing editor of Beyond Christian magazine, which the government had banned in 1988) – launched the Moral Alliance for Peace (MAP) with Professor Wangari Maathai and Martha Karua, among others. MAP signified the crystallisation of the activist clergy’s constant criticism into a more tangible institutional form. Moreover, it was an attempt at religious ecumenism in the midst of agreements among various pro-democracy activists that unity was crucial. Although MAP turned out to be of little consequence as more effective opposition groups emerged, it was an early indication that the Kenyan clergy and religious institutions could not be ignored.
The state reacted to these developments with its usual policies of terror and intimidation, but Moi could be placatory, too. For instance, he released Matiba, Rubia, and Raila Odinga in 1991. On the other hand, that July, former Kitutu Masaba MP George Anyona, former Air Force officer Augustine Njeru Kathangu, and university dons Isaiah Ngotho Kariuki and Edward Oyugi were all sentenced to seven years in jail for holding a seditious meeting in Nairobi.
However, with ever more countries suspending aid to Kenya amidst mounting pressure from various domestic social groups, Moi and KANU were clearly running out of options. On December 17, 1991, Moi convened yet another KANU Special Delegates Conference, which, among other things, agreed to repeal the contentious Section 2A of the constitution. Three days later, the constitutional amendment that legalised multi-partyism was finally enacted, ending decades of single-party rule in Kenya. The first opposition party – FORD – was registered on December 31. Clearly then, the vulnerabilities of the Moi/KANU regime presented some opportunities to the pro-reform groups, but they also allowed the conservative and opportunistic political and economic elites to reinvigorate themselves and remain relevant. For example, some wealthy old conservative politicians decamped from KANU and formed the Democratic Party with the explicit aim of preserving their collective self-interest.
Notwithstanding the repeal of Section 2A, it seems that Moi was motivated solely by a desire to remain in power for as long as possible. The ongoing violence that he sanctioned against those perceived to be anti-Moi/KANU, coupled with selective and limited registration of opposition parties and the retention of draconian electoral laws, ensured that the political playing field was never level under his rule. Moreover, the opposition remained deeply divided despite their understanding that unity was crucial if they were to defeat Moi in the 1992 election.

Incubating the constitutional change movement: the Coalition for National Convention

The Moi/KANU victory against a fragmented opposition in the 1992 general election became a transformative landmark as these struggles moved from agitating for changing the rules of the game to benefit political elites to more fundamental transformation of the purposes of the state to benefit Kenyans in general (Harbeson 1999). Despite emergent consensus for change, fragmentation among pro-reform actors deepened, especially due to competing diagnoses of the Kenyan problem as well as its cure. On the one hand, even before the 1992 general election, an emergent civil society formation coalescing around the Kenya Human Rights Commission (KHRC), RPP, and LSK called for fundamental changes in Kenyan basic law, arguing that the state reforms they envisaged were about more than political pluralism. This group saw the prevailing national debate as a search for the “most appropriate political system that would create the political conditions within which Kenyans can meaningfully achieve their social, cultural, economic and other related societal goals” (Mutua 2008: 100). For this group, such change was only possible through an overhaul of the constitution to get rid of Moi’s perverse tyrannical rule. After all, they argued, in addition to the multiple mutilations after independence, the original post-independence constitution was an imperial imposition, and therefore not a true reflection of the will of the people of Kenya. On the other hand, leaders of emergent political opposition parties were of the view that the solution to the Kenyan problem was technocratic management because the constitution was not that bad.
There were attempts to bridge these divisions before the 1992 general election. In May and June 1992, NCCK organised two symposia bringing together the different pro-change forces (Mutunga 1999; Mutua 2008; Nasong’o 2007). These symposia did not achieve the intended unity. Nonetheless, they were significant as they incubated the first constitutional change movement. Specifically, the Coalition for National Convention (CNC) – the first coalition of forces seeking a national dialogue on the way forward for Kenya after the repeal of Section 2A – was formed on June 17, during the preparations for the second symposium, which was to be held on July 11–12. The CNC was also significant for laying the foundations for the contentions that would underpin the next phase of the struggles.
The CNC brought together many unregistered groups (save for a few exceptions) with grievances against the state. These included youth and student organisations, workers’ rights and economic justice movements, human rights advocacy groups, democratisation and governance organisations as well as registered and unregistered opposition political parties. The organic emergence of these groups is testament to the deep-rooted anxieties experienced at different socioeconomic strata. Common among them was outrage over an economically predatory and anti-democratic state that they wanted to change.11
However, the CNC stagnated due to lack of decisive leadership and deep-rooted divisions between the two pro-reform groupings – civil society and the political parties (Mutunga 1999). It especially failed to attract support from mainstream opposition parties and religious organisations, which, although they participated in the national symposia (indeed, religious groups had organised them), were “horrified” that the CNC had adopted a radical civil society agenda that called for the “dissolution of parliament, the re-adoption of the 1963 Lancaster constitution [in the interim, and] the creation of a transitional government” (Mutua 2008: 100) as well as a total overhaul of the constitution through a national convention (Mutunga 1999; Kuria 1996). By contrast, opposition political parties and mainstream religious groups were only interested in reforms that would enable them to gain control of the state through free elections (Mutunga 1999; Maina 1998; Nzomo 2003, Katumanga 1999; Murunga and Nasong’o 2007). The leaders of the political parties feared that a new constitution “would inhibit the winning political parties from inheriting the machinery of violence and power reflected by the presidential authoritarianism” (Mutunga 1999: 35).
Instead of joining the CNC agitation, the mainstream religious groups’ initiatives – in particular the Catholic Church’s Justice and Peace Commission project and various National Council of Churches of Kenya processes – together with the mainstream opposition parties, formed the National Ecumenical Election Programme (NECEP). Many donors who had bankrolled the democratisation process also withheld support from the CNC, instead throwing their weight behind the NECEP.

Action and the transition to institutionalisation

The CNC dialogues, though short-lived, offered opportunities for those congregating around the idea that Kenya needed radical constitutional reform to contribute vigorously to the debate and, in the process, sowed the seeds for ensuing constitutional reform struggles. In addition, as Mutua (2008) observes, the defeat of a divided opposition by Moi/KANU, as the CNC had predicted, offered an opportunity for the first time in post-colonial Kenya for important debates to be led not by politicians and but by emergent human rights groups. More fundamentally for the cycles of contention model employed in this analysis, as the CNC declined into oblivion in early 1993, its agenda was bequeathed to a number of successors, some of which had previously opposed the CNC’s call for an overhaul of the constitution. One such grouping was the Catholic Church’s Kenya Episcopal Conference, which in March 1994 issued a pastoral letter calling for a new constitution to reflect the new multi-party reality in Kenya.
The most potent heir of the CNC agenda was the Kenya Human Rights Commission’s Model Constitution project, whose efforts kept the constitutional reform struggle on course by generating the next wave of protest (Mati 2013). Its efficacy lay in a strategy involving consultation, documentation, and dissemination in an effort to build a coalition of forces for constitutional review. This strategy was informed by lessons learned from the short-lived CNC that there was a need to craft an inter-identity alliance of different social groups as well as a need to appreciate Kenya’s deep cleavages due to its “ethnic, racial, regional, religious, generational, personality and gender” diversity (Mutunga 1999: 39). Bridging such cleavages would take time, as well as widespread consultation and negotiation.
Heeding these lessons, civil society organisations sought to do things differently by taking steps towards institutionalisation. Such institutionalisation accords with the three-phase/step process in social movement development discussed earlier in this chapter. In this phase, the social movement becomes bureaucratised as it matures, grows, and attracts different societal elements (Hoffer 1958; Katumanga 1999). This bureaucratisation results from the increasingly routinised nature of the movement’s activities, which calls for administrative and organisational skills on the part of the leadership (Katumanga 1999). Consequently, this phase requires leadership from practical men and women of action; otherwise, the movement will lose “its drive, become tame and experience paralysis and may ultimately atrophy” (Nasong’o 2007: 21).
The participants in the emergent struggle seemed to be well aware of these imperatives. Specifically, the Model Constitution project established a formal steering committee made up of members of the LSK, the International Commission of Jurists, and the Kenya Human Rights Commission. This committee led consultations in 1993 and 1994, resulting in the publication of a Proposal for a Model Constitution on November 3, 1994 at Ufungamano House (Mutunga 1999). This document enjoyed an enthusiastic reception, which seemed to indicate that interest in the constitutional reform project had already spread far beyond the handful of individuals and organisations that were actively involved (Mutunga 1999). The next step in broadening the appeal of the project should have been a Constitutional Caucus, which was held on December 9, 1994 at Ufungamano House. Unfortunately, this comprised 217 exclusively middle-class representatives of civil society and religious organisations, while the lower classes, women, Muslims, the youth and cooperative movements, and politicians were all conspicuous by their absence (Mutunga 1999). Nevertheless, the caucus had a significant impact on the institutionalisation of the agitation for a new constitution. According to Mutunga (1999: 59), Bishop John Njue of the Catholic Diocese of Embu and then deputy chair of the Episcopal Conference of Catholic Bishops conveyed a sense of moral outrage and spoke for many absent Kenyans when associating the socioeconomic and political maladies afflicting Kenya with its bad constitution. Importantly, he underscored the “God-given right” of the Kenyan people to draft their own constitution and as such tentatively supported the Proposal for a Model Constitution, likening it to a skeleton that needed some flesh. His interventions received widespread support from other participants.
Acutely aware that the Proposal for a Model Constitution reflected the opinions of the male middle class and not necessarily the whole of Kenyan society, the Model Constitution steering committee actively reached out to other groups, such as workers’ movements, women, youth, all political parties (including KANU), the business community, all religious groups, the media, and the lower classes in general. Consequently, support grew for the constitutional reform agenda. The steering committee took its task seriously and wanted to bring together all Kenyans to discuss and draft the constitution they desired.

4Cs and NCEC: inter-class alliance building and mass action

Following the 1994 Constitutional Caucus, the idea of constitutional reform gained significant traction because of its overwhelming endorsement by a variety of middle-class civil society and religious actors (Mutunga 1999). In response, on New Year’s Eve, President Moi – a skilful politician who never underrated his opponents – made a surprise announcement that Kenya needed a new constitution. He indicated that his government would invite foreign experts to collect views from across Kenya, draft a constitution, and hand it to parliament for debate and ratification (Mutua 2008). In essence, he made it clear that the constitutional review process had to be under his personal control and direction.
Seven days later, on January 6, 1995, the Model Constitution steering committee renamed the project the Citizens’ Coalition for Constitutional Change (4Cs) and announced the establishment of institutional structures to spearhead the struggle. Aware of the need for greater inclusion of different Kenyan social groups, 4Cs would have four co-chairs drawn from the Catholic Church, the Protestant churches – mainly NCCK-affiliated – women’s groups, and professional organisations (Mutunga 1999). It was destined to drive the first formal phase of the constitutional reform process from this moment onwards.
Moi’s New Year’s Eve announcement incensed the civil society and religious groups that were pushing for constitutional reform because they felt the president and KANU were insensitive to their demands for popular participation of the Kenyan people. In effect, this opened new frontiers for contention. Later chapters will discuss in greater detail the inviolability of the fundamental right of citizens to write their own constitution that 4Cs had already articulated. This remained one of the key pillars driving the contentions in the Kenyan constitutional reform project.
Ultimately, the president’s announcement turned out to be nothing more than hot air, designed to steal the thunder of the middle-class civil society groups that were coalescing around 4Cs and the Proposal for a Model Constitution. Nonetheless, it provided a golden opportunity to mobilise support for a constitutional reform project among the Kenyan people. More importantly, later, it allowed the nascent constitutional reform movement to utilise “accountability politics” when Moi reneged on his promise. This may explain the escalation of militancy in the push for a new constitution that marked the next phase of the struggle. Of significance here, as will be shown below, is that, despite repeated public pronouncements and promises to the nation, Moi and KANU spent the next two years tarnishing the idea of a comprehensive constitutional review.
In April 1997, after more than two years of inaction on the part of the government, 4Cs convened the first National Convention Assembly (NCA), which brought together over 500 representatives from civil society, social movements, and political organisations (except KANU) at Jumuhia, Limuru. This gave birth to the National Convention Executive Council (NCEC) – the NCA’s executive implementation organ (Mutua 2008; Mutunga 1999). Thereafter, it was the NCEC that challenged the Moi/KANU regime head-on through mass action and political lobbying for reform in the run-up to the December 1997 general election. These mass protests were so widespread that they threatened to derail the election.
In effect, the NCEC/NCA was a coalition of different social formations and groups that was established to push for democratisation and constitutional reform. The NCEC escalated its activities by taking the message of the National Convention directly to the grassroots after it became clear that the Moi/KANU regime would continue to oppose constitutional review. It campaigned aggressively for a boycott of the 1997 general election unless comprehensive reforms were put in place. Given that this was an election year, the Moi/KANU regime was particularly vulnerable to mobilisations and mass action, which went hand in hand with widespread civic education led by religious organisations such as the Catholic Justice and Peace Commission, NCCK, and NECEP. Although the state fought to keep the civic educators at bay, restrictions on their activities merely served to harden the clergy as well as their congregations. The upsurge in protests in many urban centres demonstrated the firm resolve of those at the heart of these struggles.
By blaming the dictatorial state for many of Kenya’s socioeconomic and political woes, the NCEC managed to sensitise the lower classes by assigning meaning to their collective suffering, identifying with their cause, and bringing grassroots struggles to the fore. However, it failed to mobilise sufficient mass support or fully integrate all of the subalterns’ everyday struggles into its campaign.12 On the other hand, it was able to link the legal reform agenda to the sociology, economics, culture, and politics of the country while also raising pertinent questions on the distribution of national resources, welfare, and pervasive poverty.
In a bid to increase its support among ordinary Kenyans, the NCEC tried to convince grassroots movements such as Muungano wa Wanavijiji to throw their weight behind the constitutional reform project. As such, while it never succeeded in becoming a true mass movement, it played an important role in pushing the frontiers of political and constitutional reform and managed to associate its narrow constitutional reform campaign with more general Kenyan socioeconomic and political struggles. Consequently, when the NCEC called for mass action, ordinary citizens responded. From then on, as Mutunga (1999) writes, it did not matter whether the NCEC leadership was in step with the country’s religious and political leaders. With ordinary citizens on its side, the NCEC called for mass protests to push for reform before the general election could be held. Its clarion call was “No Reforms, No Elections.” As a result, between May and October 1997, Kenya was a country at war with itself as NCEC supporters engaged the police and state-hired/sponsored thugs in running battles during a series of mass protests.
The first mass action started with a procession from Nairobi city centre to the historic Kamukunji Grounds on May 3. The marchers, who were led by Timothy Njoya, one of the co-convenors of the NCEC, were viciously attacked by a group of state-sponsored thugs calling themselves Jeshi la Mzee (Army of the Big Man) (Mutunga 1999). More significant, however, was the fact that members of the middle class marched alongside ordinary workers, which revealed that the reform movement was gaining support across the whole of Kenyan society. Interestingly, the youth emerged as the true leaders of this demonstration, as they organised their fellow marchers and countered police intimidation with force (Mutunga 1999).
Subsequently, there were calls for a series of mass actions across Kenya, some of which coincided with symbolic events or holidays, such as May 31 (the eve of Madaraka [Self-Governance] Day) and June 17 (Budget Day). Further protests were held on July 7, August 8, September 9, and October 10. All were met with raw state brutality and many members of the public died at the hands of the police.
Of all these mass protests, the one that fundamentally altered the rules of the game was the July 7 (Saba Saba) demonstration. Police descended on demonstrators who had taken refuge at All Saints Cathedral, beating many of them, including several opposition MPs, members of the clergy, and leaders of the NCEC. Both Njoya, the co-convenor, and Kepta Ombati, the NCEC’s youth leader, were beaten senseless and left for dead. The floor of the church was covered in blood and strewn with empty teargas canisters (Weekly Review 1997a). Twenty-one people were killed (Njoya 2007). The massacre incensed the clergy, who had, up to that point, been much less militant. Six days after the incident, there was a cleansing ceremony of the cathedral. Preaching from the Book of Daniel, Bishop Gitari, who led the ceremony, told the highly charged congregation that if President Moi did not change his ways, his days were numbered.13
The July 7 massacre was a turning point in the struggle for constitutional reform because the state capitulated after coming under increasing pressure and condemnation from the international media, diplomats, and donor countries that threatened to place further economic sanctions on the Moi regime unless he agreed to open dialogue with the opposition. In the wake of this criticism, a senior police officer visited Njoya in hospital and offered a personal apology for the “violent invasion of the All Saints Cathedral and for the attack on Njoya” (Weekly Review 1997a). Moi himself joined the chorus of those calling on the police to tone down their use of violence. More importantly, on July 15, a clearly besieged Moi summoned the leading clergy from all faiths (Christians and Muslims) to the State House to discuss a way out of the seemingly endless spiral of violence (Weekly Review 1997b; 1997c). He assured them that he was now in favour of reform and specifically asked for their assistance to end the impasse by mediating between KANU and the NCEC. The assembled members of the clergy agreed to do just that.14

IPPG: political class consensus

Moi’s game plan was not limited to appeasing and neutralising an angry clergy. While he was talking to Kenya’s religious leaders, his emissaries were in dialogue with the opposition, offering a political settlement that promised initial minimal reforms, followed by comprehensive reforms after the election. The opposition fell for the bait, abandoned their support for the NCEC’s mass actions, and returned to parliament for a round of negotiations (Mutunga 1999; Mutua 2008). Activists and politicians close to these developments have advanced several theories to explain the politicians’ behaviour at this juncture.
For instance, both Mutunga (1999) and Mutua (2008) argue that a majority of opposition politicians were already feeling irrelevant, given that the NCEC was clearly in control of the struggle, the mobilisation structures, and the networks. And most of them did not wish to be driven even further into political irrelevance by heeding the NCEC’s call for a boycott of the election. Indeed, Kiraitu Murungi (2000 cited in Mutua 2008: 107–8), one of the leading lights of the subsequent Inter-Parties Parliamentary Group (IPPG) deal, argued that the NCEC drove MPs into KANU’s arms because it deliberately fomented the crisis. Secondly, the NCEC had started to ridicule MPs who were prepared to talk to KANU. In IPPG, these politicians saw an opportunity to neutralise the NCEC and in the process ensure that it “was no longer the solo voice of all pro-democracy voices in Kenya” (Murungi 2000: 80). This demonstrates that politicians had a clear plan to either co-opt or arrest the NCEC’s agenda in order to remain dominant. When the NCEC resisted co-option, the political elites jumped ship. By doing so, they acted as a unified force to derail the reform train.
The NCEC’s reform train also stalled due to the role played by external donors. Specifically, the events of July 7 left Western diplomats wary of the possibility of an election boycott and its potential effects on both the stability of Kenya and their own countries’ interests (Ng’ethe and Katumanga 2003: 332; Mutunga 1999; Murungi 2000). Thereafter, they began pressurising the NCEC to enter into dialogue with a regime that had made it clear that it did not recognise the NCEC as a legitimate player in Kenyan politics. Therefore, the IPPG initiative came as a relief to many stakeholders, including Kenyan MPs, foreign powers, political parties, and religious groups (Mutunga 1999). It was, for many, a “rational” and “less threatening” alternative to the NCEC’s “radical” agenda (Mutua 2008: 109).
Significantly, the success of Moi’s overtures through the IPPG initiative reveals underlying cleavages within the reform movement. Nonetheless, while the IPPG process placated foreign interests, the clergy, and opposition political parties, it resulted in yet another betrayal of the popular will. Its outcome preserved the dominant elite’s interests as it gave Moi and KANU a much-needed respite. Moreover, the IPPG deal points to how the entrenched interests of the so-called donor countries have subverted meaningful transformation (Brown 2001).
In the end, the IPPG initiative proved sufficient to overcome the NCEC’s campaign to derail the 1997 general election, as only Kenneth Matiba’s Saba Saba Asili persevered with the boycott. However, the opposition political elite’s decision to close ranks and participate in the election left many activists feeling bitter and betrayed (Mutua 2008; Mutunga 1999). It also led to greater distrust of all politicians, whether in government or the opposition.15
More fundamentally, the IPPG deal marked the deflation of another movement at the heart of the struggle for transformative reform in Kenya. As we shall see in Chapter 3, one the key components of the IPPG package was the enactment of the CKRC Act of 1998. However, this did not result in general agreement among all of the stakeholders involved in the constitution review process. Moreover, religious groups and civil society felt that the CKRC Act did not effectively encompass Kenya’s various interest groups, and that the process was captive to the narrow interests of the political elite to the exclusion of the Kenyan people (4Cs 2006). In the midst of this acrimony between different interests, protests by civil society and religious organisations against the government-controlled process crystallised into a movement that came to be known as the Ufungamano Initiative, which agitated for a people-driven constitutional review process. Chapter 3 provides an in-depth analysis of the emergence of this movement.

Conclusion

Kenya’s political landscape underwent a dramatic transformation from the early 1990s. These changes were products as well as manifestations of deep structural contradictions throughout society. Specifically, a social conflict derived from a crisis of economic and political resource distribution ensured that the political economy had to be reconfigured. Initially, this conflict was an intensive intra-elite competition for access and control of rents and patronage networks due to the dominant elite’s inability to maintain its collective means of appropriation (Haugerud 1995). However, it quickly escalated as a breakaway elite faction sought alliances with subalterns and the middle class to force concessions from the dominant Moi/KANU elite faction. The concessions in question were internal party reforms and subsequently the repeal of Section 2A to make Kenya a multi-party state. Nonetheless, as these changes only addressed the grievances of the competing political elite factions, the structural contradictions in the Kenyan state continued.
The resolution of these inherent contradictions was a key demand of a group of secular and mainly human rights and governance-oriented civil society activists who sought a complete overhaul of the Kenyan constitution. In an effort to achieve these reforms, these civil society groups mobilised for collective action. In their evolution, these struggles had to contend with Kenya’s inter-ethnic, religious, generational, and class power politics and their influence on political and social mobilisation. As such, for any progress to be made, it was imperative to form alliances that reflected a consensus across different social groups. However, due to their hybridity, such alliances were, by their very nature, complex, unstable, and precarious.
These social mobilisations co-evolved in direct opposition to the actions and political behaviour of the state, political, and religious elites. As such, they were often frustrated by the activities of those elites, especially when the latter closed ranks under the auspices of the IPPG initiative. Nevertheless, although the IPPG deal stole much of the NCEC’s thunder, the lack of a new constitution following the 1997 general election, coupled with ongoing socioeconomic discontent throughout the country, meant that it was not long before a new wave of contention started to build in the form of the Ufungamano Initiative. As Chapter 3 will show, religious leaders and their institutions were the dominant players in this movement. Yet, this phase of the struggle was also characterised by complex inter- and intra-class, religious, generational, and gender alliances and fragmentations.
