5    Success and its paradoxes

Introduction

Protest movements do not always achieve their goals within the duration of their existence. In some instances, they die or metamorphose into different organisational forms. Sometimes, their action repertoires shift across time. For example, a movement may move from utilising confrontational strategies to institutionalised cooperative politics. What determines such shifts? This chapter analyses changes in the action repertoires of the Ufungamano Initiative – a remarkable movement that mobilised citizens to contribute to the drafting of a new constitution. This posed a serious legitimacy crisis for the exclusive, state-run Constitution of Kenya Review Commission process. As a result, an autocratic and intolerant regime was forced to capitulate and open space for mass participation through a merger of the two processes. While this merger denotes the movement’s success, it nonetheless dissipated the Ufungamano Initiative’s energy and focus. As such, this is also a story of limits. This chapter probes the paradoxes of success for the Ufungamano Initiative and is relevant to debates on what success portends for protest movements.
The critical question is: given its success in challenging the state, why did the Ufungamano Initiative agree to merge with the state-run constitutional review process? The chapter explains that this was a product of three interdependent factors. First, a merger was possible because dominant forces in the Ufungamano Initiative controlled the purse strings and steered the movement in the direction that served their interests. This marked the triumph of conservative forces in the struggles between a wide variety of interests that had converged under the Ufungamano Initiative banner to challenge the KANU–NDP regime.
Second, this group’s dominance and the eventual merger reflect tensions in the internal structures of the movement. Oliver and Myers (2003:1) observe that “movement trajectories evolve … and are constrained, but not determined, by structures.” This is because frictions between structures may be a movement’s death knell, or at least force it to metamorphose into something else. In our case, the relationships between structures, leadership and followers, resources and frames, determined the path taken by the Ufungamano Initiative, especially in regards to changing its action repertoires. As we saw in Chapter 4, there were myriad structural and leadership conflicts within the Ufungamano Initiative that facilitated hegemonic co-optation and the organisation of consent through a merger.
Third, the merger was shaped by interactions between sections of the Ufungamano Initiative leadership and the chairman of the rival state-led process. This is consistent with the findings of Strang and Soule (1998), Pitcher et al. (1978), Meyer and Staggenborg (1996), and Oliver and Myers (2003), all of whom observe that movement dynamics are shaped by interactions with other actors. Specifically, state actions (either accommodating or reacting strongly against movements) instigate or inhibit movements and their action repertoires. Movements take lessons from such interactions. For the Ufungamano Initiative, these lessons prompted the dominant faction to accept the offer of a merger.

First steps towards a merger

The merger of the two competing processes was influenced by a number of factors. Key among these was the role of leadership in both the Ufungamano Initiative and its rival. This is exhibited specifically by the roles played by Mutava Musyimi in the Ufungamano Initiative and Yash Pal Ghai in the state-run Constitution of Kenya Review Commission (hereafter CKRC). In particular, Ghai, a Kenyan and an internationally renowned constitutional law expert who was appointed to lead the CKRC in November 2000,1 had a tremendous impact on strategic interactions between the Ufungamano Initiative and the state-run process. His role in the merger accords with Tarrow’s (1998 [1994]: 142) assertion that “widespread contention produces externalities that give challengers at least a temporary advantage and allows them to overcome the weaknesses in their resource base.” Specifically, this chapter illustrates that Ghai’s appointment was the crucial external force that provided opportunities for strategic interactions with the Ufungamano Initiative leadership and consequently made a merger between the two processes feasible. That said, other factors also pushed the two sides towards a merger, such as resource constraints and outright intrigue among some of the dominant actors within the Ufungamano Initiative, who seized the moment to deliver the movement and its PCK processes to the state.
The Moi/KANU regime’s nomination of Ghai to lead the CKRC was hailed as a masterstroke by government supporters and critics alike (Mutua 2008; Lumumba 2008). More fundamentally, it generated conditions that enabled “strategic interactions” between the competing processes. Therefore, it is important to understand the circumstances surrounding his appointment.
Ghai was handpicked to chair the CKRC by an increasingly desperate Moi government. This was because, while the Ufungamano Initiative’s structures were insufficient to cultivate a genuine citizens’ movement (see Chapter 4), it did mobilise enough support to launch a substantial challenge to the legitimacy of the state-run process. This success is attributed to two factors. First, the Ufungamano Initiative utilised well-established religious infrastructure – arguably the best grassroots network for communicating directly with citizens prior to the cellphone revolution in Kenya. Second, the country’s religious leaders commanded a very loyal following because they were both bold and prophetic.2 This enabled the movement to bring pressure to bear on the state, which forced Moi to seek ways to save face. One of these was the president’s nomination of Ghai to lead the CKRC. According to Harun Ndubi:
Moi was desperate for international acceptance because he had lost a lot of ground. One of the results he hoped to reap from getting Ghai on his side was that he would be seen to be serious and ready for reforms. Ghai had an internationally credible record and reputation.3
As we shall see later, Ghai was able to negotiate a merger between the two processes because he had great fortitude, integrity, and credibility, an impeccable local and international reputation, and, crucially, a strategic intellect that enabled him to ease tensions between the two competing elite formations without alienating either. He employed these traits in his dealings with all of the participants in the review process, winning universal trust along the way.
At this point, it is worth describing the circumstances that led to Ghai’s recruitment and the role he subsequently played in reconciling the two processes. This narrative will help to explain how protest movements’ political opportunities sometimes expand – irrespective of their internal resources – because of contenders’ actions.
In his own words, Ghai was “pulled reluctantly” into the Kenyan constitutional struggle in September 2000 by the country’s attorney general, Amos Wako, who had been one of his students during his time as a professor at the University of Dar-es-Salaam. At the time, Ghai was based at the University of Wisconsin-Madison in the United States while on sabbatical from his law professorship at the University of Hong Kong. Wako paid him a visit and asked if he would consider joining and chairing the CKRC. Ghai initially turned down the offer and pointed out that he had lived in exile for a long time, ever since an arbitrary detention order imposed by Jomo Kenyatta’s regime had forced him to flee the country. Additionally, he was acutely aware of the deep divisions and intense political oppression that had led to the emergence of two parallel constitutional reform processes, and naturally identified with the Ufungamano Initiative because many of his civil society acquaintances were driving it. He simply did not want to be associated with a government process that seemed hell-bent on tearing the country apart.4
Nevertheless, Wako urged Ghai to accept the appointment because everybody respected him and believed that he could play a very constructive role.5 Ghai remained reluctant, but four days of persuasion finally melted his resistance and he agreed to call some of his friends at the heart of the Ufungamano Initiative and ask for their opinions. The mixed messages he received during these phone calls highlighted the deep cleavages within the movement. Some of his contacts urged him to dismiss the offer immediately as accepting it would only boost Moi’s legitimacy. For instance, Kamau Kuria, a prominent member of the NCEC and the Ufungamano Initiative as well as a former chair of the Kenyan Law Society, recalled: “I remember talking to Ghai. I told him: ‘You can help Kenya, but you cannot help Kenya by serving in that commission.’ Moi is not ready to have a democratic constitution.”6 Others advised him to delay his decision and consult widely before making up his mind.
In the end, Ghai agreed to return to Kenya and do just that. First, though, he insisted on a guarantee from Wako and the government that he would have a free hand to talk to members of the regime, civil society, opposition politicians, and other stakeholders with a view to reconciling the two processes. After finally arriving in Kenya, he explained his mission to prominent government and opposition politicians and to Christian, Muslim, and Hindu leaders of the Ufungamano Initiative. He also clarified that he had not yet accepted the nomination to chair the CKRC, but was minded to do so as long as the two processes agreed to a merger. According to Ghai, most of the people he met were opposed to the idea:
Almost everyone said no. [Mwai] Kibaki said no. [Simeon] Nyachae said no. Civil society organisations said no. Even [Mutava] Musyimi was not keen. I was almost giving up and about to go back when I received a message from Archbishop Ndingi requesting that we meet again. So I met with the Ufungamano Initiative a second time.7
In the course of this second meeting, the clergy finally agreed to explore the possibilities of a merger if government was also willing. From these consultations, Ghai realised that even though the Ufungamano Initiative had managed to launch a substantial challenge to the state’s reform process, it was riddled with limitations and uncertainties. He summarised his assessment as follows:
I felt that Ufungamano was running out of steam because they did not have much money for their work and their commissioners were complaining. I also knew Oki [the chair of the PCK] a little … When I came here [Kenya], [we] had three one-on-one meetings and I liked him very much. I thought he had vision and skill and should be playing an important role. But I also thought the Ufungamano Initiative would fizzle out. I did not think they could sustain it much further because of the intimidation from the government, and they were running out of money. I felt this was an opening to rescue them in a way. Oki was extremely keen on a merger.8
Abubakar Zein, a PCK commissioner at the time and later a member of the CKRC, explained why he supported Ghai’s proposal:
We took cognisance that the government side [also] had a critical sector that they represented. For the wellbeing of the nation, it was important to have a common process that would give birth to one constitution rather than have two processes that would give birth to two constitutions and ultimately lead to further conflict and possibly violence. Secondly, there was an ongoing debate within the Ufungamano Initiative fraternity and especially outside the technical work being done by the PCK. This was in an attempt to answer some fundamental questions. One of the fundamental questions was: how do you bring into being a constitution that would be prepared under Ufungamano? The debate went from “it will be a moral document that would act as a beacon of a possible future for Kenya that people can work with as a vision” to [launching] an armed struggle to bring a revolution in the country … But many people recognised the value of a common process. With hindsight, every time we have worked together for a cause, we have reached very high heights in this country, including independence. But every time we have pulled in different directions, it has always led to violence.9
Once he understood the dynamics within the Ufungamano Initiative, Ghai realised that there was an opportunity to negotiate a merger between the two processes. Moreover, he recognised that, although the CKRC had legal backing, it represented a minority position in the country and lacked legitimacy. Senior KANU and NDP politicians, such as Musalia Mudavadi and Raila Odinga, betrayed their fear of the Ufungamano Initiative through daily attacks on the PCK’s legality with a view to discrediting it. But these attacks merely enhanced its legitimacy (Mutua 2008: 119). Ghai therefore recognised that the Ufungamano Initiative must play a major role in any future process. Its goodwill was paramount for the fruitful continuation and conclusion of the government-led process. As such, the state could ill afford to ignore it.10
At the same time, Ghai’s credibility in the eyes of many actors within the Ufungamano Initiative, the Western donor community, and some members of the government helped him to advance the notion of a merger. Most of the country’s stakeholders had faith in him, and others were willing to give him the benefit of doubt. According to Ochieng Khairallah, a former coordinator of the National Youth Movement and an active member of the NCEC and the Ufungamano Initiative, “people, even in the Ufungamano community, expressed very strong confidence in Yash Pal Ghai … We felt that with him there, we would be able to articulate the collective aspirations of the Kenyan people.”11 Similarly, Harun Ndubi, who was vice-chair of the influential Law Society of Kenya at the time, recalled:
People said, “Okay, Wako may be devious but Yash Ghai is a steadfast person, let’s give him a chance.” As the Law Society, we actually feared and wondered whether we could be assured that Ghai would deliver a constitution. And because of the IPPG manipulation, that fear was seen to be legitimate.12
Such views were widely shared within the Ufungamano Initiative.13 Many “could not separate Ghai from Wako, Moi, and Odinga – the triumvirate that had appointed him chair of the CKRC” and felt that he could end up a Moi dupe (Mutua 2008: 120). At the same time, the movement’s leadership had to think strategically if they were to achieve their ultimate goal: a new constitution that resulted from a people-centred review process. Ndubi captures the dilemma they faced when Ghai first broached the idea of a merger:
There were fears that if we rejected engagement completely, the government had the capacity to run away with the process and discredit us as the people who want a constitution yet, when the government cedes space, they do not want to engage.14
In summary, three factors led Ghai to believe that he had a chance of negotiating a merger: the Moi/KANU regime’s desperation; the Ufungamano Initiative’s awareness of its own limitations; and the fact that influential people on both sides had confidence in him. He therefore offered to mediate between the two processes up to the point when both were willing to meet and negotiate face-to-face. The clergy agreed to this proposal during their second meeting with Ghai. Meanwhile, both Raila Odinga (chair of the PSC) and Moi seemed open to the idea of a merger. However, Moi was concerned about Ghai working with the Ufungamano Initiative, given that it had sought to create a rival review process. Ghai assured him that a merger would ensure that any differences between the two processes would soon dissolve.
Thereafter, Ghai focused on arranging meetings between the two sides in the hope that these would enable them to overcome their mutual hostility. His first move was to invite Raila Odinga and Mutava Musyimi for a chat at his home, without telling either of them that the other would be present. According to Ghai, “They were both quite surprised and not very happy to see each other. But at least they had met.” During the meeting, Ghai drove home the importance of them talking to each other directly: “I said to them: ‘Come on. If we cannot talk to each other, how are we going to make this process happen? We have to start building some trust.’”15
Oliver and Myers (2003: 4) argue that
Political opportunity is best understood as a multidimensional space in which some groups or actions are facilitated or responded to by political elites or institutions, while others are repressed or ignored. For this reason, it is best to think of political opportunity as a matrix of probabilities, where each element is the probability that a particular kind of action will meet with a particular kind of response or action from particular kinds of other actors.
In this instance, Ghai’s refusal to chair the CKRC until it became a joint commission that encompassed both the state-run process and the Ufungamano Initiative was the catalyst for a series of events that expanded the latter’s political opportunities. This raises the critical question: why was Ghai so keen on a merger? According to him, he wanted to “strengthen the integrity and honesty of the commission in the service of Kenyans given the heavy polarisation of the country along shades of religion, ethnicity, generation, class, etc.”16
It is notable that Moi did not seek someone else to chair the CKRC even after Ghai refused to take the oath of office alongside the other commissioners. Widely interpreted as tacit support for his efforts to merge the two processes,17 this provided a seismic political boost for the Ufungamano Initiative and effectively reduced the power disparity between the two sides. It also convinced some members of the Ufungamano Initiative to rethink their opposition to the merger. In addition to refusing to take the oath of office, Ghai declined all offers of government money and other resources until the merger was concluded. Such displays of integrity averted any accusations of personal bias towards the PSC.
Ghai was well aware that the poisonous nature of Kenya’s domestic politics had created the polarised environment that was ripping the country apart, but he also knew that the very people who had generated the impasse held the key to its resolution. Therefore, he pursued negotiations with the two sides’ principal administrative organs – the PSC and the Steering Council – rather than their respective implementing arms – the CKRC and the PCK.
The merger proposal elicited a range of responses within the Ufungamano Initiative that may be arranged into three main categories. Some members fully embraced the proposal and worked towards its achievement. Others were more cautious and advocated “principled negotiations.” Finally, the third group remained steadfastly opposed to the merger. This is not to suggest that these three groups were homogeneous in their reasons for embracing or opposing the proposal. Indeed, there were substantial differences within each of them. Nevertheless, this broad categorisation illuminates the major cleavages within the movement that ultimately led to its demise following the successful completion of the merger.

Those who supported the merger

This group mainly consisted of what were seen as reactionary or conservative forces, most notably the leaders of opposition political parties and religious groups. As we have seen, these were also the dominant forces in the Ufungamano Initiative. The same group of actors had abandoned the NCEC in 1997 to join KANU in the IPPG negotiations, and they were the last to embrace the idea of a parallel process. However, they also included a number of PCK commissioners. Their reasons for supporting the merger ranged from political economy to principled pragmatism, and from strategic considerations to frustration over the Ufungamano Initiative’s internal cleavages. In other words, in this respect, they were a highly heterogeneous group.
The PCK commissioners who supported the merger were generally motivated by the political economy of the reform process. Specifically, they knew that the Ufungamano Initiative had limited funds compared to the massive resources that the government had at its disposal. According to Ghai, “the big money” that was handed to the CKRC commissioners increased the likelihood of a merger because their counterparts in the PCK yearned for similar pecuniary and other benefits.18 The dearth of financial resources therefore made the Ufungamano Initiative more amenable to the notion of a common process. This confirms that, although it enjoyed greater legitimacy than the state-run process, and despite its success in mobilising the citizenry and collecting their views, the movement was unable to muster the necessary human and financial resources to draft a new constitution.
Unsurprisingly, the stark reality of its financial position caused tension within the movement: “Some groups, like the SDP, … among others, did not want a merger, yet they were not bringing a single penny to the process. We were really straining.”19 Meanwhile, resentful of the financial burdern that it had to bear, at one point the Catholic Church threatened to withdraw all of its resources unless the Ufungamano Initiative’s leadership was changed. Similarly, the secretary general of the NCCK, Mutava Musyimi, criticised the relatively modest financial contributions of the Muslim community, regardless of the fact that his own organisation’s much larger contributions allowed him to become the movement’s “first among equals.”20
One former member of the Ufungamano Secretariat, Suba Churchill, recalled that there was far from universal support for Musyimi at the time:
There was a perception that Mutava Musyimi was becoming a little bit pushy, intolerant and dictatorial. Issues were not exhaustively discussed before decisions were made. I remember in particular some press conferences the Ufungamano Initiative held in which the press statement had been prepared earlier. It was the religious community and especially Mutava Musyimi, coming from a religious background where consultations are not so exhaustively done, that came up with the practice … [It] offended a number of people.21
Indeed, it was this sort of dictatorial behaviour – rather than some actors’ failure to raise funds – that generated the most antagonism. For instance, Abdiraham Wandati, who served on the Fundraising Committee, noted:
There was always this feeling that some of the partners had more information than others. For example, by default, the NCCK secretary general became the convener and he always sprung a fast one on all of us. Every time we sat down to write a fundraising proposal, even before we presented it, he would say: “Look, I have already submitted a proposal.” So it was easier for him to mobilise funding, and that gave him more leverage because, as you know, he who pays the piper chooses the tune.22
Ultimately, those who controlled the purse strings determined the movement’s direction of travel on most issues, including the merger. In this regard, as Timothy Njoya noted: “Musyimi bought us by providing funds for the initiative to meet, produce papers, the Secretariat, etc. He bought us, [then] sold us to Yash Ghai.”23 Such resentment certainly weakened the Ufungamano Initiative, even though it was usually kept under wraps.24
Given the arduous nature of the reform process, the dearth of funds, and the incessant squabbles among the Ufungamano Initiative’s constituent groups, it could be said that many of those who leapt at the chance of a merger were motivated primarily by pragmatism. Furthermore, those who embraced the proposal, especially the PCK commisssioners and opposition politicians, argued that, since the principal point of contention had been over the issue of inclusion, there was no real reason why the Ufungamano Initiative should continue to isolate itself, given that the government now seemed willing to compromise.25 This position was also informed by doubts over what was possible. Wandati captured this sentiment by asking:
If we continued to collect the views of Kenyans and indeed write a constitution, at what time would our draft become legally binding unless we deposed the government and came up with a people’s parliament to ratify it? We did not see that happening.26
Another aspect of the pro-merger group’s pragmatism was its desire to avoid further bloodshed. The Moi/KANU state and its NDP allies continued to mete out violence against organisations associated with the Ufungamano Initiative, and the movement’s de facto leaders feared an even greater crisis if they persisted with their alternative review process, in part because of the legitimacy challenge that they themselves had initiated against the regime. As Ghai noted:
Even on the weekend I came to get a feel of the environment on the ground after being wooed by Wako, people were being shot at in the streets … and even while we were negotiating, petrol bombs were thrown at the meeting in Kisumu. I was very worried. A lot of people told me they were really worried and this merger could be a chance to avert a disaster.27
However, according to Archbishop David Gitari, although the clergy were among the most pragmatic members of the Ufungamano Initiative, Ghai still had some work to do:
Ghai convinced us that the merger was the right way to go … [W]e were not the ones in power … It was therefore imperative that we engage with them, and work together to bring change. I do not think we wanted to make our own constitution and leave it at that. To become relevant, it had to go through parliament.28
Thereafter, the clergy set about convincing others. Of course, many opposition politicians were minded to get on board, as they were in danger of losing out altogether should the Ufungamano Initiative persist with its independent process. Memories of the NCEC’s isolation after its refusal to engage with the IPPG process were still fresh in their minds, and the merger proposal presented a perfect opportunity for them to follow a script that was broadly similar to the IPPG deal.29 They were also keen to destroy civil society’s hegemony within the Ufungamano Initiative.
The clergy’s embrace of the merger proposal suggests conscious, pragmatic, and strategic appreciation that they had to engage with the state in order to remain relevant, along with a strong suspicion that their tenuous political allies would abandon the struggle, given half a chance. Meanwhile, the politicians feared that the Ufungamano Initiative would become so powerful that it would start to dictate the political direction of the country and threaten their collective interests. Such fears may explain why some of them, including Phoebe Asiyo, the leader of the Kenyan Women’s Political Caucus, “played pivotal roles behind the curtains, to negotiate and offer themselves operative opportunities” by agreeing to work with the PSC.30 In addition, some opposition politicians were worried that Musyimi already had designs on the presidency.31 This perception “sent the political class scampering but also spelt doom for the Ufungamano Initiative.”32 All of this suggests that different groups were trying to outsmart each other in the hope of becoming the dominant force in the movement and having the final say on whether to accept the merger proposal.
Although it was almost inevitable that the opposition politicians would eventually declare their support for the merger, some of them demurred at first through fear of being labelled sell-outs. Ghai recalled: “Television cameras were following me everywhere. Politicians who came to see me had to make sure there were no press people around. Some, like James Orengo, Kiraitu Murungi, Charity Ngilu, and Beth Mugo, came at night.”33

The “principled” middle group

The second group, which lay between the pro- and anti-merger extremes, comprised organisations traditionally allied to the NCEC. These included secular human rights civil society groups and youth movements. This suggests that the cleavages caused by the merger proposal and the entire constitutional reform project had a generational, as well as an ideological, angle. These groups were neither excited by the proposal nor totally opposed to it. Rather, they were wary of the government’s intentions and so gave it only qualified support. It was members of this group who brought key issues for negotiation to the table, including:
The powers of the CKRC, its independence from the Executive and its relationship to the PSC, the number of Commissioners, and the manner of their appointment, a National Referendum on the Constitution, the scope of the Review Process, the mandate and composition of the National Constitutional Conference and the legal status of the CKRC in the Constitution.
(Mutua 2008: 121)
They also advocated for what Downey and Rohlinger (2008: 4) call a “strategic trade-off.” Ochieng Khairallah, one of the youth representatives in the Ufungamano Initiative, articulated young reformers’ position on the merger:
For us, the constitution was about ourselves, about our lives, and about our future. We wanted this constitution to be processed in the best manner possible and we were not convinced that a merger could easily yield those intentions. We wanted principled engagements and outcomes that had fidelity to the very principles that the Ufungamano Initiative purported to stand for.34
Khairallah and his cohorts’ stance was predicated on their understanding that genuine constitution making is a principled dialogue in the collective interest of all citizens rather than the zero-sum game that KANU had been playing. They feared that the KANU power elite may not yield space for effective interest articulation and dialogue after the merger, which would allow the regime’s manipulative tendencies resurface and derail the review process, denying Kenyans a new constitution. They also argued that the merger could sweep the process away from the people and towards the intrigues of vested interests. Such an eventuality “could only be checked if KANU felt that somebody had the capacity to effectively respond through protests. We felt that the merger would take away the people’s interest and confidence in the process. And this it did, substantially.”35
In an effort to placate this group, Ghai, in his mediation role, wrote a lengthy reflection piece titled “Proposals for a Common Process of Constitution Review” on December 25, 2000. His aim was to provide a way forward in dealing with the issues the youth had raised, and his paper did indeed give dissenting voices something to work with. Ghai pointed out that the two processes were already in agreement on the need to overhaul the constitution in a process that would be acceptable to majority of Kenyans. This point of convergence, he argued, should be utilised to give Kenyans the constitution they desired. He also highlighted the need for a consensual and cooperative political process. This was a clear departure from the winner-takes-all system that KANU and NDP had attempted to apply up to that point. Ghai then suggested that the main contentions between the two competing processes were based on two sets of laws. While the Ufungamano Initiative adhered to the Safari Park agreements that were enshrined in the 1998 CKRC Amendment Act, the PSC had amended that act in 2000 and then formulated the CKRC on the basis of the amended legislation. Therefore, to merge the two processes, there was a need to harmonise the legal basis for a new, common process.
In the same paper, Ghai argued for a departure from the prevailing (mis)conception that the CKRC was an organ for negotiating a new constitution. Instead, everyone should understand that its role was to facilitate consultations among Kenyans to determine the constitution they wanted. The negotiations, Ghai argued, should be left to a subsequent National Constitutional Conference, and a draft constitution from that body should then be subjected to a national referendum. This would ensure that the whole process remained faithful to the ultimate sovereignty of the Kenyan people. Ghai proposed that the key organs throughout the review process should be the CKRC, constituency forums, the National Constitutional Conference, parliament, and the Kenyan people, especially when the referendum was held. His proposal also spelt out the respective roles of each of these organs. As the role of parliament had been extremely contentious, Ghai proposed a compromise in which it would be obliged to enact the National Constitutional Conference’s draft constitution. According to Ghai, if the state provided an enabling environment and guaranteed peace, this model would ensure genuine, open, and transparent popular participation and negotiations among the people, the state, and the organs of the review process. Furthermore, he insisted that the organs of the review process must be accountable to the public and advocated comprehensive civic education to ensure maximum public participation. These proposals later came to define much of the act that directed the review process after the merger was formalised.
Ultimately, though, those in favour of a principled engagement were not convinced. In the end, they united with the group that was utterly opposed to the merger when they realised that the state was not prepared to make the concessions they demanded. This happened after the pro-merger camp agreed to a merger that left parliamentary and presidential powers in the review process intact. The principled engagement group felt that this would leave the process and specifically the CKRC vulnerable to parliamentary and presidential manipulation. The NCEC, the most visible actor in this group, wanted the process immunised against such manipulation, but its efforts were frustrated by the clergy’s and opposition politicians’ willingness to endorse compromises that did not change the fundamental character of the review. Activists allied to the NCEC and some members of the Ufungamano Negotiating Team interpreted this as an act of betrayal.

Those opposed to the merger

Opposition to the merger was led by Muungano wa Mageuzi. However, this group did not offer any concrete alternatives for a way forward, other than insisting on a continuation of the Ufungamano Initiative. Its opposition was based on the fear that the merger would end up as co-option and in the process subordinate the popular will of Kenyans to the very forces that opposed change. Moreover, they believed that it would cause the movement to lose its most potent weapon – popular support. As such, they argued, it would decimate the influence of not only the Ufungamano Initiative but the masses.
For this group, the decision to merge was unprincipled, and they detested Mutava Musyimi for his role in negotiating it. Indeed, some saw him as a traitor. In response, the pro-merger camp argued that those opposed to the merger were either hard-line ideologues or groups that relied on money from pro-Ufungamano donors for their survival. This led one PCK and merged CKRC commissioner to accuse the NCEC of following
an agenda which none of the other actors understood … [T]hey were so vehemently opposed to the merger that Kibwana walked out after losing the merger vote … I think the main activists in the NCEC and Muungano wa Mageuzi had been rendered irrelevant and they saw in the merger something that would result in cutting off their donor funds and thus the resistance.36
Yet again, this points to political economy considerations dictating the positions taken regarding the merger proposal. Constitution-making contentions had their own economic and political dynamics even within civil society, which made some actors guard their turf fiercely. Instead of celebrating progress towards a common process as one of the movement’s greatest achievements, some groups read it as a threat to their very survival, given that it was their confrontational modus operandi that gave them an economic lifeline. As Otieno Ombok remarked:
For a long time, the NCEC was the power base of these struggles [but they] were now losing ground … I do not think they liked that … Kibwana mobilised youth organisations to oppose the merger. For him, if Ghai took over the process, what would be the role of the NCEC thereafter? Indeed, you realise that since that time, the NCEC has remained a skeleton.37
The broad spectrum of responses to the merger proposal suggests the presence of competing interests within the reform movement. Whatever the source of these divisions, they signalled the earliest serious and visible cracks within the Ufungamano Initiative, which later brewed into a potent storm that ultimately shattered the movement. These cleavages also reflect “conflicting visions” both within the Ufungamano Initiative and in the wider constitutional reform struggle (Mutua 2008). They were more pronounced because the Ufungamano Initiative “brought together people of extreme characteristics – university students who were demanding a return to mass action and the conservative but pragmatic religious leaders who were for dialogue and consultation.”38 Consequently, it was impossible for the constituent parts of the movement to agree on every issue. Specifically, generating consensus and laying down the principles upon which a merger would occur was never going to be easy. The disagreements between different stakeholders in the Ufungamano Initiative also suggest that social movements – unless identity based and campaigning on a single issue – will seldom be coherent entities. Their very diversity renders them susceptible to internal conflict, jeopardising the possibilities for strategic interaction between themselves and the state.
Multiple challenges emerged from the Ufungamano Initiative’s internal cleavages on the merger question. As we saw in Chapter 4, there were several nodes of authority within the movement. In such a situation, leadership is difficult to define as it is dispersed. This dispersal enabled the movement to acquire multiple resources, including supporters, which was crucial for mobilisation. But the multiplicity of stakeholders and leadership nodes also posed challenges when it came to strategic interactions between the movement and the state, especially during the merger negotiations.
The first such challenge was structural. In marked contrast to the government side, which had a
pretty clear line of command in terms of who made decisions (it was Moi, it was Raila, the two key persons besides the PSC, who theoretically were negotiating on the government side), there was much less clarity on the Ufungamano Initiative side.39
In theory, the Stakeholders’ Forum was the supreme decision-making body in the Ufungamano Initiative. However, its ideological, theological, intellectual, and generational diversity, coupled with the bifurcated nature of authority within the Ufungamano Initiative, meant that it was impractical to approach an entire Forum with representatives from more than 50 organisations with a merger proposal. In an effort to circumvent this problem, Ghai reached out first to the clerics led by Mutava Musyimi (the convenor of the Stakeholder Council), whom he perceived as a key player in the movement and amenable to negotiation. This suggests that multiple nodes of authority – which are meant to check and reinforce one another in a movement of movements, such as the Ufungamano Initiative – do not necessarily aid democratic deliberation and decision-making. Indeed, sometimes, this configuration may be exploited both internally and externally by personalising authority for co-optive or cooperative purposes. In such cases, what is usually viewed as a strength is transformed into a weakness.
The Ufungamano Initiative addressed its lack of a coherent, unitary decision-making organ by establishing a new body – the Ufungamano Negotiating Team – to negotiate the merger. This included representatives from the movement’s three core constituencies: secular civil society, religious organisations, and political parties. Additionally, the PCK was co-opted as a constituency in its own right. The Negotiating Team was meant to give both legal and political direction to the movement during the negotiations, which would be based on the proposals that Ghai had drafted on December 25, 2000. However, progress was painfully slow as Ghai had to shuttle between Ufungamano House and parliament because KANU and the NDP refused to meet the Ufungamano Negotiating Team face-to-face.
As minutes of the Negotiating Team’s numerous meetings confirm, their discussions were highly structured, dealing with each of Ghai’s proposals in turn. By January 25, 2001, there was substantive agreement on the nature and scope of the review process as well as the composition of the new CKRC. Specifically, the Negotiating Team articulated:
Nature and scope of the review to be comprehensive; Constituency forums to replace the district forum as one of the Organs of Review; the Referendum be an Organ of the Review; there be two additional Vice-Chairs of the enlarged Commission with Ufungamano providing one, and the second be a woman; Decisions of the Commission be by consensus, failing which they shall be by 65% vote; Decisions of the National Constitutional Conference be by consensus, failing which they shall be by 65% vote; Ratification by Referendum shall be by a majority vote, with a minimum of 25% vote in at least 5 [of the 8 Kenyan] provinces; the political environment supportive of the review be guaranteed through strict observance of the Public Order Act and the Code of Conduct in the Presidential and National Assembly Elections Act; There be absolute freedom for civic education activities requiring no licensing; That the Kenya Constitution Review Act be amended to reflect the proposed agreement; and the amended Kenya Constitution Review Act be entrenched in the constitution.40
At this point, it seemed like backing off was not an option. Hence, Ghai, believing that there was finally a sufficient groundswell in favour of a common process, took his oath of office on January 26, 2001.41 However, his swearing in became a source of dispute between the pro-merger camp and those at the other end of the spectrum who insisted that the government side had conceded nothing during a month of negotiations. Moreover, several members of the Negotiating Team complained of being kept in the dark and suggested that some of their supposed colleagues may have struck a secret deal with the PSC.
Days after Ghai took his oath of office, the storm that had been brewing within the movement suddenly escalated. Battles raged over the mandate and composition of the proposed National Constitutional Conference; the desirability of giving the CKRC the power to request minimal constitutional amendments; the future role, if any, of the PSC; and the number of commissioners.42 In addition, there was the unresolved question of whether the government side should have the power to veto Ufungamano nominees for the merged CKRC. Fearing another stalemate, Ghai implored the PSC to accept the right of parties to nominate their own commissioners without interference. As a confidence-building measure, he even offered to serve as deputy to Oki Ombaka (chair of the PCK) in a merged CKRC.43
By January 31, 2001, the “narcissism of minor differences”44 was overshadowing the broad agreements that had been reached as suspicion and conflicting agendas deepened the cleavages in the Ufungamano Initiative. On January 31, 2001, some members of the Negotiating Team made it clear that neither the convenor nor the chair of the PCK had a mandate to negotiate on behalf of the movement, and that any informal contacts with them or any other Negotiating Team member would not be binding.45
These new developments pointed to the extreme difficulty – if not impossibility – of keeping everyone in the movement well informed and happy. Indeed, at their January 31 meeting, members of the Negotiating Team criticised the inadequate flow of information from the Secretariat and complained that they had not been kept fully informed. Clearly, this was a paradox. How could members of the Ufungamano Negotiating Team be kept in the dark yet mandated to negotiate? Fundamental differences were emerging within the movement as non-mandated forces took the lead in the negotiations. As we shall see in Chapter 6, these differences ultimately led to the disintegration of the Ufungamano Initiative when secular civil society groups then the Muslims walked out in protest against the dominance of the Christian faction. The final straw came when the Christians welcomed evangelicals back into their fold in order to oppose the inclusion of Islamic Kadhi courts in the draft constitution of 2005.
These developments notwithstanding, it is important to note that the ultimate decision-making body of the Ufungamano Initiative, the Stakeholders’ Forum, was yet to play a role in the merger negotiations. Instead, the discourse around a possible merger and the ongoing negotiations remained the preserve of a few elite leaders who held a variety of contrasting views. Musyimi insisted that the Forum could not participate in the discussions because it met only once every three months. He also suggested that the initial negotiations, at least, were an executive responsibility, so it was proper that they were handled by the Steering Council, which had duly nominated the Negotiating Team to attend the meetings on behalf of the whole Ufungamano Initiative.46 There were, however, dissenting voices, especially from the NCEC. For example, during one Negotiating Team meeting, Kivutha Kibwana (co-convenor of the NCEC) expressed vehement opposition to a decision to establish a drafting team of four (two from the Ufungamano Initiative and two from the PSC) to harmonise the 1998 and 2000 Review Acts. Kibwana argued that this should wait until the movement itself had reached agreement on every issue and the Stakeholders’ Forum had ratified the same.47
On March 21, 2001, the Stakeholders’ Forum convened to discuss the merger proposal and decide whether to endorse or reject it. Prior to the meeting, there had been serious efforts to justify the merger by outlining its merits to the members of the Forum. The day’s debate was both impassioned and acrimonious, primarily because the NCEC-allied secular civil society groups were ardently opposed to the proposal. In addition, many conspiracy theories were raised against both the government and some members of the Ufungamano Initiative.48 Nonetheless, the religious leaders and the PCK commissioners argued against the folly of persistent obstinacy, indicating that it was likely to plunge the country into turmoil. They insisted that agreeing to the merger was a hard yet necessary choice to save the country from chaos and that continued opposition to the state was highly unlikely to secure ultimate victory. These internal contentions within the Ufungamano Initiative came to signify the dualism of a movement that was both pro-reform and anti-reform. This, in turn, undermined the movement as it was unable to identify allies and adversaries, leading to the disintegration that began soon after the merger of the two processes.49
During the March 21 debate, Mwai Kibaki – the leader of the official opposition in parliament – was vilified by some of the participants for a remark he had made during the clamour for political pluralism in the early 1990s while still a cabinet minister in KANU’s one-party regime. At the time, he had likened those thinking of dislodging KANU as daydreamers who were trying to fell a mugumo (fig) tree with a razor blade. His critics wondered whether Kibaki, who led a group of older conservative politicians in supporting the merger, was now selling out to KANU because he believed it was still unbeatable. An outraged Kibaki responded: “There is no one in Kenya who wants the review to be scuttled or delayed … we cannot go back to a parallel process and those who think so are daydreaming” (Daily Nation 2001).
Despite this fierce opposition to the merger, a vote was called. The NCEC announced that it would boycott the ballot, and violence erupted as University of Nairobi students who supported the NCEC’s position accosted opposition politicians and religious leaders. The students were then counter-attacked by youths from Chemi Chemi ya Ukweli. Ultimately, this allowed the pro-merger camp to marshal sufficient numbers to ratify the merger proposal. Musyimi recalled:
Everybody was relieved, happy and full of admiration for how much we had been able to gain in a peaceful and stable way. That had never happened before. NGOs, the religious sector, and politicians had not come together before for a cause. But leading it was not a piece of cake. It had enormous challenges.50
The pro-merger camp’s victory in the vote marked the triumph of moderate and conservative forces over radical groups in the Ufungamano Initiative, and they remained hegemonic in the next phase of the struggle. Moreover, it illuminates congruence of means and ends as a determining factor in the ensuing cooperation between the movement’s pro-merger elements and their KANU and NDP counterparts who controlled the parliamentary review process. For once, the dominant social forces that had fuelled the contention seemed to share a common position on how to draft and enact a new constitution. However, as we will see in succeeding chapters, a paradox remained: this convergence constrained the radical transformation that many members of the Ufungamano Initiative had supposedly sought since launching the movement.
Indeed, the merger process and its outcome suggest that strategic interactions between a social movement and the state are not always transformative. Sometimes, as this merger illustrates, they result in cooperation and co-optation that alienate those pressing for more radical positions. More fundamentally, the merger vote compromised the Ufungamano Initiative’s ability to influence the merger as the “terrain of contest over the nature of the Review Process shifted to Parliament … Religious organizations and civil society had extracted concessions from the state, only to cede them to the opposition parties and Parliament” (Mutua 2008: 124). The NCEC’s Kamau Kuria recalled the disappointment of the anti-merger camp on the day of the vote as well as his subsequent frustration as the process unfolded:
I remember March 21, 2001 because, for me, it brought some major significance. I was at Ufungamano and I saw the clergy selling out. I have never been to church since then because I found that there was no integrity. The clergy [did not need] to be perfect, but I expected sincerity at least. What I saw was dishonesty of the worst kind.51
Many secular civil society groups, especially the NCEC, Muugano wa Mageuzi, and youth and student organisations, felt a few conservative and reactionary religious leaders had hoodwinked them into joining the Ufungamano Initiative with assurances that it would be progressive. At the time, such groups equated progressiveness with opposition to Moi:
We would have joined anything that was anti-KANU and Moi. Little did we know that Musyimi would deliver us hook, line, and sinker to the “enemy.” Musyimi was in cahoots with Kibaki. Both sold the Ufungamano Initiative, which had a promising future in delivering the people of Kenya from the bondage of the political class. The merger turned out to be a disastrous venture, and Kenya never realised a new constitution.52
This view that those who voted for the merger were sell-outs was almost universally shared throughout the anti-merger camp. For instance, one former CEO of the 4Cs stated:
To this day, there are those of us who believe that those who joined the CKRC betrayed the initial support and principles on which this reform struggle had been based. And, in a way, that began the journey of the dwindling of the impact … of the initiative itself to a point it became a pale shadow of its former self.53
In response, Peter Ocholla, an employee of the Kenya Women’s Political Caucus (whose leader Phoebe Asiyo was accused of betraying the Ufungamano Initiative by accepting the offer of a seat on the PSC), argued that compromises on both sides were both inevitable and necessary to move the process forward.54 For Ocholla, the merger offered a win for both processes. Similarly, PCK and CKRC commissioner Abubakar Zein insisted, “It was not the intention of the Ufungamano Initiative to rip the country apart, or to have two constitutional processes. We always saw value in a process that would unify the country” rather than plunge it into chaos.55 It is impossible to say for sure that the merger averted the doomsday scenario of domestic turmoil. That said, the KANU–NDP alliance had shown a willingness to play a destructive zero-sum game, so descent into anarchy was certainly a possibility if the Ufungamano Initiative continued down the path of resolute opposition.
By and large, the pro-merger camp was bereft of demagogic ideologues, whereas the youth and student organisations, Muungano wa Mageuzi, and the NCEC
believed in some ideology and wanted everything premised on ideological or some political theory framework, and if it did not fit within the theory, they argued, you cannot use it. Religious leaders were pragmatists who said, “Let’s take the chance and merge.”56
Moreover, there was the potential for further antagonism within the pro-merger camp itself because some PCK commissioners would miss out on a seat in the new, merged CKRC. In a bid to nip this problem in the bud, the Steering Council, which had been responsible for appointing the PCK commissioners, outlined a number of “objective criteria” that would determine the movement’s nominees for the CKRC. These included: active participation in the PCK; fair representation of different stakeholders; provincial/regional diversity; and a clear articulation of the vision and values that the PCK would carry into the common process. Nevertheless, the issue developed into a major fault line within the Ufungamano Initiative as complaints about the omission of certain PCK commissioners – and indeed the “objective criteria” – grew. For instance, on April 4, 2001, the Reverend S.M. Muchuga wrote a letter to the Steering Council in which he expressed serious reservations about the decision to limit nominations to the merged CKRC to former PCK commissioners alone. Instead, he advocated looking beyond the PCK and nominating better-qualified members of the Ufungamano Initiative.
After further negotiations between the political parties, on May 8, 2001 parliament passed an amendment to the Constitution of Kenya Review Commission Act that formally sealed the merger.57 Just over a month later, on June 11, ten PCK commissioners took up their seats on the new CKRC.58 Two more commissioners nominated by the PSC were appointed on the same day.59 These new members joined the 15 commissioners60 from the government side who had been nominated on November 10, 2000. After they were formally gazetted, the chair of the Ufungamano Initiative’s PCK, Dr Oki Ombaka, and his deputy, Abida Ali-Aroni, were appointed to the positions of first deputy chair and second deputy chair of the new CKRC, respectively.
Some commentators have argued that the merged CKRC was a “hodgepodge; diverse in terms of gender, religion, region, race, ethnicity and profession … [but it is] difficult to justify why more accomplished individuals were left out” (Mutua 2008: 129). This analysis accords with Bates’s (1999: 91) observation that, during post-colonial state reform contentions, “political elites in many African countries have managed to silence the technocratic managerial bourgeoisie and divide loyalties that are subject to political manipulation [and] factionalised the ranks of the political opposition.” The new commission was a political, not a technical, body that reflected the political power games of the time. Cottrell and Ghai (2007: 19) note that many of the appointees were clients of powerful politicians who were expected to do the bidding of their patrons (see also Lumumba 2008). Moreover, “its large size betrayed the political calculations of its authors. Rather than create a nonpartisan technical body, politicians crafted a loose receptacle that would advocate their interests from within” (Mutua 2008: 129).

Post-merger developments

It is instructive to note that the Ufungamano Initiative did not disband after ten of its PCK commissioners were absorbed into the CKRC. Indeed, for a while, the movement’s dominant coalition of religious leaders and opposition politicians continued to play a critical role in the review process. In addition, besides “forcing” the state to acquiesce to demands for popular participation, the movement’s earlier work was fed into the new commission. Specifically, the views that the PCK had collected from ordinary Kenyans were handed to the CKRC, with copies archived by the NCCK. Moreover, the merged process adopted the Ufungamano Initiative’s organisational configuration, including specialist committees (steering, resource development and budget, mobilisation and outreach, civic education, and publicity and communication)61 and a Plenary, which, once again, was the central deliberation and decision-making body. However, in contrast to the Ufungamano Initiative’s Stakeholders’ Forum, this Plenary was chaired by the chair of the commission and included only commissioners. The new commission’s leadership style was collegiate, just as the PCK’s had been. This way of working emerged as a product of “the mistrust that characterised the struggle for constitution making, which dictated that nothing [was] left to chance” in implementing the 2001 Review Act (Lumumba 2008: 37).
A more fundamental outcome of the merger was that the process ceased to be explicitly exclusionary. More actors would be represented in and contribute to the various structures and organs that were established around the country. These included the CKRC, constituency constitutional forums, the National Constitutional Conference, the national referendum (which would be held much later), and the National Assembly (Lumumba 2008: 47). Arguably, therefore, the Ufungamano Initiative managed to embed certain key values into the constitutional reform process, such as the inclusion of all of Kenya’s citizenry regardless of socioeconomic status, race, ethnicity, gender, religious faith, age, occupation, education, or disability as well as the need to maintain accountability to the Kenyan people.
However, notwithstanding its obvious influence over the modus operandi of the CKRC, post-merger, the Ufungamano Initiative suffered the same fate as every other Kenyan reform movement in that it withered and died before achieving its ultimate goal. Previous movements had been hijacked for political ends, failed to develop structures to mobilise popular participation, and/or suffered from a lack of funding. All of these factors contributed to the Ufungamano Initiative’s decline, but it also had to contend with internal fractures along ethnic, religious, political, ideological, and economic lines. Most importantly, after the merger vote, groups allied to the NCEC felt betrayed by the turn of events and withdrew their commitment to the movement. Meanwhile, a majority of those who remained relaxed in the belief that they had achieved their principal goal (popular participation) through the merger. Consequently, the Ufungamano Initiative became a shadow of its former self. As a result, some of the key actors when the movement had been at its height (especially those who had opposed the merger) were granted only observer status, with no voting rights, when the National Constitutional Conference convened in 2002. This suggests that the merger outcome was a zero-sum game.
Nevertheless, there was still time for the Ufungamano Initiative to have a profound impact on the Kenyan political landscape. A year and a half after the merger, the movement’s triumphant conservative/reactionary wing buried the hatchet with the NCEC and its allies and a group led by Raila Odinga (who had fallen out with Moi) to form a new political coalition, the National Alliance Rainbow Coalition (NARC). In the December 2002 general election, NARC defeated KANU, ending its 37-year reign. The net result of this victory was a mirage of change – a transition without a transformation – that saw the NARC regime sacrifice popular aspirations for a new constitution as they concentrated on consolidating their power. Chapter 6 explains these developments in greater detail.

Conclusion

Dynamics of collective action can result in a combination of contagion, exhaustion, and even accommodation between movements and their targets. As this chapter has shown, the Ufungamano Initiative was a mess of contradictions, competing interests, and multiple vulnerabilities. The movement’s dominant faction responded positively to Ghai’s overtures because they viewed a merger as an opportunity to remain relevant, whereas the anti-merger wing interpreted it as an existential threat. Ultimately, Ghai succeeded because of a confluence of factors that included fatigue, resource constraints, the hegemonic capture of the movement by a conservative leadership, and his own ingenuity, perseverance, and persuasiveness. His influence on the direction of the reform process was so profound that one respondent argued that forging a consensus between the two processes would have been highly unlikely – if not impossible – had he not been appointed to chair the CKRC.62
Ghai approached the Ufungamano Initiative with his merger proposal because he respected its strength, regardless of a number of flaws and shortcomings. The movement therefore demonstrates that civil society can provide alternatives to state processes and structures, and proves that it is possible for a large group of citizens, diverse as they may be, to coalesce around a shared ideal and force change. Challenging the state and holding the government to account on the basis of the 1998 Constitution of Kenya Review Act were great achievements in this regard. The Ufungamano Initiative forced the KANU–NDP regime to search for ways to save face. This explains why the government attempted to restore its credibility by recruiting the credible and trustworthy Ghai to lead the CKRC. Thereafter, it was his idea to pursue a merger between the parallel review processes that was acceptable to both sides.
The leaders of the Ufungamano Initiative, who were mainly drawn from religious institutions, opposition politicians, and the PCK, became Ghai’s allies in pushing for a merger. The ensuing debate was acrimonious but they ultimately emerged victorious. Consensus building and merger negotiations were arduous both within the Ufungamano Initiative and in the state camp. Even as Ghai assured the Ufungamano Initiative that the government was serious about the merger, he had to ignore loud criticism from senior cabinet ministers who were convinced that the movement was a group of malcontents with no business talking to the state.63 But challenges to the merger came not just from those who opposed it or from the government’s refusal to deal directly with the Ufungamano Initiative. The opposition and civil society were beset with their own pathologies, which frustrated Ghai’s efforts to forge a merger (Mutua 2008). However, Ghai was able to navigate through these problems and eventually united the two processes.
