6    A dream deferred

Introduction

What happens to movements after they are absorbed into mainstream political processes? What happens to their imagined utopias? This chapter attempts to answer these questions in relation to the Ufungamano Initiative and especially how the merger affected the movement’s activities and the outcomes of the constitutional review process. It explains that, although the Ufungamano Initiative forced the state to acquiesce on the issue of popular participation, this victory spelt doom for both the movement itself and the radical state transformation project it had championed. The chapter argues that the merger fundamentally altered the Ufungamano Initiative’s fortunes and, by extension, the trajectory of the constitutional reform project for three principal reasons.
First, given its contentious nature, the merger widened existing fractures among the movement’s activists and ultimately precipitated its disintegration. This was because the merger had all the hallmarks of an elite bargain that entrenched the hegemony of Kenya’s elites and the marginalisation of subaltern voices. As a result, it did not resolve what Gramsci would have termed the “organic crisis” that was afflicting Kenya at the time. Indeed, the only cure for that crisis was a new constitution. The crisis had emanated from the state elite’s inability to accommodate or mediate between “competing interests of individuals, social groups, regions, ethnic groups and institutions” (Mutua 2008: 117). In an effort to address it, groups of elite contenders resorted to popular mobilisations that piled pressure on the dominant elite coalition and forced it to accommodate both themselves and popular participation in the constitutional review process through the merger of the two parallel processes. This meant that the merger marked the moment when different elite formations closed ranks, and its paradoxical outcome – like that of the 1997 mass protests and the resulting IPPG deal – was merely to tilt the balance in favour of one elite group at the expense of another. This was because none of the major players had a genuine interest in the sort of radical transformation that might threaten their own privileged social position. Consequently, far from delivering substantive change, the merger actually undermined transformation and reinforced elite domination of the subaltern classes, which in turn reinvigorated the crisis and generated a new wave of contention.
Second, the merger created a mirage of democratic participation, especially for those who believed it was the best step towards the realisation of a new constitution. Subsequently, many movement cadres dropped their guard even as the leadership was making deals or getting co-opted. This happened because the leaders of the Ufungamano Initiative had succeeded in organising subaltern support, and therefore effectively killed dissent. The net effect of this was the weakening of the movement’s ability to challenge the dominance of the political elite or offer a viable alternative when the proposed constitution generated new acrimony.
Third, the movement was further weakened by changes in the political environment, especially in the context of a new regime after the 2002 general election. That election saw a new coalition elected on a reformist platform. However, as will be shown later in this chapter, disagreements over how to share out the political spoils between the two main camps in the coalition ensured that the new power elite failed to agree on the content of a new constitution. Related to this were changes in the resource mobilisation environment. After the merger, donors who had previously supported the Ufungamano Initiative were no longer interested in supporting a process that was not part of the mainstream.
Ultimately, these factors conspired to constrain the realisation of a new constitution as the draft that was presented by one faction of the coalition was rejected in the 2005 referendum. Thereafter, popular frustration, coupled with intensified hostility between the different elite formations, degenerated into volatile uncivil energy characterised by ethnic and politically charged violence in the aftermath of a dispute over the outcome of the presidential vote in the 2007 general election. Ultimately, though, an intra-elite truce brokered by the Panel of Eminent Africans led by Kofi Annan managed to resolve the post-election violence and created the necessary conditions to deliver a new constitution in August 2010.
The next section analyses the reasons for the decline of the Ufungamano Initiative after the merger. Following this, the chapter explores how the merger and the movement’s consequent decline affected the progress and outcomes of the constitutional reform process. Finally, it explains why and how a new wave of contention incubated in the ashes of the 2005 referendum led to the post-election violence of 2007/2008.

The Ufungamano Initiative’s decline after the merger

Social movement scholarship advances multiple theories for why movements decline. Miller (1999), for instance, attributes social movements’ decline to success, organisational failure, co-optation, repression, or absorption into mainstream society. For Blumer (1951) and Della Porta and Diani (2006), decline is simply a stage in a social movement’s life cycle, following coalescence and bureaucratisation. For Tarrow (1996: 47), changes in the nature of political conflict and alliances can “trigger, channel and demobilize social movements.” Along the same lines, Oliver and Myers (2003) attribute a movement’s decline to changes in the environment, as this determines opportunities and constraints. Given this array of possibilities, what should we make of the Ufungamano Initiative’s decline, especially in the aftermath of the merger? The following narrative suggests that its decline resulted from an interplay of success, co-optation, its absorption into the mainstream, and the changing political environment.

Decline as a result of success

The decline of the Ufungamano Initiative following the merger was first and foremost a product of its success. In conceptualising success, Meyer and Staggenborg (1996: 1634) argue that movements can affect the political opportunity structure by “influencing policy, altering political alignments, and raising the public profile and salience of particular issues.” The question then becomes: what did success look like for the Ufungamano Initiative? More specifically, how did the merger affect the political opportunity structure and then lead to the decline of the Ufungamano Initiative?
As was noted in Chapter 3, the Ufungamano Initiative crystallised as a protest against Moi/KANU’s and Odinga/NDP’s intransigence and refusal to accept popular participation in the constitutional review process. As such, even as the movement’s leaders mobilised Kenyans to draft their own constitution, the dominant group of religious and opposition political leaders within it always favoured an inclusive, common process. Therefore, for this group, a merger between the Ufungamano Initiative and the state-led process with ensuing popular participation represented an unqualified success for the movement in “achieving what it had been created to do.”1 For them, “when PCK commissioners joined the government-led CKRC, that was the end of Ufungamano Initiative. Even though a few people remained calling themselves the Ufungamano Initiative, its mainstream team had joined the government.”2 Thereafter, opposition withered. In any event, some of the activists in the movement argued, the intention had never been to create a permanent organisation. Hence, many of them had no qualms about transitioning into the formal, state-run process:
When the process of the delivery of a new constitution came into place after the merger, many people who were in the Ufungamano Initiative subsequently became delegates at the Bomas conference [i.e. the National Constitutional Conference] … [T]hey became actors in the midwifery of the constitution … [O]thers felt they were already represented and stepped down their demands and agitation … [A]fter all, your brother or sister is there, making that constitution you have been asking for.3
This implies that the merger created mirages of democratic participation that led activists to drop their guards. But why did this happen? Tarrow (1996: 42) argues that such behaviour is occasioned by “the signals that groups receive from their immediate policy environment or from changes in their resources or capabilities.” One of these signals was the insistence that the new CKRC was sufficiently representative to oversee the review process. Additionally, a new regime was elected at the end of 2002 on the back of a promise to deliver a new constitution. Collectively, these changes in the policy environment convinced most people that a new constitution was all but guaranteed. As we will see later in this chapter, this not the case.

Decline as a result of co-optation

Miller (1999) observes that when the centralised leadership of a movement is co-opted, the movement declines. For the Ufungamano Initiative, although its original leadership was widely spread among its component groups, by the time of the merger negotiations, it had centralised around those who provided the greatest resources. Specifically, as was noted in Chapter 5, the NCCK’s secretary general, Mutava Musyimi, had become the movement’s “first among equals.” It was he, together with other influential religious leaders and the chair of the PCK – Oki Ombaka – who steered the movement towards a merger that critics labelled co-optive. Given that these leaders had been the most effective mobilisers of resources for the movement, those who opposed the merger were left with insufficient resources to mount any serious opposition, especially as many donors now “believed that the matter had moved to a different level, and there would be no point supporting groups that remained inherently opposed to the merger.”4 The movement naturally declined as its resources continued to dwindle. As resource mobilisation theory reminds us, resources are crucial not only for a movement’s emergence but for its sustenance (see McCarthy and Zald 1973; 1977; Tilly 1977; McAdam 1982; McAdam, McCarthy, and Zald 1996).

Decline as a result of internal strain

Given its acrimonious nature, the merger widened existing fractures within the Ufungamano Initiative. As Miller (1999) reminds us, internal strains and factionalism can cause movements to decline. Within the Ufungamano Initiative, those who lost the merger vote felt that the pro-merger camp had sold out or been co-opted because, for them, the merger was not based on principles.5 This charge of co-optation led to greater insularity within both the pro- and anti-merger camps, and ultimately fractured the movement’s unity and shared aims.
Specifically, the merger brought to the surface deep-rooted cleavages that precipitated the movement’s disintegration. This was because, in the face of a common enemy, the constitutional reform movement had brought together several disparate critics and advocates with conflicting visions.6 This constellation of what is loosely termed the “pro-reform movement” meant that the Ufungamano Initiative represented numerous diverse constituencies and competing interests. Moreover, it operated only at the level of improvised unity. Hence, any divisive issue – such as the merger – was almost certain to exacerbate deeply embedded fragmentations. The fragmentations ultimately led to the defeat of the draft constitution proposed by one faction of the NARC government in the 2005 referendum.

Merger, the changing political opportunity structure, and outcomes of the review process

The constitutional review contention went through several distinct phases. Each of these was defined by different shades of opinion on the part of the contending actors. Additionally, there were multiple changes in the social positions of the actors that reflected shifting dynamics in the political environment and realignments of ethnic, religious, class, and power interests. The key players would veer so frequently from outright opposition to enthusiastic support for a particular proposal (or vice versa) that it is almost impossible to identify the true reformers. While many actors in this process argued that their seesawing positions were based on changing facts, in reality their behaviour was usually dictated by the positions they occupied in the political power hierarchy at any given time. The greatest casualty of this perpetual repositioning was the reform agenda itself, which generated a sense of betrayal among the masses as their marginalisation increased.
To a large extent, the merger was the moment when the political elite’s interests converged, further embedding their hegemony throughout society and especially within the reform process. However, it also precipitated developments that profoundly shaped the political landscape. Specifically, after the merger, the short-lived truce among the various elite formations enabled the CKRC to embark on a massive civic education project and then collect opinions from the Kenyan people on the type of constitution they desired. The commissioners received invaluable assistance in carrying out these tasks from a number of secular and religious civil society organisations.
They were under intense pressure to complete their mission prior to the next general election, which was scheduled for late 2002. The expectation was that the election would be a highly significant political moment, because Moi was constitutionally barred from seeking another term as president. Therefore, on November 6, 2001, Ghai implored the CKRC to draft a new constitution as soon as possible, given that the election itself – and its outcome – had the potential to imperil the “constitutional moment” (Lumumba 2008: 57). It is easy to see why he was so eager to conclude the process. On the political front, KANU was imploding. Wary of the possible outcome of the review process, Moi was focusing most of his attention on protecting himself against future prosection for human rights violations and corruption. He no longer trusted his established lieutenants, so he overlooked them and instead anointed a political novice, Uhuru Kenyatta, as his chosen successor.
However, in the event, it proved impossible to enact a new constitution before the end of Moi’s term, primarily because the outgoing president deployed his power under the existing constitution to ensure that the election was held first. Specifically, on Friday, October 25, 2002, just as the review process was about to enter the National Constitutional Conference (NCC) stage, Moi dissolved parliament, throwing the entire process into jeopardy (Lumumba 2008; Cottrell and Ghai 2007). Reflecting on the impact of this action, Cottrell and Ghai (2007: 18–19) note:
Dissolution had the automatic effect that the Process could not continue: every MP was a member of the NCC, and MPs ceased to be such when the writs were issued for the elections … a few days after the dissolution. The dissolution could not have come at a more dramatic moment: the formal inauguration of the NCC was to be on Monday 28 October 2002. During the previous week all the non-parliamentarian delegates had assembled for a week of pre-conference activities … where discussions of the CKRC Draft were led by distinguished academics and others, including those critical of the Draft. The President dissolved Parliament at around 3 p.m. on the last day of this inaugural week, Friday 25 October.
By dissolving parliament, “Moi evidently hoped that his party, KANU, would win the elections, and that his chosen successor would slip into his seat” (Cottrell and Ghai 2007: 12).
However, he miscalculated the resolve of others both within and outside KANU to succeed him. Raila Odinga had steered his National Development Party into a full merger with KANU on March 18, 2002. However, just a few months later, sensing Moi’s commitment to Kenyatta, he had led an exodus of disgruntled senior cabinet ministers (most notably George Saitoti, Kalonzo Musyoka, and Joseph Kamotho) out of the party. This group formed the Liberal Democratic Party (LDP), which soon joined hands with the National Alliance Party of Kenya (NAK) – a burgeoning opposition organisation that was supported by many of the people and groups behind the Ufungamano Initiative. By then, NAK had already settled on Mwai Kibaki (leader of the official opposition in parliament) as its presidential candidate to compete against Kenyatta.
The LDP brigade, aware that there was insufficient time to launch an independent campaign, boosted Kibaki’s chances as the joint opposition candidate with Odinga’s rather lukewarm endorsement “Kibaki tosha” (which loosely translates as “Kibaki is competent for the position”) during a rally at Uhuru Park on October 14, 2002. This declaration was preceded by a secret Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) between Kibaki’s NAK and Odinga’s LDP.7 The union of their two organisations created the National Alliance Rainbow Coalition (NARC).
In combination, these developments provided the impetus that the opposition needed to dislodge KANU from power. Of the 14 groups that coalesced under the NARC banner, all but one had been affiliated to the Ufungamano Initiative. The sole exception was the LDP. The alliance campaigned on a platform of delivering a new constitution within 100 days.

The 2002 election: transition without transformation

In conceptualising political opportunities, Meyer and Staggenborg (1996: 1634) distinguish between “relatively stable aspects of political opportunity, such as traditions and institutions, and more volatile elements of opportunity, such as public policy, political discourse, and elite alignment.” They further argue that “political opportunity is not just a fixed external environment that insurgents confront, but also something activists can alter” (see also Tarrow 1993; Gamson and Meyer 1996). Such altering of political opportunity can generate either negative or positive impacts on a movement (Tarrow 1996; Piven and Cloward 1979).
The outcome of the 2002 general election illustrates that the Ufungamano Initiative and the wider opposition managed to confront and even alter the political environment. On December 27, over 63 per cent of Kenyans put their faith in NARC’s presidential candidate Mwai Kibaki to deliver a new constitution and a better future. NARC also won 125 out of 210 seats in parliament, while KANU was reduced to just 64 seats. The remainder went to smaller parties. In this election, many of the activists who had propelled the Ufungamano Initiative’s agenda transitioned into elective politics through NARC. KANU’s defeat therefore generated great optimism that a momentous transformation was just around the corner. Indeed, many believed that NARC would deliver precisely because so many of its cadres “had actively and consistently fought for a new constitution” (Lumumba 2008: 86).
Paradoxically, though, KANU’s defeat imperilled the reform process and diminished the Ufungamano Initiative’s significance as most of its activists dropped their guards and relaxed in the erroneous belief that the new regime would radically transform the state. In addition, co-optation of the movement’s leaders intensified through offers of important positions within the new NARC government.8 However, it was the sudden absence of a common enemy (the vanquished Moi/KANU regime), coupled with changes in activists’ social positions, that had the greatest detrimental effect on the radical transformation agenda. Following the election, the promised 100 days passed with no attempt to reconvene the NCC. Instead, NARC – a “coalition of convenience, united more by what it opposed than by what it actually [stood] for” (Barkan 2004: 92) – started bickering over cabinet positions. This paralysed not just the government’s work but the review process. The main bone of contention was the infamous power-sharing deal between NAK and the LDP, which Kibaki and his allies refused to honour.9 As Ghai had warned, these developments caused delays that allowed the constitutional “moment to lapse” (Mutua 2008: 143).
With most of the old alliances shattered, four new alignments emerged in the aftermath of the 2002 election:
  1. Those who demanded radical reform of the governance architecture in line with the secret MoU, primarily in the hope that this would guarantee them a larger slice of the power pie. Among their number were sections of the LDP brigade.
  2. Those who had viewed their ascendancy to power as an end in itself and no longer saw the need for radical reform, including most of the NAK wing of NARC and Democratic Party-allied politicians who had been members of the managerialist camp in the state reform contentions of the early 1990s (see Chapter 2).10
  3. A somewhat naive group that mainly consisted of civil society and religious actors who had supported the merger and now gave the Kibaki government the benefit of the doubt in the hope that it would deliver a new constitution (Lumumba 2008).
  4. Finally, the radical champions of reform and Muslim groups who abandoned the Ufungamano Initiative after the movement’s mainstream Christian leaders joined forces with anti-reformist evangelical Christians to oppose the retention of Kadhi courts in the draft constitution. This reconfiguration of the Ufungamano Initiative was arguably the final nail in its coffin.
Another speed bump in the path of the review process was the composition of the National Constitutional Conference, which NAK saw as an unacceptable product of KANU’s flagrant gerrymandering. Commenting on this, one prominent reform activist stated:
One way of controlling the country by Moi was to draw up more constituencies in the areas where KANU had support and leave intact areas where he had little support. For instance, contrast the Embakasi and Ijara constituencies, with 120,000 and 7,000 voters, respectively. They both elect one MP. The vote of one person in Ijara is not equivalent to that of a person in Embakasi. Therefore, any law passed by parliament governing the rewriting of the constitution could not be democratic.11
Therefore, the delegates to the NCC did not proportionally represent the interests of all Kenyas. At one point, the NAK wing of NARC even suggested drafting an entirely new list of delegates in order to tilt the balance in its favour. This was because, in addition to the gerrymandering issue, they did not want to see ex-parliamentarians who had lost their seats in the recent election continue to exert an influence over the review process via their cronies in the NCC. All of this indicates that the new regime was now primarily concerned with consolidating its hold on power.
Inevitably, given these issues, the NCC (popularly known as the Bomas process) proved to be both lengthy and sporadic. It took place in three distinct phases. Bomas I was held between April 28 and June 6, 2003.12 Bomas II began on August 18 and concluded on September 26, but there were two adjournments in that time. The first of these occurred on August 25, following the death of Vice-President Kijana Wamalwa. The conference reconvened on September 6, following Wamalwa’s funeral. However, just eight days later, the murder of Dr Chrispin Odhiambo-Mbai, chair of the Committee on Devolution, halted proceedings once again.13 Bomas III was held between January 12 and March 23, 2004.14 By this final phase, the new administration had already lost most of its revolutionary fervour. Furthermore, divisions within the political elites manifested in several attempts to scuttle the process. For example, at the outset, Mutava Musyimi – seemingly acting on NAK’s instructions (Lumumba 2008) – tried to unseat the chair, Yash Ghai, and take his place. After he failed, Musyimi left the conference, never to return, a decision he attributes to a combination of feeling unwanted and his fellow delegates’ hostility:
I remember the first day I went to Bomas as a delegate. The reception was vicious. I was almost hit by someone. I realised that a number of other people who had been in Ufungamano were getting similar treatment. It was a place where some of us were just not wanted. Eventually, I resigned from Bomas because I had problems with its inability to accommodate all people and encourage fair debate. I got the impression that, by that time, there was a plan to delegitimise Ufungamano … I resigned because I thought, “This is not why we merged” … Even some of the commissioners from Ufungamano had now turned against us. Politics had come in.15
Having turned his back on the NCC, Musyimi mobilised what remained of the Ufungamano Initiative to produce a rival draft constitution that was dubbed the Ufungamano Initiative draft. However, this did not amount to much in terms of challenging the much more popular NCC (or Bomas) draft.
Meanwhile, the government persisted with its efforts to manipulate the reform process via some of the commissioners who had joined the CKRC from the PKC and others who now reflected the conservative tendencies of the NAK wing of the new regime. Existing cleavages widened as the commissioners increasingly fell under partisan influence (Lumumba 2008). One such cleavage was ethnicity, which re-emerged to frustrate genuine transformation. In addition, NGOs and the LDP both demanded that the new constitution must scrap the provincial administration (a key instrument of state oppression, inherited from the colonial administration), whereas NAK insisted that it should be retained. Finally, as mentioned earlier, there was a bitter dispute between Christians and Muslims over the Kadhi courts issue.16
These contentions prompted Timothy Njoya and others to challenge parliament’s right to enact a new constitution without a referendum. They took their case to the High Court, and Justices Aaron Ringera, Benjamin Kubo, and Mary Kasango issued their decision in favour of the plaintiffs on March 25, 2004. Many of those who had been actively involved in the NCC process immediately accused the government of working in cahoots with the Ufungamano Initiative to secure a referendum. Meanwhile, there was vociferous support for the ruling from those who believed that the power to draft and enact a new constitution rested exclusively with the Kenyan people (Kichana 2004).
It was not only challenges from the state and the Ufungamano Initiative that sent the NCC process spiralling into chaos. In addition, a number of prominent Kenyans at the heart of the constitutional reform project died while in office. The most notable of these were: the deputy chair of the CKRC, Oki Ooko Ombaka, on June 15, 2002; Vice-President Kijana Wamalwa on August 25, 2003; and, finally, the convenor of the contentious Technical Committee on Devolution, Dr Odhiambo-Mbai, on September 14, 2003. On the subject of Ombaka’s death, former CKRC secretary P.L.O. Lumumba (2008: 69) writes:
Oki’s death was a big blow to the Review Process. He had been a fearless crusader for a people-driven Constitution and a voice of reason and honesty within the Commission. With him and Yash we had constituted a formidable bulwark against the shenanigans of state-friendly commissioners whose subterranean machinations were designed to torpedo the process.
After Wamalwa died in a London hospital, President Kibaki declared two weeks of national mourning between August 25 and September 6 in reocgnition of his instrumental role in the reform movement. Since the early 1990s, Wamalwa had been one of a small group of opposition politicians at the heart of the push for political pluralism, democratisation, and constitutional reform. Ghai eulogised him as a person with “the ability to inspire and to reconcile … a peacemaker [who] built bridges across different sectors of our society and of politics.”17
Wamalwa’s tempering influence had been apparent during the official opening of the National Constitutional Conference on April 30, 2003, when he announced:
We in this nation are of many tribes and many religions and many cultures. In our efforts to write the supreme law of the land, we must go the extra mile to cater for every community, however small, for every religious sect, however small, for every Kenyan who has a view. For the constitution is the defender of the weak, the protector of the mighty and the not so mighty, and the modern defender of the faiths in plural.18
The absence of his sobering influence on the Kenyan political scene was soon felt in the constitutional review process as the country’s cabinet ministers started pulling in different directions. This resulted in major conflicts over the content of the proposed constitution, arguably motivated by the elite’s unwillingness to compromise.
Chrispin Odhiambo-Mbai was murdered just six days after the NCC had convened for the third time. As the elected convenor of the Technical Committee on Devolution, he had spearheaded an intellectual attack on both the CKRC’s devolution proposal and an alternative NAK proposal. The latter recommended retention of the controversial centralised administration that most Kenyans – including a majority of delegates in the National Constitutional Conference – felt should be scrapped. For his part, Mbai advocated a quasi-federal model that found favour with most of the delegates. “Owing to the significance that devolution had taken, Mbai’s death heightened tension and even raised ethnic sensibilities” (Lumumba 2008: 118).
By the end of the National Constitutional Conference, deep divisions were so patent that the regime’s NAK faction, supported by what remained of the Ufungamano Initiative (mainly clergy from the central region of the country), boycotted the final vote on March 15, 2004 that adopted the draft constitution after sensing that its alternative proposal was heading for defeat.19 Specifically, the NAK wing of the government advocated a pure presidential system with a non-executive prime minister appointed by the president, whereas the LDP favoured a parliamentary system. The main bone of contention for the remnants of the Ufungamano Initiative was the retention of the Kadhi courts in the draft. Thereafter, the NAK faction tasked the attorney general, Amos Wako, with an unprocedural amendment to the popular draft to reflect its position. This further polarised the country, with Kibaki’s NAK, who supported what came to be known as the Wako draft, ranged against Odinga’s LDP brigade, who had reunited with their former allies in KANU. The LDP/KANU axis led a successful campaign that culminated in the country’s rejection of the Wako draft in a national referendum, which was held on November 21, 2005.
The referendum campaigns exacerbated existing divisions between different ethnicities, the various factions of the political elite, and religious groups. In part, this was because they did not limit themselves to the content of the draft constitution. Rather, “frustration over aborted promises in the infamous MoU, coupled with massive victimisation of former KANU power barons,”20 land tenure, the Kadhi courts, and sexual and reproductive rights were all brought to the fore. In the end, then, what was supposed to be a referendum on the single issue of the draft constitution became a plebiscite on NAK rule as parochial party-political and ethnic interests once again took centre stage.21 The draft constitution was rejected by 57 per cent of the votes cast and, with that, NARC’s promise of delivering a new constitution went up in smoke.
By the end of 2005, the Ufungamano Initiative was thoroughly disunited and incapable of commanding a mass following. Its various constituent groups could not agree on the substance of the draft constitution, and the activist clergy had lost the moral legitimacy to present themselves as the voice of reason.22 Moreover, deep religious and ethnic cleavages were dividing the movement and the country. An increasing number of Kenyans, including many within the movement, vented both justified and unjustified anger over the distribution of political power and national resources along ethnic lines.23 Once again, the opportunistic political vultures preyed on Kenya and blocked the realisation of a new constitution. Meanwhile, many of the social organisations that had united under the Ufungamano Initiative banner, such as the NCCK, the Catholic Church, and countless NGOs, imploded, with different leaders adopting contradictory positions often on the basis of their ethnicity.
In the end, then, despite its immense promise and early successes, the Ufungamano Initiative failed to transform the Kenyan state and instead became yet another example of what Barack Muluka (2011) describes as a “false dawn that did not blossom into mornings of salvation and fulfilment.” The limitations of social movements are usually exposed when they are absorbed within rigid power structures, and the Ufungamano Initiative was no exception.
This raises two further questions. What happened to the process of drafting and enacting a new constitution? And what happened to the social forces that had been at the core of the push for that new constitution? These questions are addressed below.

The 2005 referendum and after: a dream deferred?

In one of his most famous poems, the African American poet Langston Hughes (1951) muses over a dream deferred. This is relevant with regards to whether the social forces behind the Ufungamano Initiative ought to have regrouped following the country’s rejection of the draft constitution in the 2005 referendum. From the preceding discussion, it is clear that Kenya’s ethno-political crocodiles both consumed the Ufungamano Initiative and blocked the realisation of a new constitution. This was especially due to widening cleavages in both the ruling NARC regime and the wider Kenyan public. But this did not spell the end of the dream of a new constitution. Instead, a new wave of contention emerged.
Three significant developments occurred in the aftermath of the 2005 referendum that greatly affected the next phase of the constitutional reform struggle. The first was the realignment of social forces, especially within civil society, that kept the dream of a new constitution alive. The second was the further fragmentation of elite political formations coupled with the crystallisation of new political alliances and coalitions. This led to the third development – a deepening of ethnic-based antagonism that ultimately led to politically induced inter-ethnic violence in 2007. The remainder of this chapter discusses these developments and how they impacted on the struggle for constitutional reform.

Fomenting incivility: post-referendum realignments

While deep societal cleavages curtailed the opportunity to deliver a new constitution in November 2005, the immediate post-referendum period witnessed a realignment of social forces that kept the dream of a new constitution alive. Specifically, a new wave of contention emerged because the issues at the heart of the formation of the Ufungamano Initiative were, by now, widely diffused among the Kenyan population. This helped to shift the anti-reform ideology of the dominant NARC coalition as more Kenyans outside elite formations continued to agitate for the need to change the constitution.24 Consequently, new campaigns began as old ones wrapped up. It is interesting to note that the new wave of contention had a character that was decidedly bifurcated along ethnic and religious lines, even though it was fomented by some of the social forces that had participated in the Ufungamano Initiative. For example, Christian groups joined hands with Hindu leaders in opposition to Kenya’s Muslims amid rising fears that the latter wished to impose Islamic institutions and law on the rest of the population. As such, in contrast to previous phases of this struggle, the new wave of contention came to be characterised by ethnic and religious rivalries.
Nyinguro and Otenyo (2007) trace the new wave of contention to the political divisions and brinkmanship of the National Constitutional Conference deliberations. Specifically, some delegates and observers who were unhappy with the pace at which NARC was managing the constitutional reform process met in early April 2004. This meeting resulted in the formation of the Coalition for a Commonsense Constitution, which was later renamed Katiba Watch (Nyinguro and Otenyo 2007). This group advocated a resumption of mass action to force the NARC regime to deliver a new constitution and implement other political reforms with a view to ensuring fairer distribution of the country’s wealth and improving living standards for all Kenyans. For example, on July 3, 2004, Katiba Watch activists organised a peaceful demonstration in Nairobi that was violently suppressed by the police. There were ethnic undertones to the group’s campaign because, by now, it was clear that the dominant faction of NARC – which mainly consisted of politicians from central Kenya – had no intention of dismantling the existing governance architecture as this allowed them unfettered power over opportunities for rent-seeking and distribution.
In tandem with Katiba Watch, the National Civil Society Congress (NCSC) launched the Katiba Sasa (Constitution Now) campaign. Significantly, the NCSC drew most of its members from the secular civil society groups that had been at the forefront of the constitutional reform struggle under the auspices of the Ufungamano Initiative.25 Like Katiba Watch, Katiba Sasa also called for the resumption of mass action. However, there was no return to the broad-based protests of 1997. This was because the new socio-political formations and mobilisations were manifestly ethnic as pent-up anger over the NARC regime’s failure to deliver a new constitution or address long-standing socioeconomic and political concerns eventually found expression in the ethnic demagogy that characterised the 2007 election campaign.26
Inside the regime, especially in the run-up to the referendum, intra-elite ethnic divisions rendered the government inoperable. Following the country’s rejection of the draft constitution, President Kibaki’s response was to dismiss his entire cabinet and appoint a new one that included none of the LDP-allied politicians who had campaigned against the draft. The most prominent omissions were Raila Odinga and Kalonzo Musyoka, whose places were taken by members of opposition parties (mainly KANU and Ford People) who were sympathetic to Kibaki. These dismissals from the government helped to transform the referendum “No” campaign into a new political coalition – the Orange Democratic Movement (ODM). However, as we will see in Chapter 7, ODM’s unity was fragile as its leaders personal and ethnic ambitions soon conspired to tear it apart. In the run-up to the 2007 general election, this intra-elite competition resulted in it splintering into the Odinga-led ODM and the Musyoka-led ODM-Kenya (ODM-K) – which were registered as separate political parties – in addition to Uhuru Kenyatta’s KANU, which remained the official opposition.
Meanwhile, what was left of the ruling regime splintered further into the Party of National Unity (PNU) and NARC. By the time of the election, therefore, the original NARC coalition, which had dethroned KANU from power in 2002, had fractured into at least three major political formations – ODM, ODM-K, and PNU – as well as a couple of smaller parties, including the group that was still calling itself NARC. All of these parties both mobilised and depended on ethnic support. Although Musyoka ran for president, the main competitors in the election were ODM and PNU. Their contest set the stage for a deep political crisis that threatened the very existence of the nation.
More fundamentally, while the rejection of the draft constitution meant the executive retained the power to manipulate other institutions, the state’s monopoly of legitimate force had been heavily dented and the capacity for violence had been widely diffused (Mueller 2008). This would soon come to haunt Kenya. For example, a few months prior to the election, in utter disregard of the 1997 IPPG deal, President Kibaki ignored the opposition and unilaterally appointed new commissioners to the Electoral Commission. Understandably, this tarnished the organisation’s reputation and raised justified questions about its ability – or even inclination – to oversee free and fair elections (Mueller 2008). A short time later, as we will see in Chapter 7, this distrust in Kenya’s electoral process helped to ignite the post-election crisis.

Conclusion

A movement’s standing in society can be greatly altered by its own success. As this chapter has shown, the Ufungamano Initiative became a victim of its own success. To begin with, the merger with the state-run process diminished the movement’s significance as attention shifted to the CKRC when it became the sole theatre of action in constitutional reform. In addition, some members of the movement treated the merger as an end in itself. Moreover, many of the merger’s critics saw it as essentially co-optive as many of the movement’s most influential functionaries first signed up to the common process, then focused on consolidating their new positions of power after the 2002 election. In the post-election period, the various sections of civil society that had united against Moi adopted contrasting positions in their dealings with the state. Predictably, many of the fault lines were ethnic, and the Ufungamano Initiative was no longer able to serve as a counterweight to the political elites once bickering over power poisoned the environment and threatened the delivery of a new constitution.
The merger also led to the entrenchment of dominant elite hegemony because it was essentially an elite bargain between conservative religious and political elites. Although the deal they reached postponed an all-out conflict, over the long term it actually exacerbated the Kenyan crisis. New fractures emerged between the various elite formations as some of them desperately tried to preserve a dying order. These cleavages ultimately helped to scuttle the dream of a new constitution in the 2005 referendum.
The defeat of the state-sponsored draft had a profound impact on Kenya’s political development. Specifically, as we will see in Chapter 7, the two elite camps that had campaigned against each other during the referendum reformed into the three main political parties that contested the 2007 general election. Dissatisfaction over the outcome of the presidential vote escalated into widespread insurrection and violence due to the country’s earlier rejection of the institutional architecture that was necessary to arbitrate between Kenya’s competing elites. The only solution was to try again and draft a new constitution that would encourage those elites to cooperate.
