7    Uncivil energy begets civil ends

Introduction

The central questions for this chapter are why and how did a new wave of contention incubated in the ashes of the 2005 referendum lead to the 2007/2008 post-election violence and finally deliver a new constitution in 2010? The chapter advances a political economy argument to account for the realisation of that new constitution. Specifically, following on from Chapter 6, it is argued that the 2005 referendum exposed deep cleavages not only within the new regime but also in the pro-change movement and the wider Kenyan public. In addition to curtailing the opportunity to deliver a new constitution, these cleavages led to a realignment of social forces. While much of civil society and the religious institutions remained divided, a section of civil society in the form of the National Civil Society Congress launched its Katiba Sasa campaign and continued to agitate for a new constitution. However, widespread frustration over NARC’s failure to deliver a new constitution or address long-standing socioeconomic and political grievances ultimately found expression in the ethnic demagogy that characterised the 2007 election campaign (Human Rights Watch 2008). In the end, deep-seated ethnic divisions precipitated the post-election violence of 2007/2008, which was ignited by a dispute over the presidential ballot tally. The ensuing violence posed collective economic and political threats (especially loss of property) that triggered regional interventions and eventually forced elite bargains and concessions.
The violence and its resolution, the chapter argues, created the “constitutional moment.” Therefore, it is argued that the “disruptive power” of the post-election violence ultimately made reform possible (Mati 2013). Specifically, while the 2007/2008 violence was not the first instance of disruptive power in Kenya (it had been evident in various waves of contention over the previous two decades), it provided the necessary pressure from below to force elite fragmentations and the realignment of social forces, which in turn delivered a new constitution in 2010.
The chapter begins with an overview of the electoral context and its propensity to ignite violence in Kenya. Thereafter, it explores why protests over the 2007 election results escalated so rapidly into violence. This is followed by analysis of the shifts within civil society and how these developments converged with political elite interests in 2007. Finally, there is a discussion on the resolution of the post-election violence and how this delivered a new constitution in 2010.

Elections and political violence in Kenya

In many African countries, political power is critically important for elites to secure economic prosperity and, in some cases, freedom from persecution. As a result, elections are intensely contested, sometimes resulting in widespread election-related violence. In the last three decades, for example, elections have ignited violence in Ivory Coast, Zimbabwe, Burundi, Ethiopia, Nigeria, Uganda, Democratic Republic of Congo, South Sudan, and South Africa, among others. Similarly, Kenya has experienced several outbreaks of election-related violence since the reintroduction of multi-partyism in 1991. However, it was the 2007/2008 post-election violence that exposed the deep rot in a post-colonial state that had previously been hailed as a promising emergent democracy. Specifically, this particular outbreak of violence highlighted the inherent dangers of three key aspects of Kenya’s political scene: political tribalism; the high-stakes, winner-takes-all orientation of the country’s elections; and the diffusion and normalisation of violence among various groups. Political tribalism heightens elite competition for state power because it affords winners the means to buy loyalty through the legal and extra-legal distribution of public resources. In the process, those in power are perceived to favour those who are ethnically close to them. As a result, Kenya’s political parties tend to be inherently ethnic patrimonies (Mueller 2008; Omolo 2002).
The 2007/2008 violence was a catastrophic imperative incubated in the disorder of ethnicised intra-elite competition for state power and resources, which intensified in the run-up to the country’s rejection of the draft constitution. As noted in Chapter 6, the two referendum campaigns accentuated political ethnicity instead of focusing on the content of the draft constitution (Mati 2012; 2019). According to Ochieng Khairallah, who represented the National Youth Movement in both the NCEC and the Ufungamano Initiative, the situation deteriorated dramatically immediately after the merger of the Ufungamano Initiative and the state process: “In civic education, people lost the big picture and we started preoccupying ourselves with small things … to the detriment of the big picture and the collective good.”1 Consequently, what Sigmund Freud called “the narcissism of small differences” became increasingly prevalent (Mamdani 1996: 8). This paved the way for the rejection of the draft constitution as well as the violence that followed in 2007.
The politicisation of small – and especially ethnic – differences has been conceptualised as “political ethnicity” or “political tribalism” (Berman 2010; Atieno-Odhiambo 2002; Lonsdale 1994). Political ethnicity usually manifests from an intra-elite struggle for power and resources during which elites instrumentalise ethnicity as the “singular dependable locus for articulation of group interests” (Atieno-Odhiambo 2002: 230; see also Mati 2019: 4). Specifically, elites “mobilize communal solidarity and political organization of the community … against the competing interests of rival ethnicities in access to the state and its patronage resources” (Berman 2010: 10).
Political ethnicity set the stage for the violent conflicts of 2007/2008 especially due to an aggravated intra-elite competition to win the presidency between two dominant formations: the incumbent President Mwai Kibaki’s PNU and Raila Odinga’s ODM. Both PNU and ODM were essentially coalitions of regional ethnic blocs, with central Kenyan ethnic groups (i.e. the Gikuyu, Embu, and Meru) in the PNU camp, and Kalenjin, Luo, Luhya, and, to some extent, coastal ethnic groups in the ODM camp. This electoral competition fomented widespread violence primarily because of the power of the presidency in Kenya. Specifically, historically, the president has enjoyed unfettered authority over rent seeking and distribution at the expense of other state institutions. Therefore, elite formations have engaged in vicious fights for control of this power in every election since the reintroduction of multi-partyism in 1992.
In their 2007 election campaigns, ODM and PNU emphasised the ethnicity of their own candidates while painting their opponents and their supporters as power-hungry ethnic chauvinists (Human Rights Watch 2008). Moreover, ODM framed the election as a contest between 41 Kenyan communities and the Kikuyu, given that the leader of the official opposition in parliament, Uhuru Kenyatta (a Kikuyu), had thrown his weight behind Kibaki (a fellow Kikuyu). ODM, on the other hand, enjoyed majority support among most of western Kenya’s ethnic communities. It should be mentioned that the Kalenjin elite who had been key players in the Moi/KANU regime felt that the subsequent Kibaki regime had specifically targeted them in its purge of the state bureaucracy. Having lost lucrative rent-seeking opportunities, they set about consolidating ethnic opposition to Kibaki and his almost exclusively Kikuyu regime in the run-up to the 2007 election. They did this by invoking land grievances in the Rift Valley.
The contest, however, was about more than ethnic identity. For instance, religion and the majimbo (federalism) question were also significant campaign issues (Oloo 2010). Majimbo became especially divisive because, for many, it was a veiled attack on the Kikuyu, who are arguably the most dispersed ethnic group in the country (Ghai 2008). Meanwhile, religious identity became a major electoral issue after ODM’s candidate for the presidency, Raila Odinga, signed a secret Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) with the National Muslim Leaders Forum (NAMLEF) (Juma 2009). NAMLEF had grievances about the Kibaki administration’s close ties with the United States’ counter-terrorism policy. For instance, the state had facilitated the extra-judicial rendition of several Kenyan Muslims who were suspected of terrorist activity (International Crisis Group 2008). Although NAMLEF did not represent all of Kenya’s Muslims, Kibaki’s subsequent steps to assuage its unease legitimised religious identity as an important variable in the upcoming election.
Another significant factor in the post-election violence was the erosion of trust in state institutions, especially due to the pre-election activities of one of the competing coalitions. Specifically, as was noted in Chapter 6, with the old constitution still in place, the executive retained the power to manipulate other key institutions, including the Electoral Commission. Consequently, a few months before the election, Kibaki was able to appoint a new cohort of commissioners in a move that seriously tarnished the organisation’s credibility (Mueller 2008). But the Electoral Commission was not the only state institution facing a legitimacy crisis. In addition, there were widespread allegations of corruption and ethnic bias in both the police and the judiciary. For instance, Human Rights Watch (2008: 5) suggested: “many Kenyans have little faith in the police to act in a professional, impartial, and timely manner; this reality only encourages vigilantes to take the law into their own hands.” Meanwhile, the infamous “radical surgery” spearheaded by retired High Court judge Aaron Ringera had unearthed – but failed to weed out – pervasive corruption in the judiciary (Khamisi 2018). Kibaki simply turned Ringera’s investigation into an opportunity to eliminate antagonistic judges and replace them with appointees who were more sympathetic to the NAK faction.
The Electoral Commission’s credibility deficit provided ODM with the perfect opportunity to refuse to concede defeat when Kibaki was declared the winner of the presidential election with 46.7 per cent of the vote (against Odinga’s 44.3 per cent). Odinga and ODM argued that the Electoral Commission – which Kibaki had personally appointed – had manipulated the tally in the incumbent president’s favour. Moreover, ODM refused to contest the result in the courts, as it had no faith in a judicial system that was controlled by Kibaki sympathisers. This constituted outright rejection of a constitutional and institutional system that ODM argued was guaranteed to deliver an exclusionary government.
Among others, North et al. (2013), Von Holdt (2014), and Arias and Goldstein (2010) have analysed the relationship between violent politics and economics. Von Holdt (2014: 147), for example, argues that political violence in post-apartheid South Africa is shaped by both “political inclusion and high levels socio-economic exclusion.” Meanwhile, North et al. (2013: 1) suggest that exclusionary regimes generate a crisis in opportunities for rent seeking and distribution among elites. Ultimately, violence becomes a useful tool for pursuing political ends.
In the Kenyan case, violence broke out immediately after the election in various parts of the country, with Kikuyu the main targets because of their ethnic affiliation to Kibaki. The state responded with massive force against the demonstrators, looters, and those perpetrating the violence. The Kibaki side accused Odinga and his supporters of ethnic cleansing, while Odinga held that the Kibaki camp was guilty of genocide. When pressed by the media on January 2, 2008 if he would urge his supporters to calm down, Odinga stated: “I refuse to be asked to give the Kenyan people an anaesthetic so that they can be raped” (BBC 2008). Instead, he called for a “million-man march” to force Kibaki out of power (News24 2007).
The post-election violence took two distinct forms, with ethnic identity and politically constructed autochthony as the main undercurrents (Lonsdale 2008). First, there was citizen-on-citizen violence, which erupted in ODM strongholds when its supporters attacked Kikuyu. PNU supporters responded by attacking members of ethnic groups they associated with ODM. Second, there was state-on-citizen violence, in which pro-PNU police were deployed against protesting ODM supporters but did nothing when PNU supporters launched their counter-attacks. On a lesser scale, this also manifested as targeted harassment and even alleged assassinations of ODM leaders (UNHCHR 2008). Specifically, two ODM Members of Parliament died in suspicious circumstances: Melitus Mugabe Were was shot dead on his driveway in Nairobi on January 29, and two days later a policeman murdered David Kimutai Too in Eldoret. The official explanation for the latter’s death was that Too was embroiled in a love triangle with the officer who shot him and a female officer, but ODM insisted that the government had ordered his assassination to reduce the number of opposition MPs. New waves of protest and violence erupted immediately after the killings.
Other victims of the violence included: former Olympic athlete Lucas Sang, who died in unknown circumstances in a riot in Eldoret on January 1, 2008; politician G.G. Njuguna Ngengi, who was hacked to death in Kuresoi, Nakuru, the following day; marathon runner Wesley Ngetich Kimutai, who was fatally shot with an arrow in Trans Mara District on January 19; and Donald Odanga, a former basketball international, who was fatally wounded by a “stray” police bullet in Mathare, Nairobi, the next day (CIPEV 2008). Understandably, there were fears that the violence was spiralling out of control. It continued for over a month, coming to a halt only after the Kofi Annan-led Panel of Eminent Africans negotiated a power-sharing deal between Kibaki and Odinga. By the time the violence ended, over 1,300 people lay dead, 3,500 had been injured, over 700,000 had been displaced, hundreds had been raped, and millions of dollars’ worth of property had been either destroyed or looted.2

Dynamics of the 2007/2008 post-election conflict: from protest to violence

While the trigger for the violence was the dispute over the declared outcome of the presidential election (CIPEV 2008), there had been pre-election violence in Kuresoi, Molo, and Mount Elgon throughout 2007. A Human Rights Watch (2008: 4) report explained that in the Rift Valley especially,
Post-election bloodshed did not arise spontaneously. It emerged as a result of incitement before the election and coordination and organization, at least at the local level. Around Eldoret local ODM mobilizers and other prominent individuals called meetings during the election campaign to urge violence in the event of a Kibaki victory, arguing that if Kibaki was announced as the winner it must mean the polls had been rigged and the reaction should be “war” against local Kikuyu residents. In the days that followed, attacks were often meticulously organized by local leaders.
In these circumstances, protests erupted simultaneously in Kibera’s slums and Kisumu – Odinga’s heartlands – as well as Eldoret as soon as Kibaki was announced as the victor on December 30, 2007. Thereafter, they quickly morphed into ethnic violence after state security agents engaged in running battles with the protesters. Before long, the violence had spread to several districts in the Rift Valley and Mombasa. On January 1, a church in Kiambaa, Eldoret, that was hosting internally displaced refugees was torched. Over 30 people died and several hundred suffered life-threatening burns.3 The arrival of thousands of internally displaced people in Nakuru and Naivasha, from mid-January onwards led to waves of organised attacks by suspected Mungiki gangs, especially against ethnic Luo and Kalenjin (CIPEV 2008). By the end of January, ethnic-targeted violence had spread to most urban centres, which greatly compromised the state’s ability to quell it (Mueller 2008).
Another aspect of the violence was the detrimental impact it had on the domestic economy due to looting and the lack of a conducive environment for commerce and the movement of goods. Additionally, the violence affected the economies of Uganda, northern Tanzania, Rwanda, Burundi, and eastern Democratic Republic of Congo, particularly after an incident in Kibera, Nairobi, on January 17, 2008, when protesters uprooted the railway track to western Kenya and Uganda amid rumours that Uganda had sent an armed contingent to help Kibaki’s government deal with the violence in Kisumu. The railway is a critical lifeline not just for western Kenya but for the whole East African economy. In addition, in urban areas, the violence and associated looting that had hitherto been confined mainly to the slums started encroaching on middle-class neighbourhoods, such as Woodley in Nairobi (where an MP was killed), and various town and city centres across the nation. From then onwards, a full-scale civil war seemed likely, especially after thousands of displaced people were forced to move to areas predominantly occupied by their co-ethnics and organised retaliatory attacks began. Following North et al.’s (2013) postulation, therefore, it could be said that the violence greatly reduced opportunities for rent creation for elite groups not just in Kenya but throughout the wider East African economy.
It is also important to note that the post-election violence occurred in a context of deep divisions in all sectors of society, including civil society. Additionally, many of civil society’s most experienced leaders had recently transitioned into either elective politics or the bureaucracy. One of these was Kivutha Kibwana, who had been an effective co-convenor of the NCEC in the mid- to late 1990s before his election as MP for Makueni in 2002. Wangari Maathai, a prominent campaigner for women’s rights through her Green Belt Movement, was elected MP for Tetu the same year. Yet more leaders transitioned into elective politics in the 2007 election, including Mutava Musyimi, who led the Ufungamano Initiative before his election as MP for Gachoka in Mbeere. Meanwhile, Isaac Lenaola, John Githongo, Said Athman, Gichira Kibara, Maina Kiai, and Omar Hassan had all either led civil society groups or served in various capacities in state institutions since 2002. Additionally, a number of seasoned civil society leaders, such as Kamau Kuria and Davinder Lamba (both former NCEC co-convenors), had retreated with a sense of betrayal following the merger of the Ufungamano Initiative and the state-led process.4 Such transitions were significant because they left civil society without an effective leadership in a time of crisis.
The ethnic-based factionalism of the 2005 referendum and the 2007 election campaign infected mainstream religious institutions, too. As a result, various leaders of activist religious institutions openly declared their support for their co-ethnic political kingpins (Mwaura and Martinon 2010; Leremore 2013; Juma 2009; Mati 2015). Reflecting on the deep divisions in Kenya’s religious institutions during the post-election crisis, Juma (2009: 411) writes:
Like other sectors of society, the Church was largely polarised along ethnic and regional lines during the electoral process. Similarly, the Muslim side was divided by the split among imams over the alleged memorandum of understanding signed with Raila. So deep was the divide among religious leaders that if those from the Central Province (Kibaki’s stronghold) issued a statement that appeared to be supporting Kibaki, their counterparts in Nyanza would also issue a statement in support of Raila. So, when the post-electoral violence erupted, religious groups proved unable to issue a unified statement calling for peace. Suffering from an acute lack of legitimacy, these groups lacked the credibility to mediate.
For some, the fraternity of the religious leadership has never recovered. It remains deeply divided and badly bruised by this legitimacy crisis (Mati 2015).

Developments within civil society and their convergence with political elite interests

As already noted, within the Kenyan secular civil society fraternity, the struggle for a new constitution did not end with the rejection of the draft constitution in the 2005 referendum. However, the next phase of that struggle took a decidedly ethnic and ultimately violent turn. The immediate aftermath of the referendum witnessed the emergence of a new wave of contention for a new constitution that hinged around the leaders of secular civil society. Of course, this marked a return to the original agitators for constitutional reform. According to Collins George Owuor, who served as the chair of Constitution and Reform Education Consortium (CRECO) after the 2007 general election, his group and URAHIA joined hands with the National Civil Society Congress to kick-start the push for a new constitution through the Katiba Sasa campaign, which became active in most of Kenya’s urban centres.5 In various parts of the country, the mobilisations and civic education of the Ufungamano Initiative and the CKRC, along with the referendum campaigns, had left many Kenyans well informed on the need for a new constitution as well as the type of the constitution that might address the perennial socioeconomic and political problems that had bedevilled Kenya for decades. This meant that the desire for a new constitution was highly diffused throughout the country. To illustrate the dynamics of this ongoing struggle, it is helpful to focus on one of its epicentres, Kisumu.
In Kisumu, the post-election violence of 2007/2008 began as citizen-on-citizen attacks in which ODM supporters assaulted people they believed to be supporters of PNU simply on the basis of their ethnicity. A short time later, there was also state-on-citizen violence when police descended on protesting ODM supporters across the city’s slums. According to the official Commission of Inquiry into the Post-Election Violence (CIPEV 2008), tensions were already running high in Kisumu and the whole Luo Nyanza region long before the election and especially after Raila Odinga was unceremoniously ejected from the Karatina Tourist Lodge (an establishment said to be owned and run by one of President Kibaki’s in-laws) in central Kenya. In response, some ODM politicians warned Kibaki’s allies that they would face a hostile reception if they dared to campaign in the ODM stronghold of Luo Nyanza (CIPEV 2008: 174).
The CIPEV (2008: 177) further reported that the violence in Kisumu and Luo Nyanza occurred in three distinct phases:
The first was directly related to the context of elections and took place between 29 and 31 December 2007. It was characterized by demonstrations, riots, acrimony and discontent. The second phase occurred in the New Year and was reactionary apparently in protest against what was viewed as excessive use of force by police and also in response to calls for mass action by the ODM leadership which lasted until mid-January 2008. The third phase was associated with two unrelated events, first the targeting of Luo by Kikuyu gangs in Nakuru and Naivasha leading to an influx of Luo IDPs [internally displaced persons] from outside Nyanza and the murders, towards the end of January 2008, of two ODM politicians, Mr Mugabe Were, MP for Embakasi and Mr David Kimutai Too. Similar evidence was in fact tendered by the KNCHR [Kenyan National Commission on Human Rights] in their report.
The CIPEV (2008) reported that a total of 81 people died in Kisumu, 64 of whom as a result of police gunshots. The victims were mainly urban youth from the city’s slums. Consequently, in the months that followed the post-election violence, Kisumu became a hotbed of political mobilisation. A number of civil society organisations, both local and national, initiated interventions with the aim of ensuring that there would be no repeat of the violence. Among the most active were Peacenet Kenya, the Red Cross, the Amani Centre for Peace and Training, and the Grassroots Trust, which established an inter-agency hub that worked with and trained community-identified mediators throughout the city.6 Most of their attention was focused on slum communities because these had supplied the vast majority of the footsoldiers for both the peaceful struggle for constitutional reform and the violence. In this regard, Phelics Ong’ong’a, who coordinated the Voice of Slum Dwellers Federation in Kisumu, explained what motivated their activism:
We have been the people who have been at the centre of these struggles because we are the first ones to go out demonstrating and calling for change. We are the people who are killed when the police fire at demonstrators. But we continue fighting because, for many of us, it is a matter of life and death. We are the people continuously evicted all the time by the collusion of state and private interests. Yet, we have lived for [a] long [time] in these areas. The majority of us were born in these slums and we have nowhere else to go. When they evict us, where exactly do they expect us to go? As such, we are pushing for recognition as well as to be given titles over the plots we live in. As the Voice of Slum Dwellers Federation, we have been trying to enlighten our members on human rights and to fight for social economic justice.7
Such consciousness raising and mobilisation led to slum areas in Kisumu and the rest of the country becoming cornerstones of the struggle for a new constitution. Daniel Owino, a barber who plied his trade in a kiosk at Kisumu’s bus station, described his rather circuitous path to becoming an active member of the campaign:
I did automotive engineering at a polytechnic and later went into business in Garissa, where I lived from 1998. I moved to Kisumu in 2005, but there were many challenges for a garage business because there is so much competition and renting space to do business is also very expensive. I therefore switched to a hair-cutting business here at the bus station. My experience of living in Garissa at the height of the constitutional reforms tells me that that part of the country is not as exposed as Kisumu. I was not therefore as involved in following up what was happening in these struggles then. I had, of course, heard of the Ufungamano Initiative while still in Garissa. However, I took greater interest once I was in Kisumu. I have since been involved through grassroots self-help groups where we concentrate on getting people to understand the need for a new constitution … At a personal level, I have been talking to many clients who pass through my hair-cutting business, especially the youth, and we are always talking politics, and the constitution is a prominent feature in these discussions.8
Owino then elaborated that one of the grassroots organisations he had joined was the Nyanza Youth Forum, which launched in 2007 to address myriad issues affecting young people, such as drug abuse, HIV/AIDS, and economic empowerment. However, it also campaigned for a new constitutional review process and decried Kenya’s descent into ethnic factionalism. Owino himself lamented that youngsters from Kisumu were routinely hired to perpetrate politically motivated ethnic violence.9
The Nyanza Youth Forum organised constitutional forums in which three political economy issues kept cropping up. The first of these was the urgent need to devolve revenue and economic activities to the grassroots in order that all of the country’s regions were treated fairly and developed equally. According to Owino, this would ensure that
All citizens of Kenya would become interested in what they produced as a region and as a country. Every region gets taxes commensurate to what they produce and use the same to develop. [As it is,] Nairobi [gets] everything and then refuses to give us back anything, especially if you happen to be on the wrong side politically. The centralised system sucks from the regions and does not give anything in return, thus the need to devolve.10
The second key issue was the need for community involvement in running their own affairs. The third was unemployment, especially among the youth. Most of the youth who joined these grassroots struggles were motivated by these political economy issues and by a firm belief that they had “an opportunity to be that great generation that gave Kenya a better constitution.”11
Another grassroots initiative with a presence in all of Kenya’s major urban centres (including Kisumu) that agitated on various socioeconomic and political issues was Bunge la Mwananchi (People’s Parliament). This group, which was quite active in Kisumu before and in the aftermath of the 2007/2008 post-election violence, was founded on an innovative idea in a context of resource constraints, especially in poor urban neighbourhoods. As a movement, it arose “in the early 1990s as a contested social space for debates and discussion on social, political and economic issues by ordinary Kenyans” (Gachihi 2014). A similar initiative, especially in Kibera, was Kamukunji, which “subscribed to Raila Odinga’s ideologies and Raila’s influence … and was something [that was] meant to sustain Raila’s momentum on the ground.”12 These two organisations were innovative in the sense that they enabled ordinary people to meet in open spaces, under trees, or on benches in public parks to deliberate on topical socioeconomic and political issues, especially those that featured in the daily newspapers. In the words of one of the activists:
We used to have our Kamukunjis at Sarang’ombe [in Kibera]. We would sit under a tree and people would deliberate. We would buy a newspaper and appoint someone to read us articles on things to do with the constitution, on things concerning the change. These are things we used to read and even discuss. We would appoint different discussants, who would take us through these topics and who would tell us what they actually entailed.13
Funding constraints mean that it is not easy for grassroots activists to meet in formal spaces. After the 2007/2008 post-election violence, activists also started congregating in the Kisumu Peace Tent (another innovative project) to discuss issues. To some extent, this strategy was indicative of the grassroots protesters’ disaffection with larger civil society initiatives. Daniel Owino lamented:
Many of the people involved in the national struggles are in NGOs with massive funding but a lack of linkages with the grassroots … There is also a dearth of facilities to ensure popular participation. For example, if you need to have a public forum, you need a venue; and in cities and towns, nothing comes free. We therefore congregate at the Peace Tent, which in a way is a bunge la mwananchi wa kawaida [ordinary citizens’ parliament], to deliberate on issues, especially when we are under attack from the city council. This has helped us in confronting the problems of bad leadership that we see at the local level, especially among the councillors, who are doing little. Yet, during the campaigns, they are singing everywhere how they are going to help us.14
Clearly, then, grievances were raised and debated far beyond the city limits of Nairobi. More importantly, after the post-election violence, these discussions led to a growing realisation that only a new constitution would resolve the country’s problems. For instance, devolution, which had been a major issue in both the 2005 referendum and the 2007 election, was openly debated in public forums throughout Kenya. In Kisumu, the Nyanza Youth Forum explained the challenges of making devolution work in an era when the country’s politicians were exploiting tribalism to ensure their own political survival. Owino and his fellow youth activists railed against this tactic. Instead, they “wanted one Kenya where all are safe and free.”15 Furthermore, in line with arguments advanced in previous waves of the reform struggle, the new wave held the constitution responsible for the country’s poor governance and the post-election violence. Hence, a new one was needed to transform state institutions and ensure good governance and effective accountability mechanisms.16
The post-2007 mobilisations within civil society were also characterised by disillusionment with those who had transitioned into elective politics. A common lament among the remaining civil society activists was that their former leaders invariably abandoned the ideals and tenets of the struggle as soon as they were elected to parliament. For example, the chair of the Kisumu Urban Local Forum, said:
Civil society is composed of individuals who also have their own interests, so the individuals in civil society have contributed to this mess. I want to point to, for example, somebody like Mutava Musyimi. This is somebody who was part of the reform movement, but when he got to parliament, I am sorry to say that I think he messed us up. If you look, quite a number of people … left the reform agenda and pursued their own [interests].17
As a result, it can be difficult to identify who was pushing for which agenda.18 Divisions within civil society notwithstanding, the widespread violence served to remind political leaders that reform was urgently required. First, though, there was an even more urgent need to halt the violence.

Halting the 2007/2008 post-election violence and delivering a new constitution

The preceding discussion points to a convergence of factors in both civil society and the political elite that led to post-election violence throughout the country in 2007/2008. This indicates the widespread dispersal of the capacity for violence among different actors in the polity (see North et al. 2013). In most developing countries, widespread violence is usually contained through elite bargains – or “Limited Access Orders” – that “create rents so that powerful groups and individuals find it in their interest to refrain from using violence” (North et al. 2013: 3). These arrangements limit violence by creating and then institutionalising incentives for the leaders of competing groups to cooperate and coordinate an agreed – or “fair” – distribution of rents among themselves. Ultimately, they can develop into coalitions or even constitutions. For instance, elite bargaining played a key role in resolving the 2007/2008 post-election violence in Kenya and then incentivising further cooperation through institutional reform.
After several failed attempts by local and regional civic actors and regional leaders to halt the violence,19 former United Nations Secretary General Dr Kofi Annan, former President of Tanzania Benjamin Mkapa, and former first lady of Mozambique and South Africa Graça Machel finally managed to convene a summit between PNU and ODM under the auspices of the African Union. After a month of negotiations, on February 28, 2008 the rival parties signed a power-sharing political settlement that allowed President Kibaki to retain his position while Raila Odinga assumed the newly created role of prime minister. The two men then appointed a grand coalition government composed of ministers from their respective parties (Brown with Sriram 2012: 244–5).
This forced political marriage not only delivered relative peace but also incubated the constitutional moment. This was because both the National Accord signed by Kibaki and Odinga and the preceding Kenya National Dialogue and Reconciliation agreement placed strong emphasis on power sharing in order to ensure that the two coalitions would cooperate to address the fundamental issues that had led to the violence. Both sides now agreed that institutional – and specifically constitutional – reform was necessary to resolve the country’s historical grievances and forestall further violence. The end result of this long-overdue accord was the delivery of a new constitution in 2010.

The Kenya National Dialogue and Reconciliation and the National Accord

The first step in the resolution of the crisis was an agreement reached on February 1, 2008 to cease hostilities under the auspices of what came to be known as the Kenya National Dialogue and Reconciliation. This truce sought to provide the necessary conditions for a peaceful resolution to both the political impasse and the violence. Thereafter, the parties negotiated a political settlement that was entrenched in the existing constitution as the 2008 National Accord and Reconciliation Act. Under the Kenya National Dialogue and Reconciliation process, both parties committed themselves to end the violence and identified a raft of actions and conditions that demanded immediate or future action. These included:
  • Agenda Item 1: Immediate action to stop the violence and restore fundamental rights and liberties;
  • Agenda Item 2: Immediate measures to address the humanitarian crisis, and promote healing and reconciliation;
  • Agenda Item 3: How to overcome the political crisis;
  • Agenda Item 4: Addressing long-term issues, including undertaking constitutional, legal and institutional reforms; land reform; tackling poverty and inequality as well as combating regional development imbalances; tackling unemployment, particularly among the youth; consolidating national cohesion and unity; and addressing transparency, accountability and impunity.
(South Consulting 2011: 1)
North et al. (2013: 4–6) argue that violence in “Limited Access Order” polities is contained because the leaders of competing coalitions are able to discipline and organise their own followers, call on each other’s support to organise and discipline the members of their groups, and limit others’ opportunities to start rival organisations. In this context, it is noteworthy that the violence ceased almost as soon as Kibaki and Odinga shook hands at the entrance of Harambee House on the afternoon of February 28, 2008, having just signed the National Accord. Thereafter, civic order was re-established throughout the country while the Kenya National Dialogue and Reconciliation process created a number of new institutions to facilitate implementation of the National Accord. Among these institutions were:
  • an independent Review Commission to investigate the conduct and outcome of the recent election, review the subsequent crisis, and offer recommendations for electoral reform;
  • a Commission of Inquiry into Post-Election Violence (CIPEV); and
  • a Truth, Justice, and Reconciliation Commission to facilitate the effective implementation of the agreements reached under the Kenya National Dialogue and Reconciliation process and support the new coalition government in its efforts to address the root causes of the post-election crisis.
Notwithstanding ongoing elite “wrangling and a dysfunctional power sharing government” (Africa Policy Institute 2013: 2), the National Accord was sufficiently powerful to maintain the truce between the two sides of the political divide and relaunch the stalled constitutional review process. This was because the agreements that came under the auspices of the National Accord had a self-triggering mandate. In this context, political shenanigans could be ignored. As a result, in December 2008, the Constitution of Kenya Review Act was passed to guide the next phase of the review process.
Pursuant to the provisions of Agenda Item 4, a Committee of Experts was established on February 23, 2009, with its members sworn in one week later. The chair, Nzamba Kitonga (a former chair of the Law Society of Kenya), his deputy, Atsango Chesoni, and four other members – Njoki Ndung’u (a women’s rights activist who had served in the 2002–2007 parliament), Otiende Amolo, Abdirashid Hussein, and Bobby Mkangi – were all Kenyans, as was the committee’s first full-time director, Dr Ekuru Aukot (executive director of Kituo Cha Sheria, a civil society legal advice centre that had been active in the constitutional reform movement). They were joined by three foreign nationals who were nominated by Kofi Annan’s Panel of Eminent Africans: Professor Christina Murray from South Africa, Dr Chaloka Beyani from Zambia, and Frederick Ssempembwa from Uganda (Mathenge 2009). This Committee of Experts was tasked with ironing out the contentious issues in previous draft constitutions in order to produce a “harmonised draft.” This document would be subjected to public scrutiny, revised, and finally submitted to parliament and a national referendum for approval. The committee operated under the terms of an act of parliament that contained self-triggering clauses to insulate it from political interference and ensure that it could not be dissolved or ignored.
Despite its resolve to deliver a new constitution that was acceptable to all Kenyans, the Committee of Experts had many critics. For instance, questions were raised about its ability to succeed where the much more experienced Ghai and his team had failed. Unsurprisingly, the most vocal critics were religious leaders and politicians who wanted to control the process and secure an outcome that served their interests. Other challenges emanated from disagreements over the content of the harmonised draft, which drew heavily on the earlier Bomas draft that Kibaki’s faction of NARC had watered down prior to the 2005 referendum. The most contentious issues were land, the so-called “abortion clause,” the Islamic Kadhi courts, and the preferred form of government.20
When the harmonised draft was finally presented to parliament, over 300 amendments were proposed. However, none of these garnered the requisite support to go through, so parliament approved the Committee of Experts’ draft in full on April 1, 2010. The attorney general published the proposed constitution the following month, which was followed by intense civic education until the end of July (Institute for Security Studies 2010). This period also witnessed intense campaigns both for and against the draft. The National Civil Society Congress and its allies continued their Katiba Sasa campaign, while other civil society groups served in the Reference Group, constituted by the 2008 Review Act, and organised their own civic education projects. It should be said that civil society exerted less influence over the direction of the process this time than it did during the Ufungamano Initiative phase of the struggle. Nonetheless, a majority of the Kenyans who served in the Committee of Experts hailed from civil society backgrounds, and some of them had a history of involvement in the struggle for constitutional reform either individually or through their organisations.
When the national referendum was held on August 4, 2010, 67.25 per cent of Kenyans voted in favour of the proposed constitution, heralding a new dawn in Kenya’s constitutional history after a two-decade struggle. However, it should be noted that this success was achieved only after the post-election violence of 2007/2008 forced the nation’s politicians to negotiate, make concessions, and form a grand coalition in order to protect their own privileged positions. This suggests that a confluence of interests among Kenya’s political elites was a sine qua non for the delivery of a new constitution (Mati 2013; Muhula and Ndegwa 2014; Murunga 2014). This conclusion is supported by the fact that the new constitution was, in many respects, not radically different from the draft that was rejected in 2005. The unwieldy two-tier governance system (county and national), the two-chamber parliament (National Assembly and Senate), and the chiselling of county-level governments along ethnic lines all betrayed the document’s origins in elite-level bargaining. Overall, as we shall see in Chapter 8, the new constitution was specifically designed to ensure that a somewhat larger elite would continue to benefit from political rent seeking at the national and county levels.
Nonetheless, it also embodied the collective desire of the Kenyan people to usher in a new form of governance. That desire is perfectly expressed in the preamble to the 2010 constitution, which states:
We, the people of Kenya –
… HONOURING those who heroically struggled to bring freedom and justice to our land:
PROUD of our ethnic, cultural and religious diversity, and determined to live in peace and unity as one indivisible sovereign nation: …
RECOGNISING the aspirations of all Kenyans for a government based on the essential values of human rights, equality, freedom, democracy, social justice and the rule of law:
EXERCISING our sovereign and inalienable right to determine the form of governance of our country and having participated fully in the making of this Constitution:
ADOPT, ENACT and give this Constitution to ourselves and to our future generations.21
Ultimately, the 2007/2008 post-election violence, its resolution, and subsequent developments reflected what Fowler (2011: 2) would later describe as “civic energy that is uncivil and potentially destabilising” but helps to secure civil ends. Such uncivil energy stems from deep-seated societal cleavages and structural contradictions that require fundamental remedial measures. Elite attempts to suppress them only ever result in violence, rebellions, or revolutions. In the Kenyan case, the remedy for the 2007/2008 post-election violence (uncivil energy) was the forced political marriage between Raila Odinga’s ODM and Mwai Kibaki’s PNU that was brokered by international and national mediators to prevent escalation of the conflict into full-scale civil war.

Conclusion

This chapter’s key proposition is that the “disruptive power” of the 2007/2008 post-election violence forced elite bargains and concessions that delivered a new constitution in 2010. This disruptive power erupted after two decades of mass consciousness raising and civic education orchestrated by sections of the intelligentsia, middle-class civil society organisations, and religious institutions as part of the struggle for constitutional reform. The country’s elites had failed to agree on the content and form of a previous draft constitution, resulting in its rejection in the 2005 referendum. This was followed by the politicisation of ethnicity throughout the country and a legitimacy crisis for state institutions, especially the police, the judiciary, and the Electoral Commission. The result was deeper entrenchment of the exclusionary state, which denied one sector of the dominant political elite its traditional rent-seeking opportunities. Thereafter, violence came to be seen as a viable tool for securing both political and economic ends (Mati 2013). This was possible because mass grievances over landownership were framed with elite grievances over the distribution of political spoils. Finally, a dispute over the result of the presidential election provided the spark that was needed to ignite more than a month of intense inter-ethnic violence. At one stage, this threatened to consume not only Kenya itself but its neighbours in East Africa.
This triggered foreign intervention spearheaded by the African Union and its Panel of Eminent Africans, which ultimately forced compromises and institutional power sharing between the competing elite coalitions. More fundamentally, these elite compromises paved the way for further elite cooperation and bargains that finally delivered a new constitution in 2010. Therefore, the 2010 constitution was a direct result of concessions made by Kenya’s hegemonic economic and political elites to safeguard their privileged positions in the face of the “uncivil” threat of the 2007/2008 post-election violence. Consequently, it was anything but a radical departure from the previous version or indeed the draft that the people rejected in 2005. As such, as we shall see in Chapter 8, it was never likely to redress the fundamental contradictions in the citizens’ relationship with the hegemonic power elites.
